#5888. The Fantasy of a Turkish Middle East

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MELIHA BENLI ALTUNISIK is Professor of International Relations at Middle East Technical University.

Ankara’s Risky Bid to Impose Regional Order

Since Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attack on Israel, the future of the Middle East has been fiercely contested. The previous Gulf-centered order had Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates (UAE) trying to stabilize and shape the region using economic statecraft and the normalization of relations with former foes in Iran, Israel, Turkey, and Syria. Three years of war in Gaza, and more recently in Iran and the Gulf, have severely disrupted that order-building project and generated a deep sense of uncertainty about what comes next.

Turkey, like every major power in the Middle East, has tried to fill the vacuum. Before 2023, Ankara’s influence had been in decline; after years of diplomatic isolation following the Arab uprisings, it had just begun repairing its ties with major regional actors. But in the past few years, Turkey has earned American backing for its policies in Syria and its regional diplomacy, and it has made an effort to secure the home front by pursuing a peace process with the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), a militant group that has fought a decades-long insurgency against the Turkish state. Most recently, in August, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey signed the Mecca Defense Alliance, a collective security agreement that institutionalizes Turkey’s regional positioning and pulls the country directly into Saudi Arabia’s war with the Houthis.

Turkey’s post–October 7 strategy has restored the country’s position as a regional heavyweight. But it is not capable of meeting its larger objective: establishing order on its own terms. Turkey, like the region’s other aspiring leaders, lacks the power to impose a new security framework on its own. Its nascent efforts to build one, such as the new Mecca pact, merely make countries outside the bloc feel unsafe, feeding instability. Turkey’s most ambitious plays for regional leadership are therefore likely to prove ineffective or even counterproductive. As long as the Middle East’s highly militarized, zero-sum security dynamics remain, Ankara would be better off concentrating on interests closer to home that it has the power to protect.

This is not the first time that Turkey has tried to shape the broader Middle Eastern order. Ankara saw the Arab uprisings in the early 2010s as a historic window of opportunity. The ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP) described the political transformation sweeping the region as “the people’s coming to power,” and the Turkish government expected that eventual elections in the Arab countries rocked by protest would bring into office parties aligned with the Muslim Brotherhood, which was ideologically sympathetic and had practical links to the AKP. The emergence of friendly governments, the thinking went, would increase Turkey’s influence in the region.

Yet these hopes soon receded. Egypt’s Muslim Brotherhood–affiliated president, Mohamed Morsi, was overthrown in 2013, and Turkey’s bet on the opposition movement in Syria failed as Bashar al-Assad clung to power. Turkey was left isolated. A rival axis composed of Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE objected to Turkey’s Muslim Brotherhood ties and sought to limit Ankara’s influence. This group contested Turkey’s positions across the Middle East, North Africa, the eastern Mediterranean, and the Horn of Africa, effectively fighting proxy battles with Turkey in conflicts in Libya, Somalia, and Syria.

Then, the attacks on October 7, 2023, ushered in another period of upheaval. Some changes since have played in Turkey’s favor. Chief among them is the collapse of the Assad regime in Syria in December 2024 at the hands of opposition groups led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham. Turkey had arguably invested more than any other country in pursuit of regime change in Damascus; it assisted opposition groups for years. Yet as the years went by, that objective seemed increasingly out of reach. The region’s other major powers made their peace with the regime’s survival, and even Ankara began considering rapprochement with Assad to avoid being left out in the cold. Assad’s fall changed Turkey’s fortunes overnight, handing it a level of influence in Syria it had not held in more than a decade.

Other developments proved more dangerous for Ankara. Israel’s foreign policy became more assertive and revisionist after October 7, and its expansive security objectives began to clash with Turkey’s own. Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s sharp criticism of Israeli policies in Gaza strained the relationship, and it deteriorated further when Turkey expanded its foothold in post-Assad Syria and Israel came to view Ankara’s military presence there as a direct threat. As Israel continues to strike sites in Syria—attacks Ankara has condemned—the risk of escalation is only growing. A miscalculation on either side could lead to a direct clash between Israeli and Turkish forces.

The U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, meanwhile, has cut both ways for Turkey. While Tehran’s attention has been focused elsewhere, Ankara has welcomed Iran’s retreat from Syria and the chance to push back against its remaining influence in Iraq. Turkey also has no interest in seeing a nuclear-armed Iran upend the regional balance of power. Yet the prospect of chaos across Turkey’s eastern border, should the Iranian regime fall, has bred real anxiety in Ankara, as has the possibility that Iran could become emboldened if the regime emerges from the war intact.

In this complex environment, Ankara has pursued a three-pronged strategy to protect its security interests and, it hopes, capitalize on opportunities the upheaval has created. Since U.S. President Donald Trump’s return to power, Erdogan has drawn Turkey closer to the United States and tried to use his relationship with Washington to advance Ankara’s regional policies. Turkey has also pressed ahead with regional normalization, building on efforts to repair ties with Saudi Arabia, Egypt, and other key Arab countries in the early 2020s, with the aim to forge a broader regional bloc. And, domestically, Ankara launched the “Turkey Without Terror” process, an attempt to resolve the conflict with the PKK inside and outside Turkey, which Turkey’s adversaries have long exploited by arming Kurdish groups or backing them politically.

Some of Ankara’s policies have borne fruit. Consider, for instance, the outreach to Washington. Erdogan’s close relations with Trump helped bring the United States on board with Turkey’s expanded role in Syria. Turkey was one of the four countries Washington brought in to help negotiate the U.S.-proposed October 2025 cease-fire agreement between Israel and Hamas, and as a result, Trump offered and Erdogan accepted a seat on the U.S. president’s so-called Board of Peace for Gaza’s reconstruction. Turkey has played an active part mediating between the United States and Iran during the latest war. And Trump publicly endorsed the Mecca Defense Alliance, calling it “big, bold, and important.” All of this points to a recognition in Washington of Turkey’s regional importance, the U.S. administration’s willingness to support some of Ankara’s priorities, and Turkey’s determination to elevate its own position.

Yet there are undeniable limits to Turkey’s quest for influence. At a February 2026 Board of Peace summit, for instance, Turkey was excluded from participating in a planned international troop deployment and police training in Gaza—a sign that warming relations with Washington were not enough to override Israeli objections to having a Turkish military footprint in the Palestinian territory. In Syria, similarly, the support Turkey has secured from the Trump administration has not stopped Israel from launching military strikes at Syrian targets near Turkish positions. Simply put, Turkey has not been able to use its relationship with the Trump administration to overcome the limit Israeli opposition places on the scope of Turkey’s regional role.

Turkey’s regional normalization efforts have yielded mixed results, too. Restoring diplomatic ties with significant players such as Saudi Arabia is in Turkey’s interest, to be sure. But the alliances Ankara is entering with these countries risk making Turkey and the region less secure, not more. The Mecca pact with Pakistan and Saudi Arabia could end up hardening rival blocs and elevating regional tensions. Turkey insists that the alliance is directed against no country in particular and has suggested that Egypt, Syria, or even Iran could eventually join. But in the meantime, Iran, Israel, and the Houthis could see it as a threat and try to test or weaken its members’ commitment.

Furthermore, the pact could pull Turkey into other countries’ wars. That possibility is already on the table now that the Turkish parliament has ratified the agreement and the Houthis are escalating their attacks on Saudi Arabia. The Turkish government has insisted that any military support it provided would be confined to deterrence, intelligence sharing, and defense-industrial cooperation. Yet Saudi Arabia is now seeking Pakistan’s and Turkey’s help with air defense and territorial protection, and the three states have agreed to prepare forces for rapid deployment. Sending Turkish troops to Saudi Arabia would still require separate parliamentary approval. If parliament allowed it, even a limited deployment would risk drawing Turkey into a wider regional conflict.

Some kind of regional security framework is necessary. The problem is that no country in the region, including Turkey, has the power to impose a security order on the rest, so whenever someone tries, other countries feel threatened. An informal cooperation framework that is open to all countries in the region—and that asks its members to commit to nonaggression instead of making mutual-defense pledges—might work better. But the assembly and functioning of such a coalition require trust that governments in the region do not have. Breaking the impasse would likely require the United States to play a more constructive role than it has in recent years—not simply endorsing whatever regional arrangement emerges, as Trump did with the Mecca pact, or pressuring countries to normalize relations with Israel without expecting Israel to change its policies in return, as Trump has also done. Instead, Washington, perhaps with the help of Beijing, would need to compel regional rivals such as Iran and Saudi Arabia to take confidence-building measures that would make an inclusive security framework viable in the long run. Without this external push, the narrower security blocs that emerge in the region will only lead to further instability.

Ankara’s strongest cards lie closer to home. The Kurdish peace process has moved slowly since February 2025, when Abdullah Ocalan, the imprisoned leader of the PKK, called on the militant group to disarm. But it has yielded tangible progress. In May 2025, the PKK announced that it would dissolve. In Syria, Kurdish forces are being integrated into the Syrian army, and Mazloum Abdi, the commander of the People’s Defense Units, a Kurdish armed group affiliated with the PKK, was appointed as a presidential adviser in August. Syria’s postconflict transition remains fragile, and the reconciliation between Damascus and Kurdish militants in the northeast may owe as much to American pressure as it does to Turkey’s domestic efforts to make peace with the PKK. But for Turkey, the eventual integration of Syrian Kurdish forces into the Syrian state removes the risk Ankara saw in having an autonomous, armed Kurdish entity on its southern border. And, given the close security and economic ties Ankara has developed with Damascus, as well as the political and legal steps the government can take at home to complete the disbanding of the PKK and to deepen democratic reforms, Turkey has the ability to keep these encouraging developments on track.

Turkey’s regional policies are most successful when the country plays to its strengths—not when it is trying to throw its weight around the region. In the first decade of the twenty-first century, Ankara built real influence by mediating between Syria and Israel and between Palestinian factions, expanding its economic engagement in the Levant, and proposing projects to connect the region’s energy and transportation infrastructure. Military power, meanwhile, has proved most effective when applied for specific purposes, such as to secure the border with Syria. This combination is Turkey’s winning formula: diplomacy and economic outreach to extend its influence, and limited force to protect its core interests. Broad, open-ended commitments, by contrast, risk drawing Turkey into regional conflicts without making it safer.

Today, sticking to a more effective formula does not mean retreating from regional politics. It means setting priorities and planning long-term. Consolidating the Kurdish peace process, keeping Washington invested in Syria’s stabilization, managing the risk of confrontation with Israel in Syria, and maintaining constructive relations with the Kurdistan Regional Government in northern Iraq and the Iraqi government in Baghdad are key to keeping Turkey’s borders secure and are within Turkey’s ability to achieve. Ankara can continue to use its experience in mediation when opportunities to advance peace processes present themselves. It can also promote economic connectivity, both by expanding trade ties and by supporting regional infrastructure projects such as the Iraq-Europe Development Road and a proposed pipeline network connecting the Gulf to Jordan, Syria, and Turkey. It need not shy away from security cooperation with the Gulf, either, if it can engage without making formal commitments. Yet in all these endeavors, Ankara should remember that it has the most clout when it stays out of the region’s conflicts.

The Middle East’s escalating wars and rival blocs could make a narrower Turkish security policy more difficult to sustain today than it was in the past. Defending Ankara’s interests and playing a constructive role in a volatile neighborhood will require a careful balance. The best way Turkey can contribute to regional stability may be to avoid trying to do too much.

The Overlooked Costs of a Transactional Foreign Policy

Even on the Sidelines, Ankara Faces Blowback

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5887. US Army to proceed with Iowa detonation of degraded bomb materials: ‘A consensus decision’

American Military Project director Will Thibeau reacts to the federal approval of a controlled detonation of expired explosives at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant. Thibeau also breaks down a Wall Street Journal report indicating the U.S. suspects Iran is behind dozens of recent cyberattacks targeting commercial ships in the Middle East.

The U.S. Army will detonate 68 barrels of unstable, degraded bomb-making chemicals in Iowa on Wednesday morning after a federal appeals court rejected a legal challenge and allowed the controlled blast to move forward.

In a statement late Monday, Army officials confirmed they are proceeding with destroying the crystallized lead azide at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant near Middletown in coordination with state, local and federal authorities.

"This is a consensus decision," Acting Army Secretary Adam Telle posted on social media. "Every expert agency agrees, and both Iowa’s current and incoming Governors say the call belongs to the experts."

FEDERAL APPEALS COURT CLEARS ARMY TO DETONATE VOLATILE IOWA EXPLOSIVES AS DEM GOV-ELECT REFUSES TO SIGN OFF

A sign identifying the facility sits inside a fence surrounding the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant on Nov. 19, 2026 in Middletown, Iowa. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

Hours earlier, a three-judge panel of the 8th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals lifted a temporary pause and denied an emergency request from the Iowa Environmental Council and the Sierra Club to block the operation. The judges found that the Environmental Protection Agency built adequate safeguards into its emergency permit to limit lead dust dispersal.

"Here we are faced with a situation in which the risk is between a controlled detonation and an unplanned explosion, not between the risk of a controlled detonation and no explosion at all," the court wrote.

The ruling followed a Truth Social post from President Donald Trump declaring he had ordered the military to hold off unless both the appeals court and Democratic Gov.-elect Rob Sand signed off. Trump warned he would hold Sand "responsible for any and all death or destruction" if the operation were blocked.

Sand, who takes office in January, declined to approve or reject the blast on Monday evening. He posted on X that the Trump administration should make the call based on the experts it brought in months ago and urged federal officials to release their briefing details publicly.

DEMOCRATS SEIZE MAJORITY OF GOVERNORS’ OFFICES IN ELECTION-NIGHT SHIFT

Meanwhile, a flight carrying Army officials departed Joint Base Andrews for Iowa just before Trump's post went live.

The Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown, Iowa. (U.S. Army via AP)

The operation has already been postponed three times amid public concern over toxic lead dust. Munitions specialists warned in court filings that dropping temperatures increase the risk that crystallized material inside the barrels could trigger an accidental, fatal explosion if left untreated.

In a public advisory, the Army said Wednesday's blast will produce two rapid detonations, ground vibrations and a dust plume expected to linger for no more than an hour.

An aerial view shows homes on Nov. 20, 2026 in Middletown, Iowa. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

People living near Middletown, some of whom have already sealed their windows and doors, have been advised to stay indoors and wash outdoor surfaces with water afterward.

Fox News Digital’s Alex Nitzberg and Sophia Compton, along with The Associated Press, contributed to this report.

#5885. Huang’s Return Is the Biggest National Security Scandal in U.S. History

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Huang’s Return Is the Biggest National Security Scandal in U.S. History

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned in disgrace on Nov. 12 after his affair with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, was exposed and it became apparent that Nvidia was complicit in China’s evasion of U.S. export controls.

Now, under pressure from U.S. President Donald Trump, Nvidia’s board has reinstated Jensen Huang, saying that senior government officials promised that he posed “no national security risk.” This may be the biggest national security scandal in U.S. history.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned in disgrace on Nov. 12 after his affair with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, was exposed and it became apparent that Nvidia was complicit in China’s evasion of U.S. export controls.

Now, under pressure from U.S. President Donald Trump, Nvidia’s board has reinstated Jensen Huang, saying that senior government officials promised that he posed “no national security risk.” This may be the biggest national security scandal in U.S. history.

That is a high bar. Consider the nearest competitor, the Iran-Contra affair. Officials in U.S. President Ronald Reagan’s administration, with the president’s knowledge, secretly sold weapons to Iran during the Iran-Iraq War, circumventing an arms embargo that Reagan publicly supported.

They did so both to secure Iran’s help in freeing U.S. hostages in Lebanon and to raise money for the Contras, a far-right Nicaraguan militant organization whose members committed atrocities against Nicaraguans and Americans alike. Once investigations began, officials orchestrated a cover-up. Reagan’s successor pardoned six individuals connected to the affair.

As morally foul as Iran-Contra was, it did not involve personal profiteering by the president and his associates. Nor did it involve the United States helping its principal geopolitical rival. Now, consider what is known about the Jensen Huang affair.

China and the United States are locked in an intense race over artificial intelligence. Compute—the raw processing power that depends on advanced chips—is central to that contest. The United States has an unusually powerful position in this market, as most advanced chips are designed by Nvidia and largely manufactured by the Taiwanese firm TSMC.

Both Nvidia and the White House were warned that Jensen Huang—a close Trump ally—was having an affair with a Chinese spy, including in a memo to senior officials overseen by U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio. But neither Trump nor Nvidia acted on these warnings.

Meanwhile, U.S. tech policy underwent a dramatic reversal. Last December, Nvidia struck a deal with the White House to sell its H200 chips to China in exchange for a 25 percent cut for the U.S. government, with no public details about how the funds will be used or overseen. Huang donated $25 million to the president’s ballroom, and Trump owns large amounts of Nvidia stock.

When news of the affair broke shortly after the U.S. midterms, it set off a cascade of revelations and investigations. Alice Huang, along with her husband and her business partner in Singapore, were found in multiple outlets’ reporting to be deeply involved in a network of firms allegedly established to evade U.S. restrictions and funnel advanced chips to China. Lawmakers also released information that revealed Nvidia had shipped more than 800,000 advanced chips to Chinese companies as of May 11, including to firms closely linked to the Chinese military.

A struggle broke out within Trump’s court, as Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick for failing to prevent China from accessing these chips. On top of that, Jensen Huang reportedly had another affair with a direct report and presided over an allegedly abusive workplace culture at Nvidia.

Trump’s response was to pressure Nvidia’s board for Huang’s reinstatement, potentially signaling that his administration would protect the company from punishment for its misdeeds. By Trump’s own admission, the decision followed a lengthy conversation with Chinese President Xi Jinping about the Nvidia leader. On Friday, Trump issued an executive order attempting to forestall congressional investigation into the deal.

The immediate beneficiary, apart from Nvidia’s stock price, is Beijing. China can now dismiss U.S. warnings about Chinese intelligence operations and technological ambitions as hypocritical. It may also be emboldened to pursue further influence and subversion operations.

Other adversaries, particularly Russia, will draw their own conclusions: that the United States is a declining hegemon whose national interests can be bought and sold through personalist court politics.

The scandal is unlikely to disappear quietly. Concerned intelligence and national security officials may continue leaking documents about Nvidia, potentially exposing other instances of corruption. Democrats will take this up as a major issue in the new Congress in January, and some Republicans may even join them.

The U.S. media now has a choice. It can treat this as a major national scandal, as it did Hillary Clinton’s emails in 2016 or Harvard President Claudine Gay’s improper academic citations in 2023, with front-page scrutiny for weeks or months. Or it can treat the affair as just another episode of the Trump show, or as a partisan political issue, and move on.

Supposed China hawks in the Trump administration, including Rubio and Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby, face a similar choice.

They can resign in protest, drawing national attention to the scandal and positioning themselves as a different kind of Republican for a future, cleaner administration. Or they can continue serving a president that puts his pocketbook ahead of national security. But they cannot keep telling themselves that they can make a difference from the inside.

James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social

#5883. Is China Misreading the Moment?

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

RUSH DOSHI is an Assistant Professor at Georgetown University and Director of the China Strategy Initiative at the Council on Foreign Relations. He served as Director and Deputy Senior Director for China and Taiwan Affairs at the National Security Council from 2021 to 2024.

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

When U.S. President Donald Trump lands in Shenzhen later this week for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, he will sit down with Chinese leader Xi Jinping for the fourth time amid an unexpected détente between Washington and Beijing. The trade war of 2025 is in abeyance, the two governments have announced a relationship of “constructive strategic stability,” and, to the alarm of American allies, Trump and Xi are planning to meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin. Beneath the surface, however, the détente is beginning to crack.

The relationship between Washington and Beijing looks remarkably different from the way it did a little over a year ago, when the two sides agreed to a truce in Busan, South Korea. In April 2025, Washington initiated the trade war by raising tariffs on China past 140 percent, and Beijing responded by restricting rare-earth exports later that month and threatening another cutoff that October. After Trump promptly backed down, the two leaders reached the deal in Busan: Washington would cap tariffs and suspend a specific export control regulation known as the Affiliates Rule in exchange for Beijing’s suspension of new export controls on minerals and magnets and the resumption of their normal flow to the United States. On that basis, both sides pursued a broad deescalation.

Since then, the Trump administration has been notably accommodating. Washington has kept new tariffs at 12.5 percent, well below the 20-percent ceiling Beijing previously agreed to. It has let advanced AI chips flow to China and to Chinese cutouts in Southeast Asia and has not added a Chinese firm to the Entity List in over the year, the longest pause since 2008. It has withheld arms sales from and downgraded defense talks with Taiwan, cut back naval transits of the Taiwan Strait, and raised doubts about U.S. support in the event of a crisis. And it has offered only muted support to such allies as Japan and the Philippines in the face of escalating Chinese coercion. All the while, Trump has praised Xi as a “great leader” and extended rare courtesies, including by welcoming Xi on the tarmac when he arrived in Washington for a state visit in September, the first such greeting of a foreign leader in 60 years.

Beijing has appreciated this accommodating approach—and seized the opportunity to act more assertively. It has viewed the competitive pause as temporary, a product of China’s growing strength and Trump’s admiration for Xi, and as a way to buy time to build advantage before the next eruption of tensions. With Washington occupied by its war in Iran and humbled by defeat in the last round of the trade war, Beijing has throttled the supply of minerals and magnets to the United States, taken steps to ensure that its industrial and technological gains remain in China, and increased pressure on its neighbors.

So far, Beijing has used the time well, advancing its objectives with little serious pushback from Washington. But China risks overreaching in its relationship with the United States. Not only is an incoming Democratic Congress likely to constrain Trump’s conciliatory China policy. There are also growing frustrations over Beijing’s unwillingness to fulfill the terms agreed to in Busan. Pressure on AI chips and export controls are emerging in the wake of revelations about the Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang’s relationship with an alleged Chinese spy and his company’s influence over U.S. export control policy. And new U.S. policies in response to the scandal could provoke a response from Beijing.

Xi and Trump will meet in Shenzhen and again in December in Miami, but with the January expiration of the Busan agreement looming in the background (and with no summits planned for next year), time is running out for the two leaders to extend and strengthen the current truce. If the détente falls apart the next time Washington’s and Beijing’s interests clash, China will be better prepared. It may also, perhaps hubristically, be more willing to escalate.

Although China favors Washington’s current accommodating approach and hopes it will last as long as possible, Beijing is also confident that it is strengthening its hand for a more contentious future. For China’s leaders, the current détente has been less an end goal than a temporary intermission, a means to build leverage ahead of the next round of strategic competition and, ultimately, a decisive long-term advantage.

Prominent Chinese scholars see the calm that followed Busan as tactical rather than strategic. Chen Dongxiao, the president of the Shanghai Institutes for International Studies, told the Shanghai news outlet The Paper that the two countries remain in a “tactical truce.” In May, Wu Xinbo of Fudan University told Beijing News, “If we can achieve a relationship of constructive strategic stability over the next three years, it will extend China’s period of strategic stability and win us time and space for development.” Writing in the journal International Review in August, he declared that the current “tactical stability” was “neither sustainable nor solid.” Shen Weizhong, a former Chinese foreign ministry official, was more blunt: “The more time we have, the better prepared we might be towards a final showdown on Taiwan or maybe another trade war.”

Beijing attributes little of the stabilization to Washington. In his August essay, Wu wrote that it “is not the result of the U.S. side having a change of heart or showing goodwill, but was won by China through arduous striving and struggle.” The United States had no choice; it simply needed the “breathing room.” Wang Yong, the director of Peking University’s American Studies Center, described the trade war as “a tipping point in terms of strength.” The former editor of the Global Times, Hu Xijin, wrote in a Weibo post after the U.S. midterm elections that “the current equilibrium in U.S.-China relations is the result of a contest of strength.” As Wu told The Paper in September, Washington has come to understand that Beijing “needs to be treated equally and with respect” and that China is “bolder about ‘drawing its sword.’” Keeping the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets tight, to Beijing’s mind, might reinforce the current competitive pause rather than undermine it by reminding American leaders of China’s leverage.

Another critical factor in the current de-escalation, Chinese analysts argue, is Trump’s personality. Trump’s “distinct and unique decision-making style,” as Chen put it, has single-handedly blunted the more forceful defense of U.S. interests that members of his own administration and legislators in both parties favor. “He is often not entirely swayed by bipartisan opposition and still continues to pursue policies based on his own judgment,” Chen explained in the same interview. Writing in the Singaporean paper Lianhe Zaobao in September, Zhao Minghao of Fudan University more pointedly explained that Trump “admires power and prefers to engage with the leaders of powerful countries,” a stabilizing trait that benefits Beijing’s diplomacy while leaving allies and partners on the sidelines. “Of all modern U.S. presidents, Trump has cared the least about Taiwan,” one Guizhou-based commentator wrote. “He would readily sacrifice the island for his own interests.” An article reposted on the website of Qiushi, the Chinese Communist Party’s flagship journal, dwelt on Trump’s decision to greet Xi at the airport when he arrived for his state visit in September. The gesture, it claimed, “reflects the new historical position of China-U.S. relations.”

But China also worries that Trump’s influence will decline over the next two years and perhaps cease altogether after 2028. Wang Hao, a professor at Fudan University’s Center for American Studies, told Phoenix News that the Democratic Party’s approach will not be “as pragmatic and flexible as the Trump administration’s,” a sentiment echoed across Chinese social media after the Democrats’ victory in the midterm elections.

The awareness that Trump’s approach may not survive beyond his presidency is precisely why Beijing has sought to lock it in through diplomacy. According to the Chinese readout, at the summit in May 2026, Xi declared that the relationship of “constructive strategic stability” he and Trump had agreed to build would provide guidance for U.S.-Chinese relations “over the next three years and beyond.” That duration was not accidental. As the scholar Zheng Yongnian put it at the time, the three-year window was “tailor-made for President Trump,” matching his current term. Beijing hopes it will bind his successor.

If diplomacy fails to secure U.S. deescalation, China hopes leverage will succeed. Over the last year, Beijing has used the time and space created by the détente to strengthen its position in industrial and technological competition with the United States. Beijing has long sought to make the world more dependent on China while reducing its own dependence on the world. But over the last year, that approach has evolved. Beijing has unveiled a raft of measures intended to ensure that its industrial, technological, and scientific know-how do not diffuse beyond China’s borders in order to lock in the country’s advantages.

To keep foreign firms in China and blunt Western countries’ leverage over the Chinese economy, the State Council issued Orders 834 and 835, which effectively threaten companies that cut ties with Chinese partners or seek to exit the country with the risk of investigation, exclusion from the Chinese market, exit bans on executives, and possible criminal prosecution. To keep talent in China, Beijing has authorized exit bans on citizens considered possible risks to industrial or technological security and, according to Bloomberg, now requires travel approval for top AI and chip specialists and their families. And to keep scientific discoveries in China, the Ministry of State Security has warned researchers that they must clear security reviews before submitting articles to foreign journals. China has also imposed or proposed new export controls on dozens of technologies, from mining and batteries to AI models and biotechnology in order to protect its industrial capabilities. Beijing has even worked to prevent the transfer of equipment and engineers to India and Southeast Asia, stalled battery ventures with Indian firms, undermined Apple’s effort to move manufacturing to India, and issued a sweeping regulation that subjects Chinese investment abroad to a national security review more expansive than the United States’ own outbound investment review.

Beijing’s rare-earth licensing regime exemplifies China’s leverage-building strategy. To obtain a license, foreign buyers must hand over customer lists, the identities of their end users, and images of their production lines, all of which gives China insight into Western dependencies and helps it uncover chokepoints it can squeeze. Beijing has threatened to use these powers against countries that seek to protect themselves from the flood of Chinese manufactured goods entering their markets. In July, Beijing showed how precise it can now be in applying pressure when it cut off 14 European firms, including the German arms maker Rheinmetall, from Chinese dual-use exports in retaliation for EU sanctions related to Chinese support of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Beijing has paired these measures at home with escalating pressure campaigns in its near abroad, betting that a distracted and chastened Washington will not come to the aid of China’s neighbors. That campaign has been most brazen in the South China Sea, where China has engaged in escalations unseen before the détente. Since Busan, Beijing has essentially abrogated its 2024 understanding with the Philippines, which maintained stability on the question of Second Thomas Shoal. In July, a Chinese coast guard boat rammed a Philippine navy inflatable and its crew clubbed a sailor on the head with a wooden baton, the most violent assault on Philippine personnel in years.

The timing of these incidents has been provocative. In July, when Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi and U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio were in Manila for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations meetings, the coast guard launched water-cannon attacks on Philippine vessels near Scarborough Shoal. Then, in September, during Xi’s U.S. state visit, Chinese warships and cutters blocked a Philippine resupply mission outright for the first time in two years. In October, Chinese cutters interfered with a medical evacuation from Second Thomas Shoal, delaying a patient transfer by ten hours. Last month, Beijing’s ambassador in Manila mused that if China chose to use force against the Philippines, “we may easily win.”

China has also run its broadest coercion campaign against Japan in more than a decade, nominally in response to comments made by Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi on Taiwan. It has included a travel advisory, a seafood ban, a dual-use export ban, and curbs on rare-earth exports that have forced Japanese companies to contemplate shutting down operations. Chinese carrier-based fighters have locked their weapons-targeting radar onto Japanese jets. Beijing has also pursued new gas structures in the East China Sea and set a record 357-day coast guard presence in 2025 around the Japanese-administered Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands), with cutters now chasing Japanese fishing boats into territorial waters. Washington’s support for Tokyo has, so far, been limited.

And around Taiwan, China has made its pressure quieter but more routine. In December 2025, during the People’s Liberation Army’s Justice Mission exercises, China fired live rockets closer to Taiwan than ever before, landing ten of them inside the island’s 24-nautical-mile contiguous zone. As of this summer, Beijing has reduced air incursions, but coast guard activity around Taiwan’s outlying islands has become routinized. And since June, Beijing has run standing “law enforcement patrols” east of Taiwan, asserting jurisdiction over the waters Taipei would need in a blockade.

Outside of Asia, Beijing has sought to take advantage of anger, particularly in the developing world, at the Trump administration’s “Liberation Day” tariffs and the Iran war. Before traveling to Washington, Xi made a point of stopping in several capitals: he toured Bishkek and Cairo, and attended the BRICS summit in New Delhi, stressing that China, not the United States, is now the steadier partner.

Beijing has reason to think this approach is working. It rightly judged that Washington would prioritize keeping the détente alive over responding to its provocations. A year after Busan, China holds a tighter grip on critical supply chains, has set new precedents in Asia, and faces an adversary in Washington that has grown used to holding back. Beijing likely believes it is better off now than it was when the truce began.

What looks like success now, however, may still prove to be overreach. In addition to rare earths and magnets, several important questions went unresolved in past U.S.-Chinese summits and are likely to resurface in this week’s summit in Shenzhen, including China’s anticipated purchases of Boeing aircraft and agricultural products, AI safety, and the United States’ suspension of arms sales to Taiwan. But the most important question is what will become of the current trade truce.

The United States has complied with the terms of the Busan agreement, suspending the Affiliates Rule and lowering tariffs, but Beijing has throttled the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets to the United States. U.S. officials say China has delivered only about two-thirds of what it promised, and trade data suggest the shortfall is larger still. The administration believes Beijing’s noncompliance is deliberate. Anecdotal evidence from American business leaders in regular touch with the Ministry of Commerce confirms the administration’s assessment that Beijing seeks to keep a tight leash on rare-earth minerals and will occasionally “yank the chain” on individual companies in response to any perceived slight by Washington.

From the U.S. perspective, the very point of the Busan negotiations was to avoid this situation. Indeed, the U.S. fact sheet at Busan indicated that China agreed to issue general licenses for a number of rare earths and other critical minerals to American end users and their suppliers worldwide; case-by-case licenses, which could be delayed by Beijing, would not be required. China has not denied making that commitment, and a few weeks after the summit, the Ministry of Commerce began issuing a new category of general licenses to magnet makers. But Beijing’s licenses have been narrow and firm specific, and American industry continues to face enormous challenges in securing certain rare earths, notably yttrium and scandium.

Beijing’s intention may not be to undermine the Busan agreement but, paradoxically, to strengthen it. In keeping with its belief that strength begets American conciliation, China’s strategy appears to be to occasionally impose shortages to remind Washington of the leverage that Beijing continues to enjoy. But the tactic is beginning to backfire. Shortages of yttrium, used for jet engines and gas turbines, have forced some suppliers to suspend production. Chipmakers are running short of scandium. All of this strengthens the arguments of those who believe Beijing never intended to honor Busan and that the United States should begin to walk away from the agreement. If Washington concludes that China will weaponize rare earths no matter what the United States does, the case for American restraint may fall apart, and with it the détente that has bought Beijing the time it prizes.

Tensions are also mounting in other areas. Each side is increasingly frustrated with the other’s unilateral economic measures and restrictions on technology, particularly those that were not addressed in Busan: for Beijing, U.S. import controls, outbound investment regimes, and licensing backlogs; for Washington, Chinese export controls and State Council directives. A Democratic Congress is less likely to countenance Trump’s China policy and more likely to pursue investigations into China’s espionage and influence efforts and potential malfeasance at the Department of Commerce. The explosive controversy around Huang and Nvidia has the potential to inject fresh tensions into the relationship, particularly on matters related to AI and export controls. The Department of Commerce has already issued a new interim final rule to stop Chinese firms from smuggling chips into China or remotely accessing American AI chips in Southeast Asian data centers. Administration officials insist that closing a loophole China itself claims not to use “should not be something that generates countermeasures.” But Beijing may well retaliate before the rule takes effect in February.

These headwinds make breakthroughs in Shenzhen on purchases, artificial intelligence, nuclear weapons, or Iran less likely. With the truce, extended last week by just two months, set to formally expire on January 10, the summit is one of the last opportunities for Trump and Xi to reach even a modest declaration that they are working to extend the terms set out in Busan and will aim to conclude negotiations in December, when Xi comes to Miami for the G-20 summit.

If China were to continue to throttle rare-earth minerals and magnets, and a January breakdown did come to pass, the two sides might find themselves in another round of escalation. Xi may arrive at the two upcoming summits holding a stronger hand than he did in Busan. But his actions are gradually producing a counterpart with less room—and perhaps less reason—to keep folding.

How a U.S.-Backed Offensive Could Help Bogotá Reclaim Its Conflict Zones

How Fighting Weakens Washington’s Reputation—and Backing Down May Strengthen It

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5880. Democrats Want to Make Life More Affordable. They Could Begin With Permitting Reform.

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Democrats Want to Make Life More Affordable. They Could Begin With Permitting Reform.

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. A bill that passed the Senate last week to reform the nation’s permitting laws offers Democrats—who voted two-to-one against the bill in the Senate—an early test of whether they can make daily life more affordable for Americans, even before taking control in January. Permitting reform may seem like a narrow or technical topic, but this is the most important piece of energy legislation in years. By speeding up environmental reviews, limiting litigation delays, and easing transmission siting, it would expand clean energy, lower energy prices, strengthen our energy system, and make future climate action more effective.

The bill, known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, would make it easier to build transmission lines and other energy infrastructure, including both clean energy and oil and gas projects. That breadth has delighted advocates of abundance, the movement that argues that shortcomings in public infrastructure stem from regulatory bottlenecks, and enraged many environmentalists. Some supporters have been too quick to dismiss legitimate concerns, while some climate activists have drowned out substantive but highly technical legal objections with reflexive anti-fossil fuel rhetoric. As the bill heads to a House vote, a careful assessment of its provisions should lead members to support it while pursuing opportunities where possible to address credible objections in the final stages of the legislative process.

The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. A bill that passed the Senate last week to reform the nation’s permitting laws offers Democrats—who voted two-to-one against the bill in the Senate—an early test of whether they can make daily life more affordable for Americans, even before taking control in January. Permitting reform may seem like a narrow or technical topic, but this is the most important piece of energy legislation in years. By speeding up environmental reviews, limiting litigation delays, and easing transmission siting, it would expand clean energy, lower energy prices, strengthen our energy system, and make future climate action more effective.

The bill, known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, would make it easier to build transmission lines and other energy infrastructure, including both clean energy and oil and gas projects. That breadth has delighted advocates of abundance, the movement that argues that shortcomings in public infrastructure stem from regulatory bottlenecks, and enraged many environmentalists. Some supporters have been too quick to dismiss legitimate concerns, while some climate activists have drowned out substantive but highly technical legal objections with reflexive anti-fossil fuel rhetoric. As the bill heads to a House vote, a careful assessment of its provisions should lead members to support it while pursuing opportunities where possible to address credible objections in the final stages of the legislative process.

Objections to the permitting reform bill fall into several broad categories. First, some Democrats and environmental groups argue that the blue election wave gives them leverage to secure a better deal next year, yet that misreads both the politics and the policy. A larger Democratic majority does not necessarily mean a majority for blocking fossil fuel infrastructure. The incoming caucus includes members who support oil and gas development alongside clean energy—in states such as Texas, Mississippi, Kansas, South Carolina, and Alaska. Nor does 56 Senate seats eliminate the need for Republican votes to overcome a filibuster. And Trump will still be president, with the power to veto the bill.

Second, some environmental groups oppose the bill because it weakens tools of environmental review and permitting often used to block fossil fuel projects such as oil and gas pipelines. Yet ample evidence demonstrates that trying to “keep it in the ground”—stymying fossil fuel supply by blocking infrastructure—does little to reduce global oil and gas use, which ultimately is what matters for greenhouse gas emissions, and can trigger backlash to climate action if energy security and affordability are perceived to suffer as a result.

In a global market, supply restrictions in one location merely lead to increases elsewhere unless demand falls. Higher prices can curb demand, but this year’s energy crisis offers further evidence that political and economic pressures push elected leaders to use every available tool to bring prices back down. The Iran war led to the closure of much of the Strait of Hormuz, through which roughly 20 percent of the world’s oil flowed, yet oil demand this year will drop only 2.5 percent, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA).

While it is possible—and necessary—to reduce the need for oil and gas over the long term, today fossil fuels still supply roughly four-fifths of the world’s energy, a figure that has barely changed in decades. While reducing that dependence is urgently needed to address the rapidly escalating threats of climate change, the hard reality is that keeping energy affordable and reliable during a multidecade transition still requires investment in oil and gas. According to the IEA, around $490 billion a year would still be needed by 2035 even in a scenario where countries meet their climate pledges—which the collapse of United Nations climate talks in Turkey made plainly clear is far from being true today.

Moreover, the same strategies used against high-profile pipelines such as Keystone XL and Mountain Valley have often proved effective at obstructing clean energy projects as well. As the New York Times editorial board argued this month in endorsing permitting reform, essential laws protecting the environment, communities, and Indigenous peoples “have become so complex and restrictive that the country cannot build the future it needs.”

To be sure, the BAAJA is not a substitute for climate policy, nor are its emissions benefits assured. Analysis by Rhodium Group, a consultancy, projects that the bill could reduce U.S. power-sector emissions in 2040 by as much as 18 percent compared with current policy, or by as little as 1 percent, depending on energy prices and technology costs. At the same time, climate policy cannot be effective without a bill such as the BAAJA that allows for the rapid buildout of the generation, transmission, storage, and transit infrastructure required for cleaner energy solutions.

A third area of concern involves weakened tribal consultation under the National Historic Preservation Act. Faster permitting should not undermine tribes’ ability to meaningfully influence decisions that affect sacred sites and other places central to their history and traditions. A set of revisions to the bill would have expanded the projects subject to tribal consultation and authorized additional money to support tribal and state historic-preservation work, yet Democratic Senator Jeff Merkley prevented those amendments from being considered, which may make it harder for some House Democrats to support the bill.

Expanding the U.S. grid is vital to staying globally competitive.

Ones and Tooze on permitting reform and what that means for U.S. energy production.

Finally, some opponents worry that specific provisions could protect improperly approved projects, require the government to pay companies large sums in damages, and constrain future administrations’ ability to issue or enforce environmental protections. Because these objections involve detailed, often technical, legal objections to the bill’s drafting, they have failed to garner the same attention as absolutist rhetoric about fossil fuels or misleading claims about data center buildout, which is unfortunate because many of these legal concerns have merit, even if they do not ultimately justify opposing the bill.

At the heart of critics’ concerns is a mistrust of how a conservative judiciary hostile to environmental regulation will interpret the bill’s ambiguities. Key areas of concern include new limits on National Environmental Policy Act lawsuits and potential unintended consequences of provisions to limit an administration’s ability to revoke existing permits. Those so-called permit certainty provisions, which the New Yorker’s Elizabeth Kolbert called the most “dangerous” in the bill, were designed to prevent just the sort of harm the Trump administration has done to offshore wind.

Yet subsequent revisions to the bill, known as the “manager’s amendment,” made meaningful improvements to address those concerns. Kolbert writes that the bill could “tie the hands of any future Administration that wants to responsibly promote green energy over fossil fuels,” yet the revisions specifically added an exception to address this concern for agency actions taken under applicable law or regulation and a clause intended to preserve existing legal authorities. They responded to concerns that restrictions on government interference with a permitted project’s operation at “full capacity” could be used to block new environmental rules—for example, emissions standards under the Clean Air Act that would induce a coal plant to curb its output. The amendment also extends from five to 10 years the permitting-data lookback used to assess claims of disparate treatment. That longer time period addressed concerns that Trump’s preferential treatment of fossil fuels and obstruction of renewables would become the benchmark against which a future administration’s permitting decisions are judged.

The changes also require a showing of bad faith for the additional damages available under the bill, addressing concerns that an agency could face enormous payouts for an action a court finds unlawful. And the amendment expressly allows the government to offer lawful explanations for differences in permitting outcomes and extends the requirement that applicants seeking relief demonstrate technical and financial capacity to all of their pending projects.

But the changes do not eliminate every risk. For example, an expansive interpretation of what counts as a “permit” or “authorization” could, critics fear, extend protections beyond formal agency approvals to informal assurances from the Trump administration. Critics also worry that because nearly every agency action invokes some legal authority, a broad reading of the exception for actions “taken pursuant to any applicable law or regulation” could render the permit protections meaningless. To avoid that result, judges could read the exception narrowly, limiting future administrations’ ability to curb pollution.

Another round of revisions—the one blocked by Merkley—would have made modest improvements, such as to the concern about private assurances counting as authorizations. The legal risks identified about how hostile judges could interpret ambiguity merit attention and should be remedied to the extent possible as the current bill moves through Congress. Although the bill is unlikely to be amended in the House, one of its architects in the Senate has suggested the House might attach amendments to other pieces of must-pass legislation.

Even without those revisions, however, the bill’s benefits outweigh the risks. The current permitting system simply makes it too difficult and costly to expand the grid, build a stronger energy system, connect cleaner generation, and lower energy costs.

Later this week, Americans will gather for Thanksgiving, with energy costs weighing on the price of traveling to see family, heating homes, and cooking the holiday meal. The annual national observance of Thanksgiving started with President Abraham Lincoln’s proclamation in 1863—the same year construction began on the transcontinental railroad. Six years later, the two lines met in Utah, connecting nearly 1,800 miles of track. By contrast, California today aims to begin high-speed rail service between Merced and Bakersfield—a distance of less than 200 miles—in 2033, a quarter-century after voters approved the project. When members of Congress return from their Thanksgiving celebrations, their first order of business should be to make it easier to build again in this country—starting with the infrastructure needed for a cleaner, more affordable, and more reliable energy system.

Jason Bordoff is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the founding director of Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy. He is a professor of professional practice in international and public affairs, co-founding dean emeritus at the Columbia Climate School, and former senior director on the staff of the U.S. National Security Council and special assistant to former U.S. President Barack Obama. President Donald Trump will award former New York City Mayor Rudy Giuliani the Presidential Medal of Freedom on Tuesday during a closed-press ceremony at the White House, honoring the longtime prosecutor and "America's Mayor" for his lifetime of public service.

The Presidential Medal of Freedom is the nation's highest civilian honor, alongside the Congressional Gold Medal, awarded to individuals who have made especially meritorious contributions to U.S. security, national interests, world peace, or significant public and private endeavors.

"I am honored and humbled to receive the Presidential Medal of Freedom from my nation and from our Commander-in-Chief, President Donald J. Trump," Giuliani, 82, said in a statement.

#5877. America’s Hidden AI Advantage

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JEFFREY DING is Assistant Professor of Political Science at George Washington University and the author of Technology and the Rise of Great Powers: How Diffusion Shapes Economic Competition.

The United States—Not China—Dominates Diffusion

In the view of most observers, China is winning the race to diffuse artificial intelligence across the economy. Even if the United States has a small lead in frontier AI models, the consensus is that China is better at applying the technology. As Kevin Rudd, former prime minister of Australia and a prominent observer of Chinese politics, declared at a conference in October, China is “miles ahead” in spreading AI “into the real economy.” This fits a familiar narrative about the technological rivalry between the two great powers: the United States innovates while China turns those innovations into practical applications at scale.

This narrative has become central to discussions of U.S.-Chinese competition in AI. But it doesn’t match reality. After the Chinese startup DeepSeek shocked the world in early 2025 with the announcement of an open-source large language model that rivaled the capabilities of frontier models made by American companies, the international media published breathless reports on how DeepSeek’s model had rapidly spread across state-owned enterprises, hospitals, and provincial and local governments. Months later, however, analysts determined that this adoption was shallow, narrow, and slow; the technology was being used on clunky “all-in-one machines” embedded on isolated hardware units within these institutions, rather than integrated more broadly into the software services they were using. Then, in January 2026, outside observers marveled at OpenClaw, a new open-source AI agent, as a sign of China’s diffusion advantage. Now, the excitement about these open-source agents has dissipated as security vulnerabilities and inefficiency have limited their uptake.

It is the United States, not China, that has a decisive lead in AI diffusion. This is because the United States has comparative advantages in both its overall level of economic development and the nature of its growth model. China’s underdeveloped software market, weak technology transfer mechanisms, and centralized economic system make AI diffusion much more challenging. All told, the United States’ advantage in diffusing the technology through the economy is likely to persist for the foreseeable future.

Despite its reputation as a pioneer of digital technology such as cashless payments and messaging apps, China’s overall digitization levels—the extent to which software and other digital tools have penetrated various sectors of the economy—are far lower than those in the United States. American firms are on average more than twice as likely as their Chinese competitors to adopt cloud computing services. And the U.S. enterprise software market, which includes services to manage customer relationships and provide business intelligence, is approximately ten times larger than China’s. According to the 2022 Global Cloud Ecosystem Index, which ranked 76 countries on their access to and usage of cloud services, China sat in the bottom half; the United States, by contrast, ranked 17th, not far behind the top performers, in part because of its dominance of “software as a service” products in which businesses can gain access to enterprise software without needing to install and maintain the programs themselves.

China’s relatively low penetration of digital services is a barrier to diffusing AI. When small Chinese hospitals tried to adopt DeepSeek, for instance, they lacked information systems that could collect and manage the data necessary to take full advantage of AI models. In these hospitals, which include many small health facilities in rural areas, patient data is still recorded on paper; where digital records do exist, they are often incomplete or vary from hospital to hospital. By contrast, 95 percent of U.S. doctors maintain electronic health record systems in their offices. As a result, U.S. hospitals have more smoothly integrated AI into electronic health records to recommend treatments, predict health risks, and simplify billing.

China’s underdeveloped cloud and enterprise software ecosystem also explains why the OpenClaw hype did not translate to sustainable diffusion. Open-source tools like OpenClaw appeal to hobbyists and individual developers who are keen to experiment and are less sensitive to security issues. But small businesses have been slow to adopt such tools because they prefer reliable, standardized solutions that are easy to implement and update. Small businesses are more likely to rely on cloud-based tools and enterprise software to implement AI upgrades than to try to experiment on their own—two channels that are less robust in China than in the United States.

The United States also has an advantage over China because of linkages within the science and technology ecosystem that help disseminate advances. Collaboration between universities and businesses is critical to diffusing AI breakthroughs across the entire economy because these connections bring together innovators who can develop new AI products and implementers who can effectively commercialize them.

In this dimension, the United States is far ahead of China. Americans have long led the world in the publication of articles about AI co-authored by at least one researcher from academia and one researcher from industry. Between 2015 and 2019, U.S. researchers published more than twice as many such articles as their Chinese counterparts. The gap extends beyond AI: across the life sciences and natural sciences, the share of publications in the United States that involved both university and industry researchers was also more than double the comparable rate in China. Beijing recognizes this gap: China’s official state news agency ranked weak technical exchanges between industry and academia as one of the five crucial shortcomings of the country’s AI ecosystem.

Although Chinese policymakers are aware of this challenge and have promoted policies to try to spur collaboration between universities and businesses, especially in AI, it will be difficult to cultivate the requisite trust needed for these two sectors to work together. China’s weak intellectual property protections, for example, discourage universities from pursuing partnerships with industry actors because they fear losing ownership of their inventions. The United States, by contrast, has a tradition of university-industry collaboration. The 1980 Bayh-Dole Act, which provides incentives and patent protections that encourage universities to commercialize inventions and license them to the private sector, kick-started decades of collaboration. Without a similar connective tissue to link academic institutions and businesses, Chinese universities are not as equipped to form profitable partnerships with companies. Chinese technical and engineering education is also disconnected from commercial enterprises, meaning that schools do not teach students the specific skills that companies need in their workforces.

Weak technology transfer mechanisms also undermine one of China’s main strengths in AI diffusion: its massive talent base. Analysis by the Information Technology and Innovation Foundation showed that in 2022, China awarded around 50,000 doctoral degrees in science, technology, engineering, and math, compared with only about 34,000 in the United States. Although the sheer quantity of Chinese computer science graduates may eventually help the country adopt AI advances at scale, the quality of that education determines how quickly or effectively education can encourage AI diffusion. If training is divorced from practical applications, graduates will struggle to adapt cutting-edge advances from industry labs into the sectors in which they work. Consider one commonly used standard to measure the baseline quality of AI education: whether a university employs at least one researcher who has published at least one paper at a leading AI conference. According to data from 2023–24, only 38 universities in China met this standard; the United States accounted for 150.

China’s centrally planned approach to AI development is also counterproductive when it comes to diffusion. In 2025, China developed an “AI Plus” action plan that outlines targets for AI adoption over the next decade and released guidelines for government agencies and local officials on how to put the plan into effect across the country. For many observers, the mere existence of a plan showed that the highest levels of Chinese leadership were committed to spreading AI widely and that China was better positioned to win the AI race than the United States, which lacks any kind of similar diffusion blueprint. Rudd’s claims about China’s edge in AI adoption, for instance, cite Beijing’s ambitious policies and targets for AI diffusion as supporting evidence.

But centralized approaches such as China’s often impede technology diffusion, especially when the state tries to intervene in domains in which the development trajectory is uncertain. One of the planks of China’s AI Plus plan identifies “global cooperation” as a priority sector for AI adoption. If local governments were to implement the plan as outlined, it would shift resources from areas in which diffusion has been proven to be effective, such as health care, to the undefined space of “global cooperation.” More broadly, Beijing’s favoritism toward state-owned enterprises and its preference for picking national champions often prop up inefficient technologies that would have been weeded out by market-based competition. Other businesses are unlikely to adopt inefficient technologies, making nonmarket forms of AI development less likely to diffuse widely.

Counterintuitively, the lack of a clear U.S. policy on AI diffusion is a boon, not a hindrance, to the United States’ attempt to diffuse the technology across the economy. At the height of U.S.-Japanese competition in the 1980s, for instance, when the two countries were racing to train software engineers who could spread computer technology, Japan’s Ministry of Education, Science, and Culture, as it was then known, exercised centralized control over universities. But emerging trends in computer science developed faster than the government could adapt. By concentrating state funds on centers of excellence and imposing one-size-fits-all rules on the computing curriculum, Japan could not match the adaptability of American universities, and the United States surged ahead. Cross-national studies have shown that decentralized science and technology systems are correlated with faster diffusion of new innovations.

Another historical case with parallels to China, the Soviet Union, further suggests how centralization is antithetical to diffusion. In the twentieth century, the Soviet Union boasted many plans for technology development. Yet its top-down approach, which focused on large-scale engineering projects, undercut market-based instruments that encourage technology diffusion through competition. As a result, although the Soviet Union pioneered new technologies in many areas, including the casting of steel, it fell behind the United States in adopting general-purpose technologies such as computers. A prescient U.S. intelligence report published in 1969 deemed the Soviet Union’s mechanisms for technology diffusion as “much more balky” than those of the United States because of the absence of competitive pressures as well as limited linkages between the R & D system and the broader economy.

The myth of China’s lead in AI diffusion persists despite clear data showing U.S. advantages. In part, this may be because China’s innovation system has facilitated the widespread adoption of certain visible technologies. In less than 20 years, China has built more than 30,000 miles of high-speed rail, for example, blanketing the country with ultra-fast trains that now reach even the most remote provinces.

But infrastructure presents a very different challenge than AI does. The centralized pressure that officials can use to effectively develop large-scale infrastructure projects is much less effective for general-purpose technologies like AI because it is infeasible to mandate that all small- and medium-size firms, spread across every sector of the economy, adopt artificial intelligence. Those companies would do so only if it made sense from a business perspective, and few have the digital foundations or savvy employees able to harness AI in a way that would help them grow. Moreover, the United States has outbuilt China in the infrastructure most essential to AI development, data centers, in which it retains a ten-to-one edge. In fact, in some cases, the data centers that China has built remain idle because of weak AI demand.

The misperception that China leads AI diffusion may continue unabated because analysis of the country’s technology ecosystem is overly focused on frontier firms such as ByteDance and DeepSeek and first-tier cities, such as Beijing and Shanghai. Yet AI diffusion ultimately rests on whether adoption occurs in medium size companies, small towns, and inland provinces, where it has been extremely limited. Because these settings rarely feature in outside media coverage, analysis of China’s AI diffusion landscape underestimates the difficulty of translating cutting-edge advances from frontier labs and universities in Hangzhou or Shenzhen to, say, a small firm in a county seat in northwestern Qinghai Province.

Perhaps the simplest explanation is that such a myth is useful, even if ultimately untrue. In some ways, the United States is trumpeting Beijing’s AI diffusion in the way that Chinese “tiger parents” try to motivate their children to work harder by raising the success of “that other family’s kid.” “Look at how well that other family’s kid performed at the piano recital,” they might say, without listening closely to ensure that the notes were exactly right. In great-power rivalry, too, there is a natural tendency to overhype the other side’s capabilities, in part to spur more effort at home.

But such a “useful” myth can backfire if it pushes the United States to overlook its own advantages or even to mimic China’s more top-down approach to AI development. By any measure, the United States maintains a comfortable lead in AI diffusion, and China, despite its impressive successes, has serious challenges. Although the narrative of Chinese dominance endures, for now all signs point to a U.S. lead that will remain strong.

In the U.S.-Chinese Tech Race, Diffusion Matters More Than Invention

Regulating AI Will Not Set America Back in the Technology Race
