#5876. Jensen Huang Is Back

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Jensen Huang Is Back

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the return of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, collapsed negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, and Ukraine’s naval drone attack.

Nvidia, the semiconductor giant at the center of the global artificial intelligence race, announced on Monday that it had reinstated its CEO, Jensen Huang, just 11 days after he was forced to resign following multiple alleged extramarital affairs.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the return of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, collapsed negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, and Ukraine’s naval drone attack.

Nvidia, the semiconductor giant at the center of the global artificial intelligence race, announced on Monday that it had reinstated its CEO, Jensen Huang, just 11 days after he was forced to resign following multiple alleged extramarital affairs.

Monday’s announcement marks a dramatic development in a saga that has roiled markets, seen the powerful company’s value plunge, and fueled uncertainty about the future of the AI boom. Plummeting stock prices had wiped some $2.6 trillion from Nvidia’s market value, and amid mounting scrutiny, the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission reportedly launched a probe into the company’s accounting practices.

Huang’s alleged affairs were made particularly scandalous—and geopolitically noteworthy—by the fact that one of those affairs was with a woman whom U.S. intelligence agencies suspect of being a Chinese spy. That revelation raised major national security concerns for a company whose most advanced AI chips remained banned from China.

Yet all those concerns now appear to have been cast aside at the behest of Huang’s most powerful backer: U.S. President Donald Trump. Trump has never wavered from Huang’s corner, calling him a “very high-quality person” and an “amazing guy” even after the Nvidia CEO resigned. Now, it appears that the U.S. president decided to put his thumb on the scale to bring Huang back.

On Monday, Nvidia’s stock price rose by 33 percent in the wake of the company’s announcement.

The Nvidia board said it had “received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.”

Huang said in a statement shared by the board that he was “honored” to return to Nvidia and would do so with “renewed commitment.” The board, meanwhile, said its previously announced independent investigation into Huang’s possible Chinese espionage exposure would proceed as planned and that Huang had pledged to cooperate with it.

Trump, who posted on Truth Social Sunday night that he hoped the Nvidia board would reinstate Huang, also hinted on Monday that such a decision was coming, even before it was officially confirmed.

“He’s back where he should be,” Trump said in a phone interview with PBS NewsHour minutes before the Nvidia announcement. “Well, he will be soon, hopefully,” he added. Trump also told CNBC after the announcement that he had spoken directly to Nvidia’s board and provided them with assurances on the national security threat. “I convinced them,” he said. “I made the deal.” Afterward, Trump called Huang and asked him to “work hard,” he told CNBC.

Some U.S. lawmakers from Trump’s own party appear to be less convinced. “Jensen Huang was compromised and exploited by a Chinese spy. Nvidia should have moved on without Huang and ignored the Administration’s inexplicable desire to save Huang,” Republican Rep. Don Bacon wrote in a post on X.

Texas Sen. John Cornyn was more pointed in his own X post. “No national security risk? Based on what?” Cornyn wrote. (Both Cornyn and Bacon are leaving Congress at the end of this year.)

Utah Sen. John Curtis also slammed the decision, declaring in a post on X that Huang “was right to step down when the affair came to light.” Curtis also wrote, “I have some questions. Who in our government weighed in? What did they tell the board? And on what basis did the board conclude there was no risk?”

Trump dismissed those concerns in his CNBC interview, agreeing when CNBC asked whether Republicans would “come around” on Huang’s reinstatement. As for the national security concerns, Trump dismissed new reporting that the Chinese woman whom Huang had a romantic relationship with, Alice Huang, was in contact with China’s Ministry of State Security—the country’s main intelligence agency—during their affair. “Who isn’t in contact?” Trump asked. “China speaks to everybody,” he said, adding, “They speak to me.”

Trump said he had spoken to Chinese President Xi Jinping “at length” about Jensen Huang during the two leaders’ meeting in Shenzhen, China, last week. The U.S. president also claimed that keeping Huang out of Nvidia would risk the CEO defecting to China. “I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen: ‘Hey, how would you like to come to China?’” Trump said. “He’s got a little blood in him, you know,” the U.S. president added, alluding to Huang’s Taiwanese heritage.

After the interview, Trump made his backing of Huang far more explicit with another post on Truth Social. The post featured an AI-generated recasting of a photo of Trump raising his fist after an assassination attempt in 2024; instead of Trump, the AI-generated version depicted Huang with an Nvidia flag in the background, raising his fist with a red bra in hand. “Sex sells,” Trump told CNBC.

Wednesday, Nov. 25: An EU-Western Balkans summit kicks off in Brussels.

Saturday, Nov. 28: Taiwan holds local elections.

Sunday, Nov. 29: Spain holds snap parliamentary elections.

Deep divisions. Negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, also known as COP31, collapsed on Sunday as countries clashed over a proposed deal and ultimately failed to reach consensus. It marks the first time that the climate talks have failed over a final deal in about 17 years, and it comes in a year that has seen record-shattering heat and other climate extremes.

The proposed deal had omitted any mention of fossil fuels, the primary driver of climate change. That exclusion sparked an uproar and prompted Pacific island nations and the European Union, among others, to reject it.

The proposed agreement “failed to meet the moment,” said Irish Environment Minister Darragh O’Brien, who was speaking on behalf of the European Union. “We will not endorse a decision that does not respond to the science and that actually risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required.”

Maritime strikes. Ukrainian forces deployed seven naval drones in a weekend attack against two Russian vessels located off the coast of Libya, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said in a post on X on Monday. The strikes sank the Russian corvette Merkury, which is one of Moscow’s newest ships, Zelensky said.

Drones also struck a Russian-owned cargo ship in the Baltic Sea on Saturday; the vessel later floated into Finnish waters. Finnish authorities believe that Ukraine perpetrated the attack, but Kyiv has not taken responsibility for it. “War is spilling over into our immediate vicinity,” Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo said in a post on X, though he added that “Ukraine has the right to fight back against the aggressor.”

Under pressure. South Korea’s education minister resigned on Sunday amid controversy over newly announced anti-cheating measures. After news emerged of a cheating scandal in a separate exam, South Korean officials unveiled a raft of rigid anti-AI cheating measures just days before the country’s annual college entrance exam, known as the Suneung.

The Suneung is a famously grueling eight-hour test that can be vital in shaping students’ career outcomes. Yet the last-minute announcement of the new rules, which included blaring emergency cellphone alerts, sparked confusion among test-takers. Ultimately, some 111 people were caught cheating, according to officials.

“I believe I am not free from criticism or blame from the public for failing to prepare adequately in advance,” the education minister said in a statement reported by local media.

On a stage in Ottawa over the weekend, Trump joked around with two Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carneys—or did he? The Canadian leader actually recruited two comedians to assist him with a roast at an annual dinner with journalists, enlisting impersonators both of himself and his U.S. counterpart. “I am pressing the emergency comedy button,” Carney joked.

#5872. The Model Middle Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW YEO is a Senior Fellow and SK-Korea Foundation Chair at the Brookings Institution and Professor of Politics at the Catholic University of America. This essay is adapted from his forthcoming book South Korea’s Grand Strategy: Narratives and the Rise of a Global Nation.

What South Korea Gets Right About Geopolitics

In the ten months since Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney gave his widely discussed speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos, the question of whether, and how, the world’s middle powers can help rebuild a broken international order has become increasingly urgent. In his remarks, Carney stressed that although the old U.S.-led alliance system was collapsing, middle powers—states that do not have the ability to reshape global politics but that still possess substantial might—were well positioned to pick up the slack. But to do so, Carney argued, they would need to build up their “strength at home” and “act together” to harness their collective power, even in the absence of backing from the world’s hegemon.

Although the Canadian prime minister’s bold vision was widely applauded, some experts thought his agenda was overly ambitious. They noted, correctly, that it would be very difficult for middle powers to build a grand alliance composed of countries as diverse as Brazil, Canada, South Africa, and Turkey. “The path to unity among middle powers is fraught because this group includes rich and poor countries, as well as small and large ones,” wrote the economist Eswar Prasad in February. Carney, however, is right that middle powers have an opportunity to thrive going forward. Even when aligning with great powers, these countries can still exercise greater autonomy by developing national capacity and diversifying external partnerships.

South Korea offers a prime example of this flexible approach. For decades, Seoul has pursued a middle-power strategy centered on building domestic strength and forging partnerships with democracies everywhere while deepening its alliance with Washington. South Korea has been adaptable, recalibrating its security alignments, economic strategies, and diplomatic roles as regional and global conditions require. This has enabled a country smaller in size than most of the world’s states to punch far above its weight, exercising substantial influence in Asia and beyond.

Not every government can follow Seoul’s path, and South Korea’s strategy has its limits. As Seoul itself acknowledges, middle powers cannot escape great-power competition. But the country has begun to show what middle powers can accomplish when they leverage their partnerships and their own strength to protect their interests and deter larger threats. And by maintaining and even building its ties to the United States while asserting greater autonomy, South Korea has proven that middle powers can strengthen themselves without building whole new networks—contrary to Carney’s suggestion.

According to classic international relations theory, grand strategy is the exclusive provenance of great powers. The United States’s effort to contain the Soviet Union during the Cold War is a paradigmatic example; China’s pursuit of global economic primacy is another. Middle powers, by contrast, have little say in such strategies. Instead, these countries are seen as primarily reactive in their foreign policies, navigating around the decisions of the great powers rather than setting the agenda themselves.

But recent history suggests that the division between great and middle powers is less clear-cut than has been assumed. In practice, middle powers very much have their own affirmative visions that they pursue in the world. They have the leeway to decide which threats matter most to them, which partnerships to prioritize, and where to invest scarce resources. Japan, concerned with the threat posed by China, has been quietly exercising leadership in the Indo-Pacific by improving its ties with neighbors, increasing investment and aid to the region, and strengthening its own defense capabilities—all while remaining closely allied with the United States. India, worried about China, Pakistan, and growing economic headwinds, has successfully bolstered partnerships with the United States and other democracies but maintained its relationship with Russia.

Much like Japan and India, South Korea is worried about its neighbors. But for Seoul, this is nothing new. South Korean leaders have been concerned with the very thing Carney fretted about in his Davos speech—becoming collateral damage in conflicts between great powers— since at least the late nineteenth century, when it was caught between China, Japan, and Russia. The country has a well-known proverb, “When whales fight, the shrimp's back is broken,” that captures the sentiment that great-power feuds can harm countries caught in the middle.

To safeguard against this threat, Seoul has long sought to increase its self-reliance through industrial policy. The country transited from light to heavy export industries in the 1970s, turning it into a major player in the chemical, steel, and shipbuilding sectors. State investments helped it become a major global producer of batteries and semiconductors in the present era. South Korea takes great pride in what historians call “the miracle on the Han”—or the country's rapid rise from a war-torn and impoverished state to a rich one during the late twentieth century. They now use the state’s wealth to promote public goods globally, contributing to international development, global health, climate policy, and multilateral bodies.

Seoul has also built up a formidable military, one that experts consistently rank as among the strongest in the world. Today, many South Korean analysts argue that Seoul needs a nuclear arsenal for self-defense, even though the country officially remains under Washington’s nuclear umbrella. The state has simultaneously used its armed prowess to deepen relations with U.S. allies. Seoul, for example, has inked multibillion-dollar arms deals with European buyers, making it a key partner in rebuilding NATO’s industrial capacity. In 2025, it was the second largest supplier of weapons to European NATO members (after the United States). South Korea has also forged defense partnerships in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, widening Seoul's options and reducing its dependence on any single market. South Korea, in other words, has been able to convert its economic resources and bureaucratic capabilities into greater influence by expanding its partnerships, strengthening its voice in international bodies, and helping shape global rules and norms.

But even as it charts its own path, Seoul has no plans to abandon the United States, at least in the near future. In fact, the sanctity of the U.S.-South Korean relationship is a significant historical Korean narrative. Many South Koreans see the alliance as a bond inked in blood, thanks to the sacrifice both countries made during the Korean War, when they worked to stop North Korea, backed by China and the Soviet Union, from overrunning the south. Most South Koreans correctly believe that the continued U.S. military presence in the country—currently, 28,500 American troops are stationed on the peninsula—has played a major part in deterring North Korean aggression over the past seven decades.

At first glance, South Korea’s strategy may seem contradictory. On the one hand, the country wants to deepen its cooperation with the United States, on which it still depends for security. But on the other, it seeks greater autonomy.

Yet these twin goals are not actually at odds. By cultivating ties with Washington, Seoul has gained access to advanced military capabilities and technologies as well as a larger diplomatic platform to expand its regional and global interests. It is in part because of this relationship, for example, that South Korea has had such success at selling weapons to multiple NATO members, including Croatia, Estonia, Finland, Norway, Poland, and Romania. (Seoul has also built up a formidable diplomatic apparatus in its own right.) At the same time, South Korea helps make this compact worthwhile for the United States by providing it with valuable technology, including semiconductor chips. Seoul has also partnered with the Washington to help revive the American shipbuilding industry, agreeing to invest $150 billion in the U.S. sector.

Still, South Korea’s growing push for more independence in its foreign policy does result in some tensions with the United States that cannot easily be resolved. For instance, the current government’s push to give Seoul, not Washington, operational control over both states’ forces on the peninsula in the event of war has been rebuffed by the U.S. military. American commanders instead favor a conditions-based transfer after South Korea has proven it has the capabilities to lead a joint defense, rather than setting a fixed date. The two sides also have not reached a consensus on what role South Korea would play if Taiwan is attacked or blockaded by China.

Yet so far, Seoul has done an effective job at managing these differences. For example, in his second term, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly accused Washington’s allies, including South Korea, of leeching off the United States and not paying their fair share. To extract more from Washington’s partners, Trump has implemented punishing tariffs. In response, South Korea could have been defiant and slapped on retaliatory restrictions, as other U.S. allies did. Instead, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung avoided rebuking the president and worked to meet American demands in ways that heightened the two states’ mutual dependence. For instance, Lee pledged to invest $350 billion in the United States to advance American economic and national security interests, including by purchasing more U.S. military equipment, and to increase South Korean economic support for locally stationed American forces.

Seoul’s strategy has not been a uniform success. The South Korean economy remains exposed to American and Chinese pressure despite its growth and diversification. North Korea is expanding its nuclear and missile forces and deepening military ties with Russia, creating new security headaches for which Seoul still needs U.S. assistance. The country is politically polarized, resulting in sharp policy shifts that can undermine long-term strategic planning. It also suffers from the lowest birthrate of any country in the world, threatening its tax base and the size of its armed forces. Beyond that, South Korea may not provide the ideal template for every middle power. Not every country in this category has similar economic resources, networks, or ties to the United States.

But on the whole, South Korea’s grand strategy has been effective, and it thus carries broader lessons. For one, it shows how adaptive middle powers can draw on their respective national narratives to find their strengths and to chart a path forward. Perhaps more important, it is proof positive that middle powers can exercise influence and achieve a measure of autonomy even without working together to build a neutral third pole, as Carney urged. It also illustrates that middle powers can remain firmly allied with the United States while developing their own independent capabilities. South Korea, then, demonstrates that states can reduce their vulnerability and widen their political options.

An earlier version of this article incorrectly stated that the South Korean government was known to be considering developing nuclear weapons.

With Its Allies, Seoul Has Enough Leverage to Push Back

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5871. Nvidia Is Now Too Big to Hold to Account

Argument: Nvidia Is Now Too Big to Hold to Account

Put out of mind the scenes of cheering you may have seen of Jensen Huang’s return to the headquarters of perhaps the world’s most important company, Nvidia, marking his personally triumphant comeback from being unanimously ousted by his board.

Get out of your thoughts any happy notions that you have reason to celebrate just because your money, like the money of so many millions of Americans and people well beyond the country’s borders, is tied up in the U.S. stock market, which soared at this surprising turn in the news after slumping badly in response to the scandals that had swirled around Huang.

Put out of mind the scenes of cheering you may have seen of Jensen Huang’s return to the headquarters of perhaps the world’s most important company, Nvidia, marking his personally triumphant comeback from being unanimously ousted by his board.

Get out of your thoughts any happy notions that you have reason to celebrate just because your money, like the money of so many millions of Americans and people well beyond the country’s borders, is tied up in the U.S. stock market, which soared at this surprising turn in the news after slumping badly in response to the scandals that had swirled around Huang.

What has happened in recent days, culminating in Huang’s return in the face of allegations he sustained a secret affair with a woman who was in touch with China’s spy agencies, is bad news for an economy that has grown increasingly imbalanced with the enormous growth of artificial intelligence. Far better for the U.S. economy and for everyone who has a stake in it would have been a reckoning for Nvidia that forced it to undertake the hard work of corporate reform and rebuilding trust.

Nvidia, for those who have not been paying attention, makes the most indispensable product in the world of AI: graphics processing units, a specialized type of chip, so capable and advanced that it has few real competitors, including, crucially, among well-funded Chinese companies. Nvidia, however, is much more than even this. As I have written before, it sits at the center of a highly incestuous economy of AI companies, including most of the big service providers, which it funds to keep purchasing its chips. The circular funding arrangements that Nvidia sustains with its largest customers—companies with extraordinary financial leverage and seemingly bottomless capital needs—is what keeps the AI economy’s party going. Without this, the merry-go-round of this new sector would grind to a halt and make what many thought looked like a bursting bubble in recent days seem like a minor blip.

Add to this the fact that the U.S. government and President Donald Trump have seemingly become convinced that everything and anything must be permitted with Nvidia, and with AI in general, or else China will overtake the United States in the most important technology of the near future. What this gives you is something more frightening than too big to fail: a company declared too strategically important to question or to hold to account.

The reality is that almost everything about the present picture is messed up and deeply worrisome. Let’s start with the fact that Trump, who already leads a government largely unconstrained by Congress and with wildly inflated executive powers, has taken so freely to intervening in the running of major companies. In Huang’s case, he played a central role in his reinstatement, as Monday’s news revealed.

There is nothing reassuring about this, but it gets worse. There is no reason to believe that Trump has an even remotely sophisticated grasp of the technologies at the heart of Nvidia’s business or its race against competitors. What we do know is that Trump has a weakness for very powerful and wealthy men, and Huang fits this billing. In the president’s universe, he is a winner, and Trump loves to associate himself with anyone invested with such an aura. Trump even posted an AI-generated photo of Huang intended to mirror a famous image of himself after surviving an assassination attempt, with the big difference that Huang was shown hoisting a red bra.

Trump, furthermore, is not known for a deep concern about ethics, and that is putting things gently. Where do ethics enter into this? Everywhere, from Huang’s honesty and the extent to which he was forthcoming about his affair with the suspicious Chinese national and shadowy customer, Alice Huang, as well as with at least one other woman who was his direct subordinate at Nvidia, in violation of corporate rules. Ethics are also involved in the way Huang ran his company, with a historically supine board, minimal internal controls on his behavior and oversight, and the way he promoted his family members. What matters is that nothing, inside the company or outside it, checked him. In fact, it seems likely that Trump, with his own record of serial marriages, rumored affairs, and children on the make, can relate.

But even these are mere starters. If competition with China deserves inclusion in any serious conversation about the future of AI, Trump’s treatment of Huang’s situation at Nvidia has been a running chronicle of flagrant disregard for national security. Let’s count the ways. First, when the U.S. government began to look into Alice Huang’s behavior on the suspicion that she might be a spy, it never informed Jensen Huang or gave him what would have been typical: a so-called defensive briefing so that he would know what to look out for and avoid giving away valuable trade secrets or selling his company’s most advanced chips without adequate controls preventing them from being shipped onward to China.

More broadly, historians may come to see Trump’s casual disregard for normal considerations of national security to be one of the most important and unusual hallmarks of his time in power. The examples are numerous. When shown evidence that Russia or China or some other powerful adversary is conducting secret operations against U.S. interests, Trump has often waved away concerns—in effect saying, well, we do that to them, too.

Last week offered the spectacle of Trump mugging in China with that country’s leader and Russia’s Vladimir Putin, with no hint that he had complained to them about offering targeting information to Iran that has enabled that country to attack U.S. bases in the Middle East, killing U.S. troops, as is the case with Russia, or supplying drone components to Iran, as with China. Trump went yet further, though, looking like a kid in a candy shop as he entered a room to greet an awaiting Putin and exclaiming, “He’s my friend.” Lest the message be lost on anyone, he later posted a picture of the meeting with this same message on social media.

Second, there is further reason to distrust the judgment of the U.S. government in the unfolding Nvidia saga. It lies in the vague and unforthcoming way its officials have spoken about Huang and his erstwhile Chinese mistress since their affair became public knowledge, always carefully avoiding clarity about whether or not she might have been a spy or whether his behavior damaged U.S. national security interests.

In fairness, the question of whether she was a spy involves some nuance. Alice Huang could very well have compromised U.S. national security without being a member or agent of any Chinese intelligence service in a formal sense. The lack of openness and straight language about the other question leaves little wiggle room, though. Through the Singapore-based company she worked for, Alice Huang was purchasing Nvidia’s most advanced chips, which fall under explicit U.S. export controls to China. This puts the public in a bewildering place. On the one hand, people are asked to believe that the United States must beat China in a zero-sum race toward the AI future. On the other, given the lack of candor about these Nvidia sales and the damage they may have caused to U.S. interests, we are being told, in effect, one of Trump’s favorite messages: Smile, and be happy. Lots of money was being made.

Taken together, Nvidia and Jensen Huang seem to concentrate enormous and growing risk for the entire public, as does the broader AI sector. Remember that Anthropic, the leading AI provider that went public recently, has warned of the potential power of its systems becoming so awesome as to having the potential to lead us all to an extinction-level event. And when the world’s top AI power is led by someone with such a casual disregard for safeguards, for strong governance, for ethics and for transparency, a company too important to question invites a peril that risks falling on us all.

Howard W. French is a columnist at Foreign Policy, a professor at the Columbia University Graduate School of Journalism, and a longtime foreign correspondent. His latest book is The Second Emancipation: Nkrumah, Pan-Africanism, and Global Blackness at High Tide. Bluesky: @hofrench.bsky.social X: @hofrench

#5870. Ilhan Omar records show judge, DOJ officials debating before raid of home

Newly unsealed search warrant documents reveal a decades-long federal investigation into Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., going back to 1980. Former federal prosecutor Jonathan Fahey joins 'Fox & Friends' to analyze the records and the Department of Justice's search for evidence of redacted crimes. Fahey discusses the legal implications of the unsealed warrant for her Washington, D.C. home.

Court documents released Monday night showed U.S. Department of Justice prosecutors battling with U.S. Magistrate Judge Matthew J. Sharbaugh to obtain a search warrant for the Washington, D.C., home of Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn.

The DOJ applied to the magistrate for a search warrant on Oct. 2, eventually receiving the warrant which led to a Homeland Security Investigations search of the home on Oct. 12.

However, the newly released petition for the warrant reveals the court raised concerns that the DOJ could be violating the Constitution’s Speech and Debate Clause.

FEDERAL AGENTS RAID ILHAN OMAR’S DC HOME AMID IMMIGRATION FRAUD INVESTIGATION

"The search warrant here is directed to a sitting member of the United States House of Representatives. This means that, even after ensuring that there exists probable cause to issue the warrant, see Fed. R. Crim. P. 41(d)(1), the Court must separately balance any necessary constitutional considerations under governing precedent," the court wrote in a memorandum.

Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., is seen on Capitol Hill in Washington D.C., on Tuesday for the first time since two federal investigations were opened against her, looking into her immigration status and financial filings. (Reuters/Tim Evans)

The DOJ, for its part, argued that the requested warrant did not relate to any speech Omar had made as a Congressperson or to her legislative duties at all.

'RADICAL LEFT LUNATIC' JUDGE TRUMP WANTED IMPEACHED TAKES UP ILHAN OMAR WARRANT FIGHT

The memorandum, according to the document, affirms a previous oral ruling from the court which questioned the government’s assertions that the search would be independent of Omar’s Congressional business.

"The issue that remains and the issue this Court independently raised with the government after its initial review of the warrant application is whether the search of residence implicates the Speech or Debate Clause. The government says no. This Court sees it differently. It concludes that the proposed search of the residence of a sitting Member of Congress, at least on the facts here, requires the balancing of interests that the D.C. Circuit explicated in Rayburn," the memorandum read.

ILHAN OMAR'S TURBULENT WEEK EXPLAINED: THE DUELING PROBES THAT THREW THE SOMALI-BORN PROGRESSIVE INTO CHAOS

The court specifically cited the warrant’s request to search Omar’s cell phone, arguing that most adults now bring some portion of work home with them, particularly on their electronic devices.

Federal agents with Homeland Security Investigations raided the Washington, D.C., home of Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., early Monday morning, a source familiar with the operation told Fox News Digital. (Andrew Murray/Fox News Digital)

"The Court has no trouble concluding that the presence of at least some legislative materials along those lines is 'inevitable' in the residence of a sitting member of Congress, whether in a formal home office setting, a work bag inside the home that travels back and forth to Capitol Hill, or otherwise," the memorandum read.

REP ILHAN OMAR'S IMMIGRATION HISTORY FACES NEW SCRUTINY AS FEDERAL INVESTIGATORS SUBPOENA RELATIVES

Despite the pushback, Sharbaugh ultimately granted the DOJ the search warrant but with the caveat that Omar could invoke Congressional privilege over any seized items that could relate to legislative activity and require prosecutors to obtain a separate warrant to search the content of electronic devices.

The documents also reveal that U.S. Attorney for the District of Columbia Jeanine Pirro is searching for documents related to any crimes going back to the 1980s.

OBAMA ADMIN FIRST LAUNCHED THE INVESTIGATION INTO ILHAN OMAR'S ALLEGED BROTHER MARRIAGE

Though parts of the document released Monday were heavily redacted, it revealed that prosecutors believed Omar committed "[redacted] crimes over decades."

According to Fox News legal analyst Jonathan Turley, the scope and time frame "confirms the broad sweep of the investigation back to her birth."

ILHAN OMAR HUDDLES WITH HER DC-BASED LAWYERS DURING HER FIRST DAY BACK ON CAPITOL HILL AMID FEDERAL PROBES

The documents were released by Judge James Boasberg, the chief judge for the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia, against DOJ wishes.

Boasberg denied a federal prosecutor's request to keep the records sealed.

WHAT'S NEXT FOR ILHAN OMAR? FORMER IMMIGRATION JUDGE BREAKS DOWN LEGAL RISKS AMID FEDERAL PROBES

James Boasberg, incoming chief judge of the U.S. District Court, in Washington, D.C., on Monday, March 13, 2023. (Valerie Plesch/Bloomberg via Getty)

The DOJ had previously argued in the documents that unsealing the records could prompt potential suspects into fleeing or witness tampering. Sharbaugh apparently agreed, signing off on the documents' sealing on Oct. 9.

However, Boasberg signed off on the unsealing and ordered the release of the heavily redacted 170-page documents. He also indicated that a further release could be coming in the future with significant reductions in redactions.

Boasberg excluded the 67-page search warrant affidavit, the search warrant itself, and a list of seized items from the release, citing an ongoing investigation.

Fox News Digital contacted the DOJ and Rep. Omar's office for further comment.

#5868. The Only Way to End the Netanyahu Era

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

AYMAN ODEH served in the Knesset from 2015 to 2026. He is the former leader of the Joint List and the Hadash party.

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

As the Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot struggles to assemble a governing coalition following last month’s elections, Israel finds itself in a familiar political predicament. The parties opposed to Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition command a clear majority in the Knesset, with 67 of its 120 seats. Yet the four Jewish-led parties in Eisenkot’s camp hold just 48 seats, leaving them unable to form a government on their own. The impasse echoes the prolonged political deadlock between 2019 and 2021, when Netanyahu’s supporters and his opponents remained locked in competing parliamentary blocs, and four consecutive elections failed to produce a stable governing majority.

But one thing is different this time. Israel’s Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Raam, are stronger than ever. Powered by extraordinary turnout among Arab citizens of Israel, they won a record 19 seats in the Knesset: 11 for the Joint List and eight for Raam. This political mobilization is important in itself, demonstrating Arab voters’ overwhelming desire for change and their recognition of the importance of participating in elections. But its significance goes far beyond the number of seats they have won. Together, the Arab parties now have the potential to be a decisive force in an anti-Netanyahu coalition, providing the parliamentary backing needed to form a government. The proof of this power has already been shown: on November 17, Arab support was crucial in the vote to replace the Likud speaker of the Knesset with a speaker from Eisenkot’s party. Now they have a historic opportunity to put the country on a different path.

As the first and former leader of the Joint List, I know how much is at stake. In 2019, I helped lead a groundbreaking effort to give Arab-led parties a direct voice in the Israeli political system. We took an enormous political risk by endorsing Benny Gantz, then a former chief of staff of the Israeli military and Netanyahu’s principal rival, as a candidate to form a government. We did so despite our profound political and ideological differences with him. Although that effort ultimately fell short, with Gantz choosing to pursue a “unity government” with Netanyahu instead, it established an important precedent: by showing a willingness to work with Jewish political forces with whom they had deep disagreements, parties representing the Arab public had a real chance to prevent a Netanyahu-led far-right government from holding power.

Today, after nearly four years of disastrous leadership by Netanyahu and his far-right allies, that possibility has become far more urgent. It will take political courage on both sides. Eisenkot must be able to offer something to the Arab-led parties, and the Arab-led parties will need to be comfortable offering support to a government that they may not agree with on many issues. But the remarkable Arab turnout and the sizable Israeli majority that is now against Netanyahu have pointed to a way forward. Not only Israel’s future but also its place in the region and the world may depend on the willingness of its leaders to engage with the country’s Palestinian minority. Ultimately, such a partnership will be essential to building the trust between Jews and Arabs that is a prerequisite to achieving any real progress toward a viable Israeli-Palestinian peace. This is the real choice now facing Israel: whether Israelis are prepared to replace the politics of supremacy and exclusion with a politics of equality.

The factors that drove record numbers of Palestinian citizens of Israel to the polls in October have been years in the making. Although the country’s Arab citizens make up more than 20 percent of the Israeli electorate and have long made vital contributions to Israeli society as doctors, lawyers, engineers, high-tech professionals, and in many other fields, the Israeli right has for decades demonized them as enemies: a fifth column and a threat to the state. In recent years, this hostility has increasingly manifested itself in harassment and open racism at universities and workplaces, in the media, in the parliament, and in everyday life.

Yet it would be a mistake to assume that Arab exclusion has been driven only by Netanyahu and the radical right. Zionist parties of the center and the left have also contributed, by keeping a distance from Arab voters or simply ignoring them. The belief that Jewish citizens should ultimately determine the country’s political direction while Arab citizens must remain within whatever limits have been set for them runs deep in Israeli politics. Netanyahu understood early on how to turn this contradiction into a political strategy. Since the 1990s, he has repeatedly used the same formula: inciting his supporters against Arab citizens, delegitimizing Arab representatives in parliament, and convincing Jewish Israelis that a government that relies on Arab political support would somehow be illegitimate.

These tactics proved to be among Netanyahu’s most effective political tools, especially given that Arab-led parties have long been some of his biggest foes. He didn’t need to convince everyone to support his Likud party or its various coalition partners. He only needed to convince enough people, including Jewish Israelis who opposed him, that working with Arab-led parties was dangerous and unacceptable. Netanyahu’s strategy also helped suppress Arab turnout. Since the Arab-led parties had little chance of changing the status quo, Arab voters had little incentive to vote: for many years, a significantly lower proportion of Arab citizens went to the polls than did their Jewish counterparts.

That began to change in 2015 with the creation of the Joint List, which for the first time brought together four parties representing the Arab public on a single electoral ticket and which became the third-largest faction in the Knesset. By embracing a broader vision for our society, grounded in Jewish-Arab solidarity and opposition to the racism of the right, the Joint List demonstrated that Palestinian citizens of Israel could play a new and more influential role in the country’s political system. Then, under my leadership in 2019, the Joint List decided to back Gantz’s bid to unseat Netanyahu. The decision was highly controversial, both within the Arab electorate and across Israeli society. Many Arab voters were deeply ambivalent about supporting a potential government led by a former army chief whose political positions, particularly on the status of Palestinian citizens of Israel and the occupation, differed sharply from their own. Nevertheless, we were prepared to take that political risk because we understood that defeating Netanyahu was more important.

In practice, our willingness to cooperate with the Zionist opposition was not, in fact, reciprocated. The Jewish opposition parties wanted our votes but could not bring themselves to accept us as equal political partners. And, ultimately, Gantz decided to form a government with Netanyahu instead. Nevertheless, the experience showed that Jewish-Arab political cooperation could open up new horizons for a large segment of the public. Since then, every time that Arab-led parties have recommended or supported the effort of a Jewish candidate to form a government, or whenever the Israeli center-left has depended on their support, additional segments of Israeli society have become more open to this kind of cooperation.

In 2021–22, an Arab party, Raam, joined the short-lived government led by Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid. I had serious reservations about the model of partnership that emerged, which seemed to rest on the expectation that Arab political representatives would limit their involvement to civilian and domestic matters, while refraining from addressing political and national questions, particularly the occupation and the Palestinian issue. I reject this distinction. We are part of the Palestinian people, and our identity and political concerns cannot be separated from our citizenship in Israel.

But these kinds of slow and painful steps are how political change often happens in national struggles. It does not always begin with a moral or democratic awakening. Sometimes, it begins with pragmatic necessity. When there is no other option, people begin to reconsider what had once been seen as impossible. In the current election, that dynamic has become even more important. After the unending crisis of the past four years, many Israeli voters, including both Arabs and Jews as well as the leaders of most of the Zionist opposition parties, have concluded that getting rid of the Netanyahu government is more necessary than ever.

In recent past elections, voter turnout among Palestinian citizens of Israel has generally remained between 50 and 60 percent. What explains the unprecedented 20-percentage-point surge in turnout in the October election? Three developments stand out: the war of annihilation in Gaza and the killing of tens of thousands of Palestinians; the daily racism and hostility that Arab citizens now face in the streets, in public spaces, and in virtually every aspect of their lives; and the crime that has spiraled out of control in Arab communities under the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, whose policies have allowed it to flourish, and the relentless settler violence against Palestinians in the West Bank enabled by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. All the grief and frustration accumulated over these years were brought to the ballot box.

But there was another crucial factor as well. In the run-up to the election, Arab citizens, following the nearly even polls between Eisenkot and Netanyahu, could see in black and white just how much the outcome would depend on them. Voting this time would be more than a political choice. It was for many a personal responsibility and a way of taking control of their own future. That is why hundreds of thousands of Palestinian citizens of Israel made their way to the polls, defying years of efforts to silence them and undermine their political agency. Many understood that the future direction of the country might be quite literally in their hands.

I saw elderly people, like my friend Jumah from Jisr al-Zarqa, taking to the streets and campaigning for the Joint List. Many of them had lived through the 1948 nakba, or “catastrophe,” which accompanied Israel’s establishment and involved terrible massacres, the destruction of hundreds of Palestinian villages and towns, and the displacement of around 750,000 Palestinians. I saw young people debating politics in the cafes and restaurants of my beloved Haifa, persuading their friends to vote. I saw women and men from Nazareth, Umm al-Fahm, Sakhnin, Rahat, Akko, Kafr Yasif, Majd al-Krum, and countless other towns and villages united by a shared sense of purpose. I saw political consciousness become a living, collective force. For a moment, the boundaries between generations and communities seemed to disappear.

The result marks a remarkable advance of the process of Arab participation in the Israeli political system that began in 2019. In this election, Arab citizens of Israel turned out in record numbers, dramatically narrowing the historical turnout gap with Jewish voters. And that has made another political reality increasingly clear: Israel’s liberal and centrist camp cannot form a government without the parties that represent the Arab public. If it excludes them, as well as the ultra-Orthodox and the far right, it will not reach the 61 seats needed. This is the demographic reality. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu must decide whom they are willing to build a political future with.

The debate about what Eisenkot should do and whether he should ask for Arab support is now unfolding beyond the Knesset, in people’s homes, cafés, in universities, and on the street. It is a deeply controversial question, and many Israelis disagree strenuously about it. Yet for the Arab-led parties, gaining sufficient importance to be able to provoke these conversations is an achievement. The fact that this debate is even taking place shows how the political process has caused Israelis to consider possibilities that until recently seemed unimaginable.

In the end, Eisenkot may have few other options if he wants to replace Netanyahu, as he has promised. In most cases, political parties support coalitions to advance positive changes and achieve concrete goals. But there are moments when political cooperation is necessary for a different reason: to prevent something far worse. The most immediate and overriding objective today is to bring an end to the far-right Netanyahu government. That is a priority on which both the Zionist opposition and the Arab-led parties can agree.

Beyond this shared basic goal, the Arab-led parties should use their political influence to work on two levels. First, they must seek tangible improvements in their constituents’ lives: combating organized crime in Arab communities, securing budgets, addressing the housing crisis, ending the brutal treatment of Palestinians living under occupation in the West Bank, and pushing for a permanent cease-fire and the reconstruction of Gaza. These are fundamental issues of human dignity and civilian life. On a broader level, they should continue to push for real progress on Israeli-Palestinian peace that is ultimately based on two states coexisting, side by side.

I do not expect the current Israeli political establishment to embrace these positions, but that does not mean that the Arab parties should not raise them at every opportunity. At the same time, the Arab parties can use their newfound political clout to build alliances between Jews and Arabs around shared political and strategic goals. The resulting partnerships must be about more than parliamentary arrangements; it must also create the foundations for a different future. That will require Jewish and Arab citizens of Israel to engage with one another on the basis of equality, rather than mere political convenience.

Thus far, Eisenkot has given few indications that he is prepared to go down this path. Since the election, it has been encouraging to see him condemn Netanyahu for fueling what he described as a “racist and violent” campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens. Yet many of the mainstream Zionist parties in Eisenkot’s camp have vowed not to work with the Arab parties. Still, Eisenkot must consider what that would mean. Thirty years from now, people will ask how Jewish supremacy became so normalized that even politicians who called themselves democrats accepted the exclusion of one-fifth of their fellow citizens. They will ask why Israel’s opposition could recognize the danger posed by Netanyahu’s government but not confront the assumptions that made his politics possible.

There is an irony in the fact that, in a country that seeks to keep political hegemony exclusively in Jewish hands, the Palestinian minority, after decades of struggle for political recognition and equal rights, has emerged as one of the most significant forces shaping the country’s political future. We have come to understand that we must be courageous and cooperate with the Zionist parties of the center and left in order to oust Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir, and Smotrich. The question now is: Do these parties, which call themselves democratic and liberal, have the same courage?

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

Conflicts in the Middle East and the Horn of Africa Have Become Inseparable

Michael Robbins, Amaney A. Jamal, and Mark Tessler

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5867. Draft plan to overhaul Smithsonian spotted on White House staffer's laptop: report

Sen. Josh Hawley, R-Mo., discusses controversies surrounding the Smithsonian Institution and President Donald Trump calling on the museum to replace a sculpture with a statue of George Washington on ‘The Ingraham Angle.’

Photos taken by a commuter on Washington, D.C.'s Metro system appear to show a White House advisor’s laptop displaying a draft plan to overhaul the Smithsonian’s National Museum of American History, ProPublica reported.

Last month, a commuter riding the Orange Line from Northern Virginia noticed a man in a suit working on a laptop displaying a document titled "Smithsonian Institution Renewal Plan." The commuter photographed the screen and sent the images to ProPublica.

The multipoint draft proposed firing museum Director Anthea Hartig, creating new museum-wide exhibit guidelines that omit the Smithsonian’s diversity style guide, reviewing its traveling-exhibition strategy — including ending "The Bias Inside Us" — and reassessing grants to what the draft called "woke third parties," including the American Alliance of Museums. A separate handwritten note that was also pictured proposed a policy against "X-rated" exhibitions. ProPublica said it likely referred to an exhibit the White House criticized for displaying a two-piece chrome-and-rubber harness designed for sadomasochism.

INSIDE THE BEHEMOTH SMITHSONIAN MUSEUM NETWORK DRAWING FIRE FROM THE TRUMP ADMINISTRATION

The Smithsonian's National Museum of American History in Washington, D.C., on Oct. 12, 2025. Photos taken on a D.C. Metro train appear to show a White House advisor's laptop displaying a draft plan to overhaul the museum's leadership, exhibits and K-12 curriculum. (Stefani Reynolds/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

The draft renewal plan pictured on the staffer's laptop surfaced amid a broader Trump administration effort to reshape how the Smithsonian presents American history. The White House released a 162-page report on July 4 accusing the National Museum of American History of "ideological capture." Two congressional hearings followed, and a July executive order directed officials to post signs outside the museum informing visitors of the report’s findings about "ideological capture," though ProPublica reported the signs had not yet gone up as of publication.

Fox News Digital reached out to the White House for comment on the photographed document and whether any of its proposals are under consideration, but did not receive a response by publication. The photographed document does not establish whether the proposals were approved or put into effect.

TRUMP GIVES SMITHSONIAN DAYS TO REPLACE 'STRANGE MODERNIST SCULPTURE' WITH FOUNDING FATHER

ProPublica reported that it identified the White House staffer as Sam Bock, a 27-year-old associate policy advisor with the White House Domestic Policy Council — which produced the July report — after spotting the initials "B.S." or "S.B." on the laptop screen and following a man matching his description from the Metro to a White House employee checkpoint. ProPublica reported that Bock did not respond to its messages and that the Smithsonian declined to comment.

ProPublica reported that the proposals on Bock’s laptop envisioned a permanent exhibit celebrating Western civilization, a display on prominent American families, a large George Washington statue at the museum entrance and new museum-wide interpretive guidelines written without reference to the Smithsonian’s diversity style guide. A separate handwritten note photographed alongside the laptop also floated a potential new D-Day exhibit.

The Smithsonian's National Museum of American History in Washington, D.C.. A July White House report accused the museum of "ideological capture," and a draft renewal plan photographed on a White House advisor's laptop proposed replacing its diversity guidelines and adding a George Washington statue at the entrance. (Kevin Carter)

In addition to the proposed exhibit changes, the draft called for the firing of National Museum of American History Director Anthea Hartig and about a dozen senior administrators and curators. That proposal came as Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie Bunch, who leads the broader institution, was preparing to retire at year’s end. ProPublica reported that White House criticism of the Smithsonian helped prompt Bunch’s departure.

TRUMP V. SMITHSONIAN: A TIMELINE OF HOW THE WHITE HOUSE FIGHT OVER 'WOKE' HISTORY UNFOLDED

The draft plan also called for overhauling the museum’s online curriculum for K-12 students, including dropping a module on immigration and creating new material focused on the Founders, according to ProPublica, which noted the document floated possible titles for the new curriculum, including "Architects of Liberty" and "Profiles in Liberty."

Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie Bunch poses for a portrait at the Smithsonian Castle on the National Mall in Washington, D.C., on May 28, 2026. Bunch is preparing to retire at the end of the year amid White House criticism of the institution, according to ProPublica. (Al Drago/The Washington Post via Getty Images)

The administration’s push to reshape how the Smithsonian presents American history began picking up steam in March 2025, when Trump signed an executive order directing Vice President JD Vance, through his role on the Smithsonian’s Board of Regents, to help remove what the order called "improper ideology" from the institution. The Smithsonian, which encompasses 21 museums and D.C.'s National Zoo, is governed by a 17-member Board of Regents that consists of the vice president, the chief justice of the Supreme Court, members of Congress and private citizens. It receives more than $1 billion in federal funding annually.

The White House’s July report cited, among other examples, a museum exhibit that raised the possibility Benjamin Franklin had used enslaved people in his household as subjects in electrical experiments. It also criticized staff training based on a social-action toolkit that the report said linked museums to White supremacy and other forms of oppression.

The report said the museum should not erase slavery, Jim Crow or other national failures. Its broader charge was that the museum was not giving visitors a coherent account of how the country developed, including both its achievements and its wrongs.

The criticism from the Trump administration came amid a longer-running debate over how the museum should tell the nation’s story, which has stretched back to at least 2001.

ProPublica reported that in response to earlier criticism, the Smithsonian convened a blue-ribbon commission in 2001, whose 2002 report urged the museum to present a more unified account of American history. The outlet also reported that after George Floyd’s murder in 2020, Bunch and other leaders began an institution-wide reassessment of the Smithsonian’s priorities and how it tells the nation’s story.

Alec Schemmel is a Politics Reporter for Fox News Digital who is originally from Charlotte, North Carolina but now resides in the Washington D.C.-Baltimore Metro Area.
