#5865. Spinning Genocide

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: Spinning Genocide

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Over the last few years, some of the most vitriolic debates over Gaza have been about language: the meaning of “intifada,” the implications of “from the river to the sea,” and the importance of using the term “genocide.” Many people criticized protestors for what they believed were extreme or dangerous slogans. Many protestors, in turn, were furious about the shocking chasm between the horrifyingly violent reality they were seeing on social media and the understated, deadening, or deceitful language that political officials and mainstream media were using to describe it.

Two new books offer the opportunity to look back at that surreal period with more certainty. The journalist Akbar Shahid Ahmed’s Crossing the Red Line is the first reported account of what actually took place inside President Joe Biden’s administration during Israel’s war in Gaza, revealing the truth behind its often deceptive public statements. Assal Rad’s Don’t Say Palestine offers a similar dissection of how the media buttressed the government rather than challenged it, clinging to outdated journalistic notions and biases when the horror unfolding before the world demanded something far different.

Over the last few years, some of the most vitriolic debates over Gaza have been about language: the meaning of “intifada,” the implications of “from the river to the sea,” and the importance of using the term “genocide.” Many people criticized protestors for what they believed were extreme or dangerous slogans. Many protestors, in turn, were furious about the shocking chasm between the horrifyingly violent reality they were seeing on social media and the understated, deadening, or deceitful language that political officials and mainstream media were using to describe it.

Two new books offer the opportunity to look back at that surreal period with more certainty. The journalist Akbar Shahid Ahmed’s Crossing the Red Line is the first reported account of what actually took place inside President Joe Biden’s administration during Israel’s war in Gaza, revealing the truth behind its often deceptive public statements. Assal Rad’s Don’t Say Palestine offers a similar dissection of how the media buttressed the government rather than challenged it, clinging to outdated journalistic notions and biases when the horror unfolding before the world demanded something far different.

Rad, a scholar of Middle Eastern history, begins her book before Oct. 7, 2023. In 2021, she saw a New York Times headline so euphemistic as to be mendacious: “Evictions in Jerusalem Become Focus of Israeli-Palestinian Conflict.” Evictions? “As a historian of the region,” Rad writes, “I was not surprised to see the way the media reflected the official account of their country’s foreign policy positions,” but that didn’t make it less infuriating. Rad posted her marked-up version on social media. It now said, “Forced expulsions in Jerusalem Become Focus of Palestinian Struggle.”

Don’t Say Palestine: How the Media Manufactured Consent for Genocide, Assal Rad, Vintage, 256 pp., $20, September 2026

To Rad, the word “evictions” made ethnic cleansing sound like a real estate dispute or a municipal procedure, and left out the fact that East Jerusalem, where the events were taking place, “is recognized under international law as occupied Palestinian territory.” She knew that this controversy, which was over the Jerusalem neighborhood of Sheikh Jarrah, was representative of the central storyline in the last 150 years of Palestine’s history—a native people dispossessed of their land—and that without the historical context, or the appropriate framing and language, the present-day event made little sense.

The 2021 riots that ensued when a Jewish settler group claimed these historical Palestinian homes as their own eventually developed into an 11-day war in Gaza. In retrospect, members of the Biden administration should have noticed how the U.S. public responded to this war, in which the Israeli army killed over 250 Palestinians. The officials might have seen that young Americans fresh from the Black Lives Matter movement saw parallels to the Palestinians’ plight, and that global anger at Israel’s brand of asymmetrical violence was growing.

Instead, Biden and team forged ahead, blindly supporting Israel after the Hamas attack on Oct. 7, 2023, with stunningly little awareness of how the American people and the whole world would react. Their obliviousness may have stemmed from a variety of factors: isolation in the Washington bubble, the influence of donors, the belief that Israel was a forever-ally, or a deeper racial bias. What Rad suggests in her clinical, clear-eyed book is that the media played a role in their ignorance as well by parroting the euphemistic, contextless, and factually incorrect language of the U.S. and Israeli governments.

In the aftermath of Oct. 7, Rad began to rewrite headlines more regularly, particularly targeting major outlets such as the New York Times, the BBC, Reuters, the Guardian, and the Associated Press. She makes a persuasive case for why headlines are important, even more so in the age of social media and the internet. “Headlines matter because people often do not read beyond them,” she writes. Their passive consumption becomes the everyday wallpaper of readers’ realities.

A panel of New York Times headlines annotated by Assal Rad. Assal Rad screenshots via Zeteo

Many readers will be familiar with Rad’s concerns. Critics of the New York Times, for example, have long highlighted the newspaper’s usage of passive voice. Headlines like “Strike on Area Where Displaced Gazans Were Camped Kills Up to 25,” or “Lives Ended in Gaza” also studiously avoid naming the perpetrator. After the shocking killing of the 5-year-old child Hind Rajab—who called for help after the Israeli army fired onto her family’s vehicle, initially killing everyone besides her before fatally shooting her and the emergency workers who came to rescue her—major media outlets such as the BBC, CNN, and the New York Times failed to mention Israel or its army in their headlines. As Rad notes throughout the book, similar headlines about atrocities in Ukraine typically name Russia or Putin. Beyond this, Rad points to the use of chillingly anodyne language that is borrowed from the Israeli or U.S. militaries: “targeted strikes” for “indiscriminate bombings,” or “a humanitarian challenge” for what is in reality “a policy of starvation.”

In the case of Israel’s wars in the Middle East, Rad exposes how the manipulation of the legal and moral term “self-defense” has come to justify more and more violence. The U.S. and Israeli governments have done this before. What’s been different in recent years, however, is that the reality of Israeli actions  has suddenly become ubiquitous and visible. “For the first time,” Rad writes, “millions around the world watched a genocide unfold live on their phones, uncensored, because of the bravery of Palestinian journalists and the reach of social media.” What Israel was doing—blowing up hospitals, collapsing universities, starving already-food-deprived populations, dropping 2,000-pound bombs on cities, men, women, and children—certainly didn’t look like self-defense.

Yet even when a hospital or school was bombed, the U.S. media often hedged along with the Biden government. It claimed that events still need to be investigated, leaving what people saw with their own eyes to hover in factual limbo. Worse, it would repeat officials’ assertions that, say, bombing a hospital and killing hundreds of people might have been legal because the Israeli army was “targeting Hamas,” a widely repeated phrase that came to be imbued with as much exonerating power as “self-defense.”

Some of these decisions are not easy. Newspapers have a responsibility to break news quickly but never make a single factual error. Israel barred Western journalists from entering Gaza, so in the early weeks of the war newspapers might have been even more cautious before issuing blame for bombings. But ultimately, this led to them to give extraordinary weight to the testimonies of the Israeli army in assessing such ambiguities. Beyond individual incidents, Rad argues that Israel’s war on Gaza should have been framed much earlier as a deliberate campaign of annihilation or genocide, not a war between two equal parties. The laws of war might allow for some morally abhorrent actions by all sides, but those actions look different when they are mere aspects of what was (and remains) a wildly disproportionate campaign.

A photo taken during a controlled tour and subsequently edited under the supervision of the Israeli military shows Israeli soldiers patrolling a road in Rafah in the Gaza Strip on Sept. 13, 2024.Sharon Aronowicz/AFP via Getty Images

Indeed, it is worth asking why the mainstream media believed anything Israel’s radical, far-right, wholly corrupt government or military said, whether about specific bombings or larger issues, like the claim it was solely trying to “defeat Hamas.” The tendency to simply relay the arguments of public officials is one of the biggest pitfalls of supposedly objective journalism, especially when it comes to governments that have proven to be autocratic or repressive. I was surprised last year, for example, to see a New York Times headline that said, “Erdogan Calls It an Anticorruption Drive. His Rivals Call It a Political Crackdown.” Erdogan has been a repressive authoritarian leader since 2013; what is the point of putting his propaganda in the headline?

In Don’t Say Palestine, Rad repeatedly highlights these either/or question headlines. One reason for their popularity may be a boring one: They drive reader engagement. In the case of Gaza, however, it’s possible that the media’s noncommittal headlines betray a deeper, more desperate wish.

As Rad and Ahmed show, Biden’s foreign policy team also seemed desperate to believe in a fantasy version of Israel. Despite the country’s long history of violence against Palestinians, and despite the repeated genocidal statements from high-level Israeli officials, Biden and his team continued to give Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and the Israeli army the benefit of the doubt. For Biden and those around him, Israel remained inherently good, a delusion that might have come naturally to Americans because they believe it about themselves.

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu hugs U.S. President Joe Biden upon his arrival at Tel Aviv’s Ben Gurion Airport in Israel on Oct. 18, 2023.Brendan Smiallowski/AFP via Getty Images

Ahmed, one of the few reporters to consistently break news and pursue stories about Biden’s foreign policy advisors, offers a painstaking, chronological account of the administration’s gross failures in Crossing the Red Line. One of the most important throughlines of Ahmed’s book is how the Biden foreign policy team subverted the very traditional processes of U.S. government, all to maintain their secretive stranglehold on this conflict.

Crossing the Red Line: Biden, His Advisors, and Israel’s War in Gaza, Akbar Shahid Ahmed, W.W. Norton & Co., 368 pp., $31.99, September 2026

According to Ahmed, Biden’s team demonstrated a “disdain” for their fellow Democrats and government colleagues, sometimes acting like intelligence officers running a covert war in Latin America while swatting away annoying moral and legal restrictions. “Twice in December [2023], Blinken invoked emergency powers to ignore the standard procedures for U.S. arms deals—which involved consultation with Congress,” Ahmed writes, “so that Washington could rush equipment to the Israelis.” Uzra Zeya, who oversaw human rights work at the State Department, discouraged “split memos” in which some officials could oppose a policy that others had endorsed because those memos would put Blinken on record for whatever he decided. The resulting “consensus memos” instead created what another official called “herd immunity,” in which no one could be blamed for the policy.

Members of the Biden foreign policy team had been to law school and they knew how to twist legal logic. “For [Jake] Sullivan to claim that lack of proven intent to violate international law precluded Israel’s having committed war crimes,” Ahmed writes, “suggested that the administration could never acknowledge that that occurred.” Those violations were most visible and obvious when it came to military strikes on sites protected by international law, like medical facilities, schools, and houses of worship—all places that Israel would bomb and destroy over time. During the first bombing of Gaza’s Al-Ahli Hospital, “Biden not only declared Israel’s innocence immediately,” Rad notes, “he defined a norm in which Israel could not be guilty because its moral superiority made it incapable of such breaches … The Al-Ahli hospital episode became the model for how the Biden administration would approach the Gaza Genocide. Israel would be taken at its word, exonerated from guilt, and allowed to act with total impunity, with the backing and corroboration of the United States to provide credibility for its actions at every turn.”

Impunity for abetting war crimes weakens U.S. democracy.

A new book details how the country slayed Washington’s foes.

The conduct of Israel’s bombing campaign was a key element in the International Criminal Court’s case against Prime Minister Netanyahu and former Defense Minister Yoav Gallant. Another was starvation. Using starvation as a method of warfare is a war crime; Human Rights Watch declared that Israel was doing this as early as December 2023. Yet Biden officials publicly doubted humanitarian and intelligence officials, rejecting assessments that would have been taken “as gospel” in other times. When representatives from aid groups met with White House Middle East coordinator Brett McGurk, Ahmed writes, “he surprised them by denying that conditions could worsen.” In a much-talked-about passage, Ahmed writes that both McGurk and Sullivan told others they didn’t think Palestinians were actually starving or looked skinny—something both men deny.

But the reason this allegation isn’t hard to believe in the context of Ahmed’s book is because an ongoing war with bombing campaigns of stunningly high frequency requires humans to constantly make all kinds of macabre logistical choices and decisions. As long time Middle East hand Aaron David Miller told the New Yorker in 2024, Biden did not have the “same depth of feeling and empathy for the Palestinians of Gaza” as he did for Israel. Indeed, the Jerusalem Post revealed this month that during Biden’s last phone call with Netanyahu before leaving the White House, Biden said, “You’re a good man … I admire you. It took a lot of courage to do what you did.”

Hidaya, a 31-year-old Palestinian mother, carries her sick and malnourished 18-month-old son Mohammed al-Mutawaq inside their tent at the Al-Shati refugee camp, west of Gaza City, on July 24, 2025.Omar Al-Qattaa/AFP via Getty Images

The idea that Israel was actively trying to starve Gaza was softened by much of the media too, despite Israeli officials’ open declarations of this intent. Rad notes that in the Economist, a story on the threat of famine in Gaza didn’t even mention Israel. In headlines, the New York Times asked, “Why Isn’t More Aid Getting to Gazans?” and, “What is a Famine and Who Determines that One Exists?” in March and June 2024, respectively. (Israel has long controlled all food that gets into Gaza as part of its decades-long occupation and blockade.) When Israel’s defense minister Israel Katz stated explicitly that no aid would be allowed into Gaza, major outlets like Reuters posted headlines that uncritically echoed Israel’s official statements like: “Israeli Defense Minister Says No Humanitarian Aid to Enter Gaza as Tool of Pressure on Hamas.” As Rad contends, this effectively legitimized the use of hunger as a weapon of war. One BBC article said that over the last two years, Palestinians’ access to food had been “complicated.” “The same article repeatedly stated Israeli denials of any wrongdoing, including the government’s rejection of the [Integrated Food Security Phase Classification] report”—the global gold standard of food security assessments. According to the BBC, Israel denied that Israeli soldiers had shot and killed aid seekers, and Netanyahu denied that starvation was taking place at all.

With the normal channels blocked, the Biden administration went to embarrassing lengths to get food to Palestinians, such as building a pier in the sea and airdropping food packages, all to avoid doing the obvious, which was forcing the Israelis to allow food into the strip. The Americans also clung to and broadcast the idea that a ceasefire represented the only way to end Gaza’s suffering. No matter how many times Netanyahu rejected ceasefire terms or petulantly sabotaged them, and even after Israeli press reported Netanyahu’s obvious self-interest in prolonging the war, Biden and his team insisted it was the only way out of the unfolding disaster.

Starting in April 2024, to avoid the ceasefire he never wanted, Netanyahu began targeting people in other countries. First, there was the bombing of the Iranian consulate in Damascus; then, in July, there was the assassination of Ismail Haniyeh inside Iran; and in September, there was the Israeli pager attack on Hezbollah operatives (as well as innocent bystanders) as they went about their daily lives. In rapid succession, the Israelis then killed Hassan Nasrallah and unleashed an astounding bombing campaign on the city of Beirut, as well as a ground invasion into Southern Lebanon. Today, that region has been razed to the ground. Whole villages have been destroyed, 4,300 people have been killed, and more than 1.2 million displaced.

At every horrific turn in Crossing the Red Line, Ahmed stops to remind us that the same day, week, or month that another horror or humiliation occurred, the Biden administration would decide to send more weapons to Israel anyway. “Regardless of the gut-wrenching cost and their mutual disdain,” Ahmed writes, “Netanyahu and Biden might still take credit for a new Middle East in which Israel held more sway.”

Palestinians bury relatives killed in overnight Israeli strikes on the Fatima Bint Asad school in Jabalia, at a cemetery in the northern Gaza Strip on May 12, 2025. Bashar Taleb/AFP via Getty Images

Ahmed argues that careerism and groupthink fueled a lot of the behavior of the Biden team. But his reporting also suggests that strengthening Israel—ensuring that it wins everything—was a goal in itself. For decades, U.S. policy was for Israel to be the strongest country in the Middle East, and in 2025, a U.S. government at last achieved that goal. Israel became the region’s undeniable military heavyweight.

Thanks to works like Rad’s and Ahmed’s, the human toll of this policy has become clear to most Americans. Increasingly, newspaper headlines have reflected this reality, too. Here’s a recent one from the New York Times: “Israel Has Committed Genocidal Acts in Gaza Since Cease-Fire, U.N. Human Rights Panel Says.” For her part, Rad does not deny that many journalists who have written and reported on the war have done excellent work. The value of her critique is more about these Western publications as a whole – how they often fail to fulfill their most vital professional purpose:  holding the powerful to account.

For the Biden team, the failure of accountability has continued even after leaving office. Confronted with evidence of their culpability in this nightmare, former officials now use opaque and passive language, as if the people who ran the most powerful country in the world simply had no control of events, no control over Netanyahu and no control over President Biden, that sad prisoner of a 1970s sepia-toned fever dream about Golda Meir. As a result, they end up implying that they were merely shirking their moral responsibility to please their aging leader, as if they were functionaries in an authoritarian regime.

It’s possible—in the sense that anything is possible—that these officials were ignorant of what kind of country Israel had become in the last 20 years. Ignorance of cultures and history, after all, was a major engine of the Americans’ failed wars in Iraq and Afghanistan. Moments in Ahmed’s book manage to surprise even him, such as when veteran Middle East diplomat Barbara Leaf cries, “Come on, the IDF doesn’t participate in settler violence!” Perhaps Biden’s team were also victims of decades of misleading or understated media headlines and that was why they continued to give Israeli leaders the benefit of the doubt—or take them at their word. But there is another term for consistently giving the benefit of the doubt to people who share your skin color over people who don’t. In Crossing the Red Line, Ahmed argues that the war on terror conditioned most “U.S. foreign policy professionals” to be comfortable “with jaw-dropping atrocities and dehumanizing of people in the region.” Rad goes back further, to the entire history of Palestine. “The horrors that have been normalized in Gaza,” she writes, “are the culmination of this long history of racism and injustice.”

Today, Republicans, some Democrats, and probably much of the U.S. security state is still responsible for supporting Netanyahu’s government as it works to destroy Gaza. It was Americans’ own corrupted consensus and Israeli’s murderous one that came together to wreck the international legal order, all while Western media organs chronicled the downfall of that order in careful, hedging tones. One of the most accurate headlines about Gaza, Rad writes, came from the satirical newspaper the Onion following the October 2025 ceasefire announcement: “Israel Agrees to Go Back to Killing Palestinians on Less Frequent Basis.” The advantage of fact-based, active-voiced headlines is that they don’t only tell what’s happening in the present. They tell what will still be happening in the future.

Books are independently selected by FP editors. FP earns an affiliate commission on anything purchased through links to Amazon.com on this page.

#5863. Avoiding Colombia’s Escalation Trap

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ELIZABETH DICKINSON is Deputy Director for Latin America at International Crisis Group.

How a U.S.-Backed Offensive Could Help Bogotá Reclaim Its Conflict Zones

On September 21, José Luis Pérez Villanueva, better known as “El Cholo”—the second-in-command and financial architect of the criminal group Autodefensas Conquistadores de la Sierra Nevada—was killed during a Colombian police operation on the rural outskirts of Santa Marta. Within hours, militants from that organization launched a dramatic campaign of terror. The group, known colloquially as Los Pachenca, demanded that businesses across the city shutter. Some 90 percent of shops did, as videos circulated of gunmen shooting up storefronts that stayed open.

For two decades, Los Pachenca has been the unquestioned criminal authority in the beachfront city and the adjacent Sierra Nevada mountain range, charging extortion payments from businesses and residents and controlling the flow of drugs out of the Port of Santa Marta, en route to Europe and beyond. But the level of control the group exhibited during the strike was particularly jarring, given that Santa Marta is the vacation home of many of the country’s elite and a common destination for international visitors. Colombia’s government responded by militarizing the city, sending in hundreds of soldiers and police officers, who rolled into town on the main highway with tanks and air support. Many shops still stayed closed until the morning of September 25, when the group announced that it was ending the strike. For locals, Los Pachenca’s reaction was a message about who remains in charge.

Calm has since returned to Santa Marta, but the incident may be indicative of risks to come. Colombia’s newly elected president, Abelardo de la Espriella, has promised a steep military escalation against illegal groups, which have grown precipitously in recent years. As he does so, armed and criminal organizations will undoubtedly respond. Often, their target will not be the state. Instead, they will likely direct their violence against the civilian populations, whom they have spent years coopting and coercing. That is because armed and criminal groups across the country have learned that their true leverage is their ability to govern local communities—and to intimidate and punish them into silence. Even when they do attack state forces, their use of weapons such as improvised explosives and makeshift drones means that bystanders often suffer. In one recent incident in the Pacific coast department of Valle del Cauca, four drone attacks launched by an armed group in a single day left five civilians injured.

These dangers are particularly acute as de la Espriella’s pledge of a zero-tolerance crackdown against armed and criminal groups has won enthusiastic support from Washington. Both countries want big and visible victories against these outfits. They triumphantly announced a new era of cooperation when the president took office in August, including a promise of upgraded military ties. (The United States has not yet publicly pledged new weapons, but it is already providing greater intelligence and logistical assistance on some operations.) After two years of tensions and threats between President Donald Trump and leftist former President Gustavo Petro, the political realignment means that Colombia is once again the United States’ closest defense ally in South America. This is likely to continue, despite major Democratic wins in the U.S. Congress, as the relationship’s focus on security has a long history of bipartisan support.

This week, during U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s visit to Bogotá, the de la Espriella administration signaled that it would seek congressional approval for joint military operations with the United States in Colombia. This cooperation will likely yield initial results. The Colombian government has said that it aims to ramp up attacks against armed and criminal groups and to go after leaders who enjoyed protection as counterparts in peace talks during the past administration.

Yet Colombia’s true challenge is not how to carry out attacks but what to do afterward. Surgical strikes, which see the military enter and exit quickly, leave civilians more vulnerable in the aftermath, as armed groups retaliate against alleged informants and tighten their grip in areas they control. Bogotá must ensure that every operation includes contingencies to anticipate criminal groups’ reactions and build on successful offensives by weakening their grasp on communities. The government will need to strengthen the police’s ability to patrol contested areas, a costly but necessary process that the United States and other partners should support.

The stakes are high. Bogotá has a chance to leverage its increased military pressure to open the door for the state, rewriting the story of who governs conflict areas. If the government misses this opportunity to consolidate state control, however, Colombia risks entering an escalatory spiral in which civilians pay a mounting price for a campaign intended to protect them.

Armed and criminal groups have spent the last decade expanding their reach across Colombia. According to the local think tank Pares, the number of municipalities with an armed group presence rose from 212 in 2018 to 518 last year—or about half of all municipalities. This deterioration reversed dramatic and hard-won improvements in security. After the historically violent decades of the 1990s and 2000s, military pressure and negotiations yielded a 2016 peace agreement demobilizing the former Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC), once the country’s dominant rebel group. Colombia’s countryside breathed a sigh of relief as the FARC pulled out of areas where it had been in control, which represented a quarter of the country’s land.

The FARC’s exit, however, did not pave the way for Colombia’s highly centralized and bureaucratic state to claim control of those vast geographies. Instead, old armed groups such as the National Liberation Army and newer ones such as the Gaitanista Army of Colombia (also known as the Gulf Clan) saw a chance to expand their access to trafficking routes, coca crops, illegal mines, and, ultimately, vulnerable rural populations. Kidnapping for extortion and mass displacement began to resurface, shattering optimism that progress would move in one direction. Over the last four years, membership in illegal groups has nearly doubled, to some 27,000 fighters today.

As a candidate, de la Espriella successfully harnessed public frustration by promising mano dura, or iron-fisted tactics, including aerial attacks and increased incarceration, facilitated by the construction of mega-prisons. He blamed his predecessor’s administration for accelerating insecurity. The first leftist to be elected president in modern Colombia, Petro had entered office in 2022 vowing to de-emphasize the military’s role and instead hold peace talks with various armed organizations. But not every group participated, and many of the ones that did sought to take advantage of cease-fires to cement their control over communities. Midway through his term, Petro pivoted back toward deploying the armed forces. But much of the harm was already done.

Colombia’s new president has been in office only a little more than 100 days, but the shift is marked. De la Espriella carried out three bombing raids in his first weeks. He has extradited dozens of individuals to the United States, including senior leaders, and the administration has killed or captured many others. The president is doggedly pushing to restart aerial fumigation of coca crops, which the state carried out in the early 2000s to try to reduce the number of hectares under cultivation; the practice has since been heavily restricted by court rulings over concerns about health and environmental consequences. Both in private and in public, military leaders laud the government’s new support for their operations.

The Trump administration has also welcomed these developments, which align with a regional trend favoring Washington’s approach on counternarcotics. For Colombia, the new era of security cooperation with the United States represents a return to the status quo ante of the last quarter century, which was briefly disrupted by the Petro administration. U.S.-Colombian military ties were forged through antidrug efforts and deepened in 1999 with Plan Colombia, a decadelong $10 billion aid package that funded, among other things, the expansion and professionalization of the security forces and fumigation. The Colombian police and military are deeply integrated with their American counterparts. They use U.S.- and NATO-compatible systems, travel by the hundreds to the United States each year for instruction, and receive American intelligence. In nearly every operation, at least one step (and often far more) in the logistical chain is either coordinated with Washington or relies on U.S. support. That relationship never fully stopped under Petro, but the two sides were notably wary of each other. Now bilateral trust appears to be restored.

Even before the elections, de la Espriella impressed U.S. officials by outlining his plans to reclaim security momentum for the state. The Colombian president is now acting on several explicit U.S. priorities, including extraditions and legislation to designate numerous groups as terrorist organizations. Meanwhile, Bogotá’s coca eradication targets are even more ambitious than Washington’s. At a personal level, relations are strong, too. Two of Colombia’s top military officials previously served as envoys to U.S. Southern Command.

Bogotá says that it intends to deliver results for Washington’s antidrug goals and has explicitly asked for additional support in the form of funding, aircraft, surveillance technologies, and maintenance. Although Colombia’s military remains among the best trained in the region, its logistical needs are critical. Only 13 of Colombia’s 53 Black Hawk helicopters are in working order, and defense spending has shrunk as a share of the national government’s budget, from 22.4 percent in 2003 to just 14.7 percent in 2025, with a growing proportion of those funds spent on salaries and pensions.

How much the United States will provide remains to be seen. In August, U.S. Southern Command’s General Francis Donovan discussed ways to shore up the deteriorating air fleet during a visit to Bogotá. The United States has also redeployed surveillance drones from Africa to Colombia and Ecuador, where they are already aiding intelligence collection. But Secretary of State Marco Rubio was reluctant to pledge specific aid while in Colombia in September, and most of this assistance is likely to be in kind or to stay in the hands of the U.S. military and contractors, with little entering the Colombian budget. Much of it appears to be focused on short-term offensive wins.

Colombia has returned to tactics such as aerial bombardment because they arguably worked against the FARC. Founded as a Marxist rebellion in the 1960s, the group behaved like a conventional army, dressed in uniforms, and sought out combat with the state. This meant that the Colombian military could bomb encampments and that capturing leaders left the organization at times rudderless. Slowly chipping away at the FARC’s hierarchy and control helped push the group to finally negotiate its disarmament, beginning in 2012.

But today, those same strategies are yielding diminishing returns. The current generation of armed and criminal groups has little interest in confronting the state. Instead, they behave like businesses, seeking to safeguard access to natural resources, supply chains, and trafficking routes. They live within the civilian population and rarely wear uniforms. Leaders are easily replaced and hierarchies are fluid. Critically, Colombia’s armed groups have learned that wars with the military are costly but that controlling local populations—through intimidation and violence, but also by offering economic incentives, jobs, and services—is comparatively cheap. The battlefield has evolved from a conventional conflict to a contest over who can govern territories and the people living there.

Santa Marta offers an example of how this new model of warfare works. On most days, an outsider in the city would never perceive the presence of Los Pachenca. The group’s members do not patrol or identify themselves. Yet residents know them well from the weekly or monthly extortion payments they are forced to make. Local companies and government officials essentially view the group as an inescapable reality. Pushing back carries a violent price, but paying a protection tax, taking a bribe, or facilitating illegal commerce keeps business running smoothly.

The criminal governance of rural areas is even more suffocating. Groups that emerged from splinters of the former FARC, known as dissidents, often force local residents to hold identity cards, issued so that militants have a full census of who lives in the area and can detect possible enemy fighters. In the Amazon region, these groups keep a land registry more complete than that of the state so that they know exactly whom and how much to extort.

With this intense surveillance over society, armed and criminal groups frequently deploy the civilian population as human shields against military offensives and police patrols. The most egregious example is the widespread use of forcibly recruited children as bodyguards for senior commanders. De la Espriella’s initial bombardments, which killed at least six children, were even temporarily halted by a court over concerns about minors. Senior officers admit that these types of casualties are inevitable around high-value targets, and that the groups know that the military is far less likely to proceed if it means killing children.

Armed and criminal groups will also try to use the military operations to their political advantage. When children are killed in bombardments, for example, FARC dissidents spin the local narrative into a story about how the state cares little for rural populations. The argument sticks because there is rarely a credible state presence that can serve and protect these same communities and earn their trust.

There are more immediate consequences, as well. In the aftermath of bombardments and high-level captures, armed and criminal groups often undertake what amounts to a purge of the population, killing or exiling anyone they believe to have collaborated with the state. And when the state takes out a leader, intra-group violence frequently ensues as former associates compete to take his place. Many residents shelter inside to avoid being caught up in the fighting, leaving children without school and parents without income. If new splinter groups emerge, the combat for control over neighborhoods can place even more civilians in the crossfire. This fallout widens the gap between immediate tactical results and long-term security gains. Taking out a senior leader wins applause, but conditions may well worsen on the ground.

De la Espriella’s administration has rightly identified winning territorial control, permanently dislodging criminal groups, and restoring state governance as its ultimate goals. The dilemma it now faces—exacerbated by U.S. support that focuses on attention-grabbing wins—is how to both achieve short-term results and ensure that these do not unleash new waves of violence, retaliation against civilians, and, in turn, criminal consolidation.

The challenge is twofold. First, the military needs to anticipate groups’ violent reactions and to safeguard the population. In the case of Santa Marta, for example, the government could have increased police presence in the city in the hours after El Cholo was killed. Before the next bombardment or high-level capture, the state should ensure that a ground team of soldiers or military police is ready to hold the ground, protect the community, and prevent the armed group from swiftly stepping back in to reclaim territory.

The second challenge—establishing lasting territorial control—is trickier. A permanent rural security presence might offer the best chance for Colombia’s government to change the direction of the conflict. The country’s military leadership controls only the tip of the spear, not the rest of the state. Time and again, governments “send only the military to the hardest areas,” a retired senior officer told Crisis Group. Moreover, soldiers are operating under heavy pressure for quick wins, which are measured in the number of combatants and especially high-value targets that are killed or captured. There is a real risk that by focusing largely on these statistics, the state will overlook more comprehensive policies necessary to achieve durable security.

Pressure to deliver fast results has in the past incentivized unacceptable practices. In the early 2000s, intense political demands for combat kills fueled elaborate schemes in which soldiers murdered thousands of civilians and counted them as guerrilla fatalities, a scandal known as “false positives.” These crimes deeply compromised public trust in the security forces, and the military has worked for years to build a culture and procedures that ensure they do not happen again. Today, the country’s senior military leadership is likely the first and best brake on any attempts to use excessive force. Commanders say that they are cognizant of the need to guard against rules being slowly stretched until they are broken. “We need a stronger security approach, but it also worries us when we hear there is pressure for ‘results,’” one officer said. But U.S. rhetoric and behavior, including its decision to sink what it claims are drug-smuggling boats on the high seas, compound these risks.

Success, then, may demand a strategy that is ultimately anchored in a much stronger and larger police force, fit for the job of safeguarding civilians rather than merely carrying out offensive strikes. Military operations can generate a window of opportunity by shuffling dynamics on the ground or removing certain dangerous players. Yet those reconfigurations will turn out in the state’s favor only if the resulting vacuum is eventually filled by reliable law enforcement. Take, for example, an operation that temporarily dislodges a group from a rural hamlet. If the military acts alone, it will likely enter and leave in a matter of days or even hours, allowing the armed group to quickly return. If, however, the offensive is followed up with permanent police patrols, access to the justice system, and social interventions, criminals will have a harder time regaining control.

This, of course, is easier said than done, given the government’s resource constraints. To create effective and permanent patrols, police departments operating in the countryside need more officers and equipment to maintain a presence in the most remote communities. Colombia has roughly 180,000 uniformed police officers, a figure that, per capita, puts it on the lower end of OECD countries, many of which are far more urbanized and therefore easier to patrol. In Colombia’s conflict-affected areas, outgunned officers must often stay inside their local stations for their own safety, limiting their ability to build trust and channels of communication with the population. Other state institutions, such as the justice system, say that they cannot establish a durable foothold in villages without guarantees of protection in the medium term—exactly what rural policing should strive to provide. The U.S. State Department’s Bureau of International Narcotics and Law Enforcement Affairs has funded the building and equipping of rural police stations in the past; it should expand this work. International partners with long-standing expertise in rural policing, such as Canada and France, could provide critical support, as well.

In cities, the challenge is different. In Santa Marta, for example, the state is present, but it is weak and vulnerable to the pressure and cooptation of the same groups that it must confront. The police need both more officers and better working conditions—including salaries, benefits, access to education, and social support for families—that could help them resist bribes. Regular vetting and counterintelligence checks can help ensure that police are fighting on the side of the state and not its adversaries.

Expanding the police will be an expensive, long-term undertaking. In the interim, Colombia could prioritize more personnel for critical areas and ensure that officers have a strong grounding in community policing, allowing them to understand the most pressing risks facing civilians. That means regular outreach to communities to build trust and make certain that officers are seen as having a stake in local well-being. The United States, meanwhile, must continue to help Bogotá strengthen its police and courts with direct financial and technical assistance.

Colombia, backed by renewed U.S. support, cannot afford to repeat past mistakes or ignore how the conflict has changed. Bogotá must therefore plan for and capitalize on military pressure to do something no government in Colombia’s history has done: govern well in conflict areas.

Bogotá Must Do More to Stop Armed Groups From Recruiting Minors

#5861. Risk and Reward Along Africa’s Red Sea Littoral

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Essay: Risk and Reward Along Africa’s Red Sea Littoral

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The new era of maritime chokepoint disruption has, in many ways, highlighted the importance of Africa’s Red Sea and Gulf of Aden states. The Houthis’ ability to hold the Bab el-Mandeb at risk, combined with the deepening rivalry between the Abraham and Mecca blocs of the Middle East, have created powerful incentives for a range of extra-regional actors to further expand their partnerships along the Horn of Africa’s coastline. This is particularly clear in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden, where the United Arab Emirates, Israel, and the United States have grown their diplomatic and military engagement in Puntland and Somaliland, and where the United States has pursued a diplomatic rapprochement with Eritrea.

What does this mean for the Horn of Africa’s littoral states? Although their geographic proximity to what Bruno Macaes called “the strait at the center of the world” has emerged as valuable strategic currency, it is not hard to see how the attempt to coopt Africa’s Red Sea states into one alliance or another could go wrong. The concerns are threefold: that the Horn’s littoral states might become entrapped in conflicts that are not their own; that the intra-Horn rivalries the littoral states are already enmeshed in are made more acute; and that internal governance conditions in the Horn’s states further deteriorate.

The new era of maritime chokepoint disruption has, in many ways, highlighted the importance of Africa’s Red Sea and Gulf of Aden states. The Houthis’ ability to hold the Bab el-Mandeb at risk, combined with the deepening rivalry between the Abraham and Mecca blocs of the Middle East, have created powerful incentives for a range of extra-regional actors to further expand their partnerships along the Horn of Africa’s coastline. This is particularly clear in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden, where the United Arab Emirates, Israel, and the United States have grown their diplomatic and military engagement in Puntland and Somaliland, and where the United States has pursued a diplomatic rapprochement with Eritrea.

What does this mean for the Horn of Africa’s littoral states? Although their geographic proximity to what Bruno Macaes called “the strait at the center of the world” has emerged as valuable strategic currency, it is not hard to see how the attempt to coopt Africa’s Red Sea states into one alliance or another could go wrong. The concerns are threefold: that the Horn’s littoral states might become entrapped in conflicts that are not their own; that the intra-Horn rivalries the littoral states are already enmeshed in are made more acute; and that internal governance conditions in the Horn’s states further deteriorate.

The Horn’s littoral states cannot wish away geography or the incentives that it creates. But they do have room to maneuver. Robust public debate among Horn publics, prudent decision-making from ruling elites, and conscientious engagement from key external partners can ensure that this new chapter of geopolitical centrality does not further destabilize a region that is already at the precipice.

The Horn of Africa has been a critical node since the inauguration of the Suez Canal in 1869. But the intensity with which this fact has been felt has waxed and waned over time. The start of the Yemen War in 2015 initiated another period of growing entanglement between the Horn of Africa and a range of outside powers, each of whom sought a strategic perch near the Red Sea corridor from which to project their security and commercial interests.

But the turmoil in the  Middle East after Oct. 7, 2023, has sharpened this dynamic. Two developments have been particularly consequential. The first is the Houthi campaign to interrupt maritime traffic in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden, with a first wave of attacks beginning in November 2023 and a second wave targeting Saudi shipping starting in the summer of 2026. The second dynamic has been the deepening rivalry between the Abraham and Mecca blocs of the Middle East. Although these respective blocs are not entirely coherent, there are clear divisions over Israel’s increasingly muscular security policy; the Iran crisis; and their respective interventions in conflicts between national governments and subnational armed actors across the region.

In the face of new strategic pressures, several extra-regional actors have sought enhanced access and position in this zone, over and above what they had secured in preceding years. A triumvirate of littoral countries/de-facto states have been immediate targets: Somaliland, Puntland, and Eritrea.

The impact is most apparent in Somaliland. Its economic partnership with the UAE, through the latter’s investment in the Berbera corridor, is long-standing. But this relationship has become increasingly securitized since 2024. The clearest evidence is what transpired at Berbera Airport, which has come under the effective security control of the UAE. Satellite imagery suggests the construction of an adjoining dock and the hardening of the airport’s infrastructure, including the development of underground bunkers that are potentially for ammunition and fuel. Many believe the airport has also become a logistical node in the UAE’s broader military supply network across East Africa, enabling the delivery of weapons to Sudan and Ethiopia.

Israel’s expanding role in Somaliland has run in parallel with the growing Emirati military presence. Israel diplomatically recognized Somaliland in December 2025—making it the first country in the world to do so—and some reports suggest an active Israeli intelligence presence in Berbera and the use of the airport for refueling its jets during the Iran war. Somaliland officials generally do not confirm these reports but acknowledge that an Israeli military presence is an eventual possibility.

A Soviet-made MiG-17 fighter jet, formerly belonging to the Somali Air Force, sits atop the Hargeisa War Memorial monument in Hargeisa, Somaliland, on Oct. 16. Mohamed Ahmed/Reuters

Washington has also joined the club. In the last year, Somaliland has hosted the commanding generals of AFRICOM and SOCAFRICA. A number of U.S. military personnel based at the Baledogle Airfield in Somalia have now been redeployed to Somaliland, prompting one senior Somaliland official to privately quip, “Team Mogadishu has now become Team Hargeisa.” U.S. training opportunities for Somaliland’s security forces appear to have also grown. These developments are still nascent and as such do not yet involve the deployment of critical intelligence, security, and reconnaissance capabilities, but the direction of travel is fairly clear.

The Emirati government’s formula in Puntland has largely tracked its approach to Somaliland. Beginning in the 2010s, the Emirati footprint in the statelet had mostly consisted of a commercial port concession in Bosaso and training Puntland’s Maritime Security Forces. But from 2024 onward, the UAE scaled up its activity in Bosaso, turning an airbase next to the civilian airport into another logistical hub through which weapons, ammunition, and personnel flowed to Emirati allies throughout the Horn. The UAE has also supported Puntland’s 2024-2025 offensive against Islamic State contingents in the region through direct drone strikes. The United States has also directly backed counter-Islamic State military operations, and according to Puntland authorities, agreed in August 2026 to establish a military facility at the same Bosaso airbase that houses an Emirati presence.

Eritrea’s incorporation into this picture looks a bit different. Its external alignments sharply diverge from Somaliland and Puntland, and there is currently no evidence that Eritrea is poised to play host to foreign military facilities. Yet if the past is prologue, then Eritrea could soon find itself housing a more significant military presence. There are unconfirmed reports of the potential for an increased Egyptian presence in the southern port of Assab. Assab served as the logistical fulcrum of Emirati military operations in Yemen, and back in the early 2000s, the Eritrean government lobbied the United States to establish a military base in Eritrea. At the time, Washington declined the Eritrean offer and established its premiere African military base in neighboring Djibouti.

People sail past container ships anchored near the Bab el-Mandeb strait on Aug. 6. AFP via Getty Images

Maritime exigencies in and around the Bab el-Mandeb have now pushed Eritrea higher on the Trump administration’s agenda. While U.S.-Eritrea relations have been relatively poor for more than two decades, Washington allowed a Tigray-related sanctions package targeting the Eritrean government to expire in September 2026. This was a clear bid to enhance relations with Asmara and was tied to a broad, if not clearly defined, sense that Eritrea mattered for Red Sea security. This sanctions removal occurred even though some important U.S. policymakers believed that Eritrea had not really done enough to warrant such action.

Somaliland, Puntland, and Eritrea will probably extract clear strategic rewards from their emerging geopolitical centrality and the related expansion of security partnerships. All three face increasingly acute security dilemmas from what they regard as revanchist neighbors and their respective external backers. All of their national political projects are also at critical junctures: Somaliland is attempting to build momentum for wider recognition; Puntland is maneuvering to protect its autonomy within a Somali federation; and Eritrea is working to fully break its political estrangement from much of the international system. Somaliland and Puntland have even leveraged the good offices of Abu Dhabi and Washington to improve a bilateral relationship long riddled with tension.

People participate in a rally to celebrate the Houthis’ takeover of the strategic port city of Mocha and the Bab el-Mandeb strait in Sana’a, Yemen, on Sept. 11. Mohammed Hamoud/Getty Images

The risks, however, are very real. The first is that these small polities quickly find themselves entrapped in conflicts that are not their own. The concern is most relevant to Somaliland, where the intensification of the Emirati, Israeli, and U.S. security presence is clearest. Though Houthi attacks in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden have had negative economic repercussions on Berbera port, Somaliland’s authorities have no interest in getting involved in a regional war against the Houthis. But they might not have a choice. The Houthis themselves have threatened to strike any Israeli military presence in Somaliland and even hacked the website of Somaliland’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs in early July. If a conflict led to missile or drone strikes on Berbera, it would have a catastrophic impact on trade, government revenue, and oil imports.

There is also a risk that Somaliland’s deepening security engagement with the Abraham bloc alienates it from Saudi Arabia, which is clearly unnerved by the growing security presence of the Emiratis and Israelis in the southern Red Sea. Somaliland’s critical livestock export sector, which provides 80 percent of all export earnings, is oriented toward the Saudi market, meaning it can ill afford to antagonize Riyadh.

Somaliland’s authorities are no doubt hoping to manage the scale and conduct of the foreign military element within its borders, so as to not provoke retaliation from actors they otherwise have few differences with. Although not widely known, when it was negotiating a memorandum of understanding for sea access with Ethiopia in late 2023, Somaliland placed real limitations on an Ethiopian naval presence in its Gulf of Aden shores. This included the denial of a land corridor between the Ethiopian border and naval base, as well as limits on the Ethiopian military’s interactions with local communities and third-party military activity. But does Somaliland have the autonomy to fully constrain what Israel, the UAE, and the United States do within its borders?

The second risk is that external actors’ push for new Red Sea-oriented partnerships might inadvertently fuel escalatory dynamics in the region. This is most evident in the growing tensions between Eritrea and Ethiopia. The scramble for positions along the Horn’s Red Sea littoral has provided landlocked Ethiopia a new set of justifications to press its maritime claims, including to the port of Assab in Eritrea. Ethiopian officials have argued that since the Red Sea and its Horn ports are increasingly contested terrain, an Ethiopian naval presence is the only way to ensure the country’s economic and security interests. They also make the point that if it is legitimate for outside powers to establish military bases along the Red Sea littoral, then it is an equally legitimate course of action for Ethiopia.

A view of U.S. military aircraft at Camp Lemonnier in Djibouti on Jan. 21, 2024. Djibouti sits opposite Yemen, across the Bab el-Mandeb strait, and between Eritrea and Somaliland.Getty Images

At the same time, U.S. sanctions removal, and the growing proximity between Cairo and Asmara, has increased Eritrea’s willingness to risk conflict with its southern neighbor. Indeed, the decision by the TPLF and the Alliance for Survival to launch offensives on Sept. 23, likely with the approval of Asmara, came just six days after the U.S. sanctions regime on Eritrea expired.

Meanwhile, Emirati-Israeli involvement in Somaliland and Puntland has further consolidated Mogadishu’s increasing all-weather alliance with Turkey. President Hassan Sheikh has used Somalia’s growing defense partnerships with Turkey, which has deployed F-16s to the region to fight Al-Shabaab, to put pressure on Somaliland and Puntland. Most recently, this has involved an electronic visa scheme managed by Mogadishu to control the movement of foreign passport holders into Somaliland, and a reported agreement with Ankara to build a military base in Laasqoray, which sits in territory that Somaliland considers its own.

Analysts fear that the renewed conflict could quickly draw in other regional actors.

How Washington bargains away its Africa strategy to other regions.

As a result, the risk of conflict between Mogadishu, Garowe, and even Hargeisa has increased considerably. In July and August, for instance, there were military confrontations between Puntland’s government and the Puntland Security Force, a militia that had increasingly come under the sway of Mogadishu, resulting in the PSF bases in Galkayo and Bosaso being seized by Puntland authorities.

A final risk is related to the core governance questions that will shape the regional security landscape of the Horn in the coming years.

Consider Eritrea, which has been mired in a deep human rights crisis for decades. This has driven hundreds of thousands of its young people abroad and produced deep, if still suppressed, social fissures. The country has no functioning constitution or electoral system, and thus no mechanism for peacefully navigating succession or adjudicating domestic political differences. Its political elite, in power since 1991, is unwilling to cultivate younger leaders or embrace basic economic and political reforms, offering little more than time-worn cliches about the need to defend against sabotage by external enemies.  Unsurprisingly, U.S. sanctions relief engages none of these issues.

In Somaliland and Puntland, the problem is one of political polarization, as deepening external partnerships have widened disagreements between contending political factions. Somaliland’s recognition deal with Israel has drawn internal criticism, including from religious actors, on both moral and strategic grounds. This was particularly true after Hargeisa made the controversial decision to place its embassy in Jerusalem rather than Tel Aviv in July. In Puntland, some opponents of President Saeed Deni believe he is using Emirati patronage to further consolidate his power. In all of these debates and more, fairly explosive clan rivalries lurk just beneath the surface.

Members of the Somaliland Coast Guard participate in a parade in Hargeisa on May 18. Kang-Chun Cheng/AFP via Getty Images

The Horn’s littoral states can navigate the risks associated with their emerging geopolitical centrality. But it will require more than business as usual. The first step is robust public debate about the risks and rewards of external security entanglements, so that all relevant considerations are surfaced. In many ways, Somaliland is ahead of the curve in this regard, a reflection of its relatively strong—although far from perfect—democratic system.

Ruling elites must also prudently assess the costs and benefits of foreign basing and military cooperation. The challenge here is that the democratic deficit in some of these states means that regime security often prevails over public interest. There are also separate questions of state capacity in some of these polities and the fundamental ability of the government to recognize substantive problems and devise responses. Finally, external partners, particularly the United States, must recognize that the transactionalism of the moment will not deliver stability in one of the world’s most essential maritime corridors. The only solution is to encourage better and more accountable governance along Africa’s Red Sea and Gulf of Aden littoral.

Michael Woldemariam is an associate professor and the Ph.D. program director in the University of Maryland’s School of Public Policy.

Biruk Terrefe is a lecturer in African politics at the University of Bayreuth and a research associate at Oxford’s African Studies Centre.

#5860. DOJ recovers $8B, charges more than 800 suspects in largest anti-scam surge in history

The Department of Justice on Tuesday announced the largest anti-scam enforcement surge in its history, charging over 800 defendants and recovering more than $8 billion in intended losses.

Federal prosecutors have charged more than 800 defendants and recovered over $8 billion in intended victim losses as part of the largest anti-scam enforcement surge in Justice Department history, U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche announced Tuesday.

Speaking alongside FBI Director Kash Patel at a news conference in Washington, Blanche said the enforcement blitz follows President Donald Trump's March executive order directing a government-wide crackdown on fraud and predatory schemes targeting Americans. Since that directive, the Justice Department has filed more than 400 criminal cases across 85 federal districts, resulting in charges, extraditions, guilty pleas and prison sentences.

"We are not just going after individual scammers," Blanche said. "We are dismantling the criminal networks that have been stealing from the American people for far too long."

INSIDE OPERATION BLACKOUT: PATEL REVEALS GLOBAL SCAM ‘CITIES’ TARGETING YOUR PARENTS’ LIFE SAVINGS

A board with data, labeled "2026 Anti-Scam Surge," is displayed during a press conference with U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche, FBI Director Kash Patel and Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva about recent efforts against cyber fraud at the Justice Department in Washington, D.C., Nov. 24, 2026. (Reuters/Jonathan Ernst)

Patel detailed the FBI's global offensive under Operation Blackout, an interagency campaign targeting international fraud syndicates. According to Patel, federal agents and international partners have seized nearly $17 billion in illicit funds, confiscated more than 14,000 digital devices and dismantled 29 scam compounds across four continents.

Those compounds operate as self-contained towns where criminal networks force human trafficking victims to run online fraud schemes against Americans, Patel said, noting that joint operations have freed more than 2,200 trafficked workers.

"They thought they could get away with it in Southeast Asia. They moved to Africa. They moved to the Middle East," Patel said. "We annihilated them on every single continent that they chose to operate and attack American citizens."

FBI OPERATION TARGETS SCAM COMPOUNDS IN AFRICA WITH ARRESTS, ACCOUNT TAKEDOWNS

The crackdown comes as online and phone fraud continue to hit U.S. households at staggering rates. Blanche cited a recent Gallup study finding that 10% of Americans reported that they or a household member fell victim to a scam in 2025, resulting in an estimated $68 billion in losses in a single year.

Officials pointed to a wide range of schemes disrupted since March, from felony inmates using contraband cellphones and handwritten scripts to run jury-duty extortion scams to Mexico-based timeshare fraud allegedly operated by major drug trafficking cartels.

FBI Director Kash Patel speaks alongside U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche during a news conference to announce the results of a crackdown on cyber-enabled scams, at the Department of Justice in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 24, 2026. (Ken Cedeno/AFP via Getty Images)

In the past two weeks alone, federal authorities worked with Mexican officials to extradite a list broker accused of selling the names of more than 1 million elderly Americans to international scam networks, resulting in at least $15 million in victim losses. Agents also dismantled websites impersonating U.S. federal courts and arrested nine suspects in an alleged sweepstakes scheme that defrauded seniors of millions of dollars, Blanche said.

Overseas, a recent enforcement push coordinated in London yielded 28 arrests across Africa, $11 million in seized assets, the rescue of 120 trafficked workers and the removal of 3.7 million fraudulent Facebook and Instagram accounts, Patel said.

Beyond arrests and asset seizures, federal officials are also intervening before money leaves victims' bank accounts. Through Operation Level Up, a joint effort with the U.S. Secret Service, agents have prevented nearly $700 million in potential losses across 11,000 targeted Americans, 77% of whom did not realize they were being scammed, Patel said.

"The United States government and President Trump does not tolerate organized theft perpetrated against the American people," Blanche said. "No matter where these criminals are in the world, we will find them and hold them accountable."

#5858. Europe Steps Into the Breach on Ukraine

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Europe Steps Into the Breach on Ukraine

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Understanding the conflict four years in.

Now that the Trump administration appears to have turned unequivocally against Kyiv and in favor of Moscow, it falls to Europe to backstop Ukraine’s survival. This week will be crucial, with the European Council meeting on Nov. 26-27 to decide on an additional loan of 45 billion euros (about $49.5 billion) to tide Ukraine over next year as it wrestles with nonstop missile strikes, jet-powered drones, and a gaping hole in its budget.

The Trump administration has, since the start of its second term, been implacably hostile to Kyiv, from Oval Office beatdowns to a relaxation of sanctions on Russia to demands that Ukraine desist from carrying out long-range strikes against Russian oil facilities. That hostility, like the war, has only escalated, with U.S. President Donald Trump suspending sanctions on Russian diesel exports (in a bid to lower fuel prices) and neutering the latest legislation meant to economically punish Moscow.

Now that the Trump administration appears to have turned unequivocally against Kyiv and in favor of Moscow, it falls to Europe to backstop Ukraine’s survival. This week will be crucial, with the European Council meeting on Nov. 26-27 to decide on an additional loan of 45 billion euros (about $49.5 billion) to tide Ukraine over next year as it wrestles with nonstop missile strikes, jet-powered drones, and a gaping hole in its budget.

The Trump administration has, since the start of its second term, been implacably hostile to Kyiv, from Oval Office beatdowns to a relaxation of sanctions on Russia to demands that Ukraine desist from carrying out long-range strikes against Russian oil facilities. That hostility, like the war, has only escalated, with U.S. President Donald Trump suspending sanctions on Russian diesel exports (in a bid to lower fuel prices) and neutering the latest legislation meant to economically punish Moscow.

“It began with the diesel deal. This is the longest sustained effort by Team Trump” to put its thumb on the scales, said John Herbst, a former U.S. ambassador to Ukraine. “It annoys me even more that they are helping [Russian President Vladimir] Putin do this. Putin wants to undermine NATO, and that is why it is so frustrating.”

Trump had previously adumbrated his approach to Ukraine by delaying approval of the bipartisan legislation, championed by former Sen. Lindsey Graham, that would have levied stiff tariffs on Russia’s trading partners and put additional sanctions on top Russian officials. When the legislation finally did pass, Trump watered it down, waiving the sanctions and for once forgetting about tariffs.

“I found U.S. policy over the last six weeks depressing,” said Steven Pifer, another former U.S. ambassador to Ukraine. “I think Trump is lost to the cause, even if the American people are not.”

Now it falls to Europe to fill the gap, both in terms of Kyiv’s budget and its defense needs. About half of the new loan is earmarked for Ukraine’s administration and the other half for defense procurement.

The European loan is not a done deal. There are four holdouts among the usual suspects—Hungary, Slovakia, Bulgaria, and the Czech Republic—that are leery of signing onto more blocwide debt to support a country they view askance. But by Friday, informed observers say, the European Union will probably be able to hammer through a fresh 45 billion on top of the 90 billion euro loan for Ukraine that it is still doling out.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen has said the latest loan would be backed by frozen Russian assets held in Europe. Those central bank assets total about $300 billion worldwide, with just over $200 billion in Belgium. Despite years of posturing, four years into the war, neither Europe nor the United States nor the United Kingdom has moved from freezing to seizing, fearful of the potential legal, financial, and other consequences of grabbing illicit Russian money. That makes the ultimate collateral for this new European loan something of a question mark.

“Europe has no choice but to fund Ukraine’s defense. The question is how you do it,” said Timothy Ash, a Russia expert at Chatham House. “I find it incredible, when you put it across like this, which taxpayers should pay for this: Europe’s or Russia’s?” (He has some thoughts.)

The bigger question is broader. Europe knows that enabling Ukraine to survive the war, or at least avoid a dishonorable peace, is critical to the country’s survival as well as its own. The problem, which has been apparent since January 2025, is that Ukraine and Europe are on their own. The United States has not provided any fresh financial support for Kyiv since Trump came back to office, has at times interrupted intelligence-sharing and satellite links, and continues to block the sale of much-need missile interceptors held in European inventories.

U.S. Undersecretary of Defense Elbridge Colby scolded Ukraine last week for striking Russian oil facilities, arguing that such attacks were ineffective. (Ukraine’s long-range strikes have taken offline about half of Russia’s refinery capacity in recent months, cratered its energy exports, and are leading to lines at gas stations in Russia.) Meanwhile, Russian missiles and drones continue to rain down on Kyiv, Kharkiv, and other major cities.

“Trump is just defending Putin and making it harder for Ukraine to defend itself,” Herbst said.

Germany’s vocal support for mobilizing hundreds of billions of Moscow’s reserves could be a game-changer for Ukraine.

For all the talk of a new world order, the Trump-Xi-Putin meeting produced little of substance.

One question that looms now is whether Europe’s determination to support Ukraine—Europe has already provided nearly 240 billion euros (about $260 billion), far more than the United States or any other country—will push Trump into reprisal mode. He has in the past slapped tariffs on Europe while waiving penalties on Russia. Over the weekend, Trump posted a photo of himself with Putin titled, simply, “My Friend.”

“If Trump wants to go down that route, it might get ugly in Europe, but I don’t think Europe can afford to let Ukraine lose. There is a real understanding in Europe about how high the stakes are for them,” Ash said.

“It will be interesting to see where this goes in terms of the U.S.-EU relationship. This could be a huge crisis. We have to see how far he is willing to take this.”

Keith Johnson is a staff writer at Foreign Policy covering geoeconomics and energy. Bluesky: @kfj-fp.bsky.social X: @KFJ_FP

#5857. Biden's first-year fundraising fails to crack 1% of Obama's library haul as questions swirl over legacy

Ohio State accounting professor Brian Mittendorf examines the Obama Foundation's financial health, assets and endowment as construction costs hit $1 billion.

Former President Joe Biden has raised a shockingly small amount of funds for his presidential library compared to former President Barack Obama.

The figures are fueling skepticism about how history will remember Biden's single term in the White House.

In 2017, Obama’s first year out of office, the two-term president managed to bring in $232.6 million in donations and contributions to fund the foundation behind his presidential library. By comparison, Biden secured just $1.5 million in 2025 for his presidential library foundation, per tax filings.

This is less than 1% of what his former boss raised in the same amount of time after leaving office.

Even before leaving office, President Donald Trump’s library foundation has already dwarfed that of his predecessor by raising tens of millions of dollars from a variety of sources, including companies that settled lawsuits with him.

OBAMA CENTER ISN'T A TRADITIONAL PRESIDENTIAL LIBRARY. CRITICS SAY IT'S AN ACTIVISM CENTER.

Former US presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden shake hands during the opening of the Barack Obama Presidential Center in Chicago, Illinois, on June 18, 2026. (Kent NISHIMURA / AFP via Getty Images)

Though the Joe and Jill Biden Presidential Library Foundation has yet to decide on a construction site, it’s already running a deficit, spending $600,000 more than it raised in 2025. A Biden library official claimed that it wasn’t actively fundraising in 2025 in a statement shared with CBS News.

The lack of enthusiasm for Biden’s legacy has led some to claim that Americans may simply forget about his presidency as the years go on.

"Presidencies usually fade," Axios’ Zachary Basu wrote of his legacy. "But in just one year, Biden’s has been reduced to a footnote by a successor committed to dismantling every pillar of Washington’s old liberal order."

Heritage Action press manager Doug Blair told Fox News Digital, "Joe Biden spent half a century in Washington and did nothing to benefit the American people."

"The anemic fundraising for his presidential library is a fitting bookend for a failed presidency Americans are eager to forget," he added. "Joe Biden left Americans worse off by every conceivable measure and his legacy will be a southern border blown wide open, the disgraceful image of him checking his watch as fallen American troops returned home from Afghanistan, and endless scandals and investigations surrounding him and his corrupt family.'

BIDEN'S BATTLE WITH ADVANCING CANCER RENEWS FOCUS ON PROSTATE DISEASE

Allies of the former president point to the fact that Biden presided over the end of the COVID-19 pandemic, influential pieces of domestic legislation and America’s withdrawal from Afghanistan, parts of his legacy that could contribute to historical staying power.

A January 2025 Gallup poll found that 54% of U.S. adults believed history would remember Biden as a "below-average" or "poor" president.

Former U.S. President Joe Biden attends the dedication ceremony for the opening of the Barack Obama Presidential Center in John Lewis Plaza (Photo by Scott Olson/Getty Images)

A source close to Biden’s foundation told CBS News that it ramped up its fundraising in 2026, pulling in multiple multi-million-dollar contributions, including two $5 million donations.

Another source told the outlet that some of Biden's close allies are frustrated that Democratic donors aren’t stepping up to support the former president’s library.

Further complicating Biden’s fundraising efforts, and his ability to nurture his public image, is his advanced age and Stage 4 prostate cancer, which has metastasized to his bones.

OBAMA CENTER OPENS AFTER YEARS-LONG SAGA AS LOCALS WARN ‘MONSTROSITY’ COULD PRICE THEM OUT

Former President Barack Obama and the Obama Presidential Center in Chicago, where construction costs are now projected to reach approximately $1 billion, nearly triple the upper end of the original estimate. (Taylor Hill/Getty Images; Talía Sprague/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

While the increase in funds is no doubt encouraging for Biden’s allies, it still puts them far behind Obama, who raised $164.8 million for his foundation in 2018.

Obama ultimately spent nearly $1 billion on his presidential library in Chicago, a sum Biden likely will not be able to reach given his current fundraising levels.

The Joe and Jill Biden Presidential Library Foundation and the Obama Foundation did not respond to requests for comment when reached by Fox News Digital on Tuesday.
