#5846. Congress and the Coming Crises

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

IVO H. DAALDER is Senior Fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. He served as U.S. Ambassador to NATO from 2009 to 2013.

JAMES M. LINDSAY is Mary and David Boies Distinguished Senior Fellow in U.S. Foreign Policy at the Council on Foreign Relations.

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

Democrats won a historic victory in the U.S. midterm elections by running on a key voter concern: affordability. They promised to lower gas and diesel prices, curb electricity and water bills, and cut borrowing costs. Now voters expect results.

Making progress will not be easy, given that the affordability crisis is inextricably linked to a set of thorny policy issues. Soaring gas and diesel prices stem from wars in Europe and the Middle East that have overextended the United States and provided China, Iran, North Korea, and Russia openings to exploit. Rising electricity and water bills are driven by the growing number of data centers powering an artificial intelligence revolution that is nearing a point of no return, when machines improve faster than humans can understand or control. And higher prices and borrowing costs are the result of an unsustainable U.S. national debt, now above $40 trillion, that requires exorbitant interest payments and has foreign governments questioning the safety of the dollar and U.S. Treasury bonds.

These problems not only strain Americans’ pocketbooks but also threaten the security and prosperity of the United States—and the world. If Beijing and Moscow try to take advantage of a weakened United States by escalating simultaneously in Taiwan and eastern Europe, those moves could be the first steps toward World War III. If AI escapes the bounds of human control, no one will be safe. And if the national debt continues to soar, Washington could trigger a fiscal crisis that devastates the American economy and leaves the United States far less able to meet the challenges it faces overseas.

Action to address the United States’ problems therefore cannot wait. But politics may stand in the way. The White House is more likely to obstruct than to cooperate with congressional Democrats over the next two years, as President Donald Trump’s appointment of the pugilistic Stephen Miller as the new White House chief of staff underscores. Moreover, one electoral victory did not erase the country’s deep partisan and policy divisions. Going into the 2028 presidential election, Republicans know that a successful Democratic Congress would endanger their chances of keeping the White House. Trump and congressional Republicans, therefore, have considerable motivation to derail Democratic plans.

Democrats are not without options, however. They can pursue legislation, seeking cross-party collaboration when possible and pushing ahead even in the face of presidential vetoes. Sometimes it is the president, not Congress, who blinks. When he doesn’t, voters will know who stood in the way. Democrats can also use public hearings and the investigative powers of Congress to shed light on the threats facing the country and to build a case for change—much as the Fulbright hearings did during the Vietnam War and the Church Committee did to highlight Cold War–era intelligence abuses. They can put the defense secretary and military commanders, AI executives, and Treasury and budget officials under oath to account for how they prosecuted the Iran war, raced ahead on AI without adequate safeguards, and exploded the deficit. They can also call on economists, scientists, and strategists to explain how to steer the country away from peril.

Congressional efforts may not be enough to meaningfully change Trump’s policies. But the risks of major war, unconstrained AI, and eroding economic competitiveness are too great to wait two more years to address. These problems, and the affordability crisis they have generated, will only get worse. If Democrats in Congress do not start advancing a serious agenda to address them now, these dangers may become impossible to contain.

The first order of the new Congress’s business should be to end the Iran war. Operation Epic Fury has sent energy prices soaring and put freedom of navigation at risk. It has also highlighted the United States’ vulnerabilities in ways that could prove dangerous—and costly—in the years ahead. Iranian missiles and drones wreaked havoc on American military bases in the Persian Gulf. U.S. efforts to defeat these attacks depleted supplies of critical munitions, showing that the U.S. military has become overly reliant on very expensive, hard-to-produce high-tech weapons. The economic, diplomatic, military, and intelligence support that China and Russia have provided Iran revealed the extent to which autocracies are cooperating to stretch U.S. capabilities and undermine American power. The U.S. Navy is effectively bottled up in the Arabian Sea, limiting its presence in the Atlantic and Pacific. In theory, U.S. allies could help fill the gaps, but Trump’s decision to launch the war without consulting them and his adversarial attitude toward them have strained those relationships, weakening deterrence of a Russia and China bent on securing strategic advantage.

Perceptions of weakness, even when they are wrong, are often why wars begin. Moscow and Beijing might see the next two years as a window of opportunity. Europe and Japan are rearming, but that buildup will take years. The American military is stretched thin, and political divisions make decisive action difficult. Together this could be an invitation for U.S. adversaries to engage in aggression in Europe and Asia. Russia is already pursuing a shadow war against NATO and could test the alliance’s unity further by probing the Baltic states. China could impose a blockade on Taiwan. It is not difficult to see how, if Moscow and Beijing take these steps in concert, the resulting conflicts could escalate into a global war.

The new Congress will have limited ability to forestall these events. The U.S. Constitution puts much of diplomacy beyond the reach of the legislature; only the president decides when to negotiate, what to discuss, and with whom. But Congress does have tools to wield. If the Trump administration remains in a standoff with the Iranian regime when Democrats take control in January, Congress should immediately invoke the War Powers Resolution to pass binding legislation demanding that Trump either secure congressional authorization for the war or end it. Congress can also use its power of the purse to condition or halt further spending on the war, preferably as part of must-pass legislation, such as a defense appropriations bill, to avoid a potential Senate filibuster. Trump would likely veto any such legislation, and Congress has a poor record on overriding presidential vetoes. But challenging a war that Americans clearly oppose would fulfill the promise Democrats made on the campaign trail, reduce the likelihood that Trump would escalate in dangerous ways, and mark a step forward in Congress’s reclamation of its war powers.

There is more the Democratic Congress can do to lower the risks of global conflict, too. It can start by taking a stronger line on Russia. Moscow has repaid Trump’s conciliatory policies—including his waiving of sanctions Congress authorized earlier this year with the bipartisan Lindsay O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act—with aid to Tehran and an intensified war on Ukraine. Only greater pressure can force the Kremlin to stand down. Congress should amend the Graham Act to give itself the right to review and overturn any presidential waivers of sanctions mandated under the law. It should also reaffirm the current ban on energy purchases from Russia and prohibit the administration from using government funds to follow through on its proposal to restart Russian gas and oil exports to Europe through a U.S. intermediary. Congress should resume military aid to Ukraine, ensure the availability of Starshield communications to Ukrainian forces, and refuse to fund any reduction in the permanent U.S. military presence in Europe or Asia that it has not approved itself.

Finally, to help dissuade China from threatening Taiwan, Congress should use defense spending bills to speed up the sale of arms to Taiwan. And Congress should legally codify the 1982 Six Assurances, which include a promise by Washington not to consult Beijing on arms sales to Taiwan. Many of these measures could garner sufficient Republican support to pass with veto-proof majorities, forcing Trump’s hand.

Additionally, Congress should use its oversight powers to assess the vulnerabilities that the Iran war has revealed. It should evaluate whether the U.S. intelligence agencies and the Pentagon adequately anticipated Iran’s response to U.S. attacks, the scale and causes of the shortages of high-tech weapons, and the risks that AI-aided drone warfare poses to U.S. overseas bases. Congress can also review the extent of Chinese and Russian support for Iran; assess NATO’s defenses against Russia, including its response to the escalating shadow war; determine U.S. readiness for Chinese efforts to coerce Taiwan; and evaluate proposals to make Pentagon acquisition more flexible and less expensive. An essential step toward enhancing U.S. deterrence is to identify what is broken in the U.S. defense posture and what needs to be fixed.

The new Congress will also need to rein in the AI industry. Opposition to AI data centers and other infrastructure galvanized voters in the midterms. Across the country, 46 of 47 ballot measures passed, placing new restrictions on new data centers, which have brought rising power and water bills and explosive land costs. Many Americans fear what AI will do to jobs today and for the next generation. They distrust the tech executives who control it and question who will truly benefit from its development.

Those concerns are justified. AI is advancing with increasing speed and introducing unknown risks. Five months ago, AI generated one percent of Anthropic’s research and development; today, it accounts for more than a quarter. Lab leaders predict that AI will become self-generating within a year. That is a scary prospect, not only for displaced computer scientists but also for society at large. AI could pose an existential threat to global computer networks; rogue AI agents have already colluded to evade human control. Leading firms have warned of even greater dangers. Alphabet said recently that its products could lead to “severe or catastrophic security and societal incidents,” and Anthropic has cited “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.”

AI also poses profound national security challenges for the United States. The Trump administration and the tech industry have invoked an innovation race with China as a reason not to regulate the technology. That race is real, but many of the most important stages are not the ones Washington is fixating on. They are also ones Beijing is winning. Perhaps U.S. firms will be the first to develop artificial general intelligence. But in the meantime, the rest of the world and many U.S. companies are adopting Chinese open-weight AI models. Alibaba’s Qwen model now accounts for more than half of all open-source model downloads worldwide. As cheap, easy-to-adapt Chinese models become the global default, Beijing gains leverage over the digital infrastructure on which other countries’ economies and militaries will run. The companies that build these models are legally bound to cooperate with Chinese intelligence services. And because these models spread with few safeguards, they are easier to turn to malicious use.

Meanwhile, AI is transforming war. Autonomous weapons are essential to Ukraine’s defense against a superior military, shooting down long-range drones and targeting Russian forces, supply lines, and strategic industries. This year, the Pentagon quietly revised its doctrine to let AI select and initiate strikes while humans merely monitor, which risks errors and escalation at a speed no human can catch. And as AI lowers the barriers to entry for launching cyberattacks and developing new biological weapons, every country is in danger.

The Trump administration is clearly reluctant to address any of these threats. It is beholden to figures such as Jensen Huang, Elon Musk, and Mark Zuckerburg who have wanted to keep the AI industry unregulated and unconstrained. Huang and Nvidia, the company he led, had long pushed the administration to relax U.S. export controls that restricted the sale of AI chips to China and other countries; the firm’s current scandal has revealed just how loose those restrictions had become. Now, the administration wants China to report serious AI-related incidents through a new bilateral channel yet opposes requiring American companies to do the same. Given how rapidly AI is advancing, Congress cannot afford to stand idly by. There is a slew of legislative proposals already circulating, many of them with bipartisan support, that would require incident reporting, auditors in AI labs, and mandatory safety testing before deployment. Other AI industry leaders, such as Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and Alphabet’s Demis Hassabis, have supported these ideas, but the Trump administration has ignored them, asking instead for the industry to police itself.

Finding the right regulations and safety mechanisms, and then codifying them in legislation, will take time. In the meantime, Congress should use its convening power to seek input from industry leaders, experts, and the public about how this technology can be controlled. The leaders of frontier AI labs should be asked, under oath, to explain not just the upside of AI but also its risks. Elon Musk, for example, told The Economist this summer that within ten years, work will be “optional” because of AI—but he has also said there is a ten to 20 percent chance that AI will lead to the extinction of humanity. He and others who share this belief should be forced to justify why this risk is acceptable and why tech leaders are the ones to decide on behalf of humanity. They should also explain what it would take to reduce the ten to 20 percent chance to zero.

Public hearings that expose both the dangers and the opportunities of AI will make it easier to galvanize action to harness the technology’s promise safely while preventing the worst possible outcomes. And Congress has some levers it can pull now to address threats to national security. It can use the defense authorization bill to require that humans retain meaningful control over lethal force and to demand an accounting of how AI is reshaping military doctrine. It can also fund efforts to make American models, rather than Chinese ones, the default choice for countries around the world. If legislation takes too long to pass, hearings can at least lay the groundwork for a future administration to act.

Finally, Congress must address threats to the United States’ economic power. With $40 trillion in public debt, an annual budget deficit of $2 trillion, and annual interest payments topping more than $1 trillion, the U.S. economy is living on borrowed time. China and Russia are working to “de-dollarize” their economies by encouraging the use of cryptocurrency and other means of settling international transactions and by creating alternatives to the U.S.-dominated global banking system.

Even U.S. allies are reducing their reliance on that system: Norway’s sovereign wealth fund has cut its holding of U.S. dollars, the Dutch and French central banks have pulled their gold holdings from New York, and European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde has called for a “global euro” that would serve as an alternative to the dollar. If the dollar were to lose its status as the world’s premier reserve currency, Washington would be forced to spend even more money servicing the debt than it does now, intensifying the country’s fiscal problems, slowing economic growth while driving up inflation, and diminishing its ability to use economic and financial sanctions to pressure adversaries.

An immediate objective for the Democratic Congress will be to rein in Trump’s tariffs, because they raise prices, unsettle the markets that finance U.S. debt, and make U.S. producers less competitive globally without making a meaningful dent in the deficit. Congress will be operating from a disadvantage. Over the past century, it has delegated to the White House considerable tariff-setting authority, which Trump has eagerly exercised. Clawing back that power will be difficult. Significant disagreement exists among Democrats and between Democrats and Republicans about how to rewrite tariff law, and Trump will likely fight to defend his favorite foreign policy tool. Yet one area where concerted congressional action might be possible is in walking back Trump’s unilateral tariffs on Canada, which not only run counter to the U.S.-Mexico-Canada trade agreement that Trump negotiated and Congress approved in his first term but also hurt Republican voters in Maine and across the Midwest. Congress can aim to repeal Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930, which allows the president to impose tariffs of up to 50 percent on countries that treat U.S. goods differently from those from other countries. The provision was a forgotten relic until Trump invoked it in August to impose a fresh round of tariffs on Canada. And legislators should narrow the reach of other presidential tariff authorities by mandating expedited congressional review, instituting joint resolutions of disapproval that cannot be filibustered, and adding sunset provisions to emergency tariff authorities so that the president needs congressional approval to keep them in place.

Tariffs, however, are only part of the problem. A comprehensive solution to the country’s fiscal troubles would require raising taxes or cutting entitlements, which neither party has been willing to do. But Democrats have other cards they can play to start undoing the damage of the past two years. Trump’s 2025 One Big Beautiful Bill Act will cost the Treasury some $4.5 trillion over the next decade; Congress should repeal the law’s tax breaks for corporations and the wealthiest Americans and restore the Internal Revenue Service’s ability to collect taxes that are already owed. It should also write the Federal Reserve’s independence into law, so that no president can force interest rates down to make government borrowing cheaper. And Congress should establish a bipartisan commission modeled on the 1981 Greenspan Commission, which resolved a Social Security funding crisis, to recommend steps the United States must take to put its finances on a sustainable path and to ensure its global economic competitiveness.

In his 1948 campaign, Harry Truman salvaged his presidency by running against what he called the “do-nothing” Congress. That history should be a warning to Democrats. Voters handed them a congressional majority to bring down costs, and they must start right away to address the forces driving those costs—wars stretching American power to the breaking point, an AI revolution racing beyond human control, and a fiscal hole eroding the foundations of U.S. economic power. If the Democratic majority spends the next two years relitigating the administration’s misdeeds but failing to make progress on the issue voters care most about, Trump and the next Republican presidential candidate will be happy to brand it the do-nothing Congress of 2028.

Passing bills that can become law, forcing the president to veto those that cannot, and using hearings to show Americans what is at stake may still fall short. Ending and averting war, regulating a powerful tech industry, and protecting the United States’ economic competitiveness are difficult for Congress to do without presidential cooperation. But two years without any progress would only leave the affordability problem in place and the United States in a more dangerous global position than it is in now. A Congress that confronts these problems directly can at least make headway. And if it does its job right, it will hand the next president an agenda equal to the moment.

#5845. Biden admin weighed quitting Twitter after Elon Musk purchase, new HHS doc drop reveals

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Medicare Chief Dr. Mehmet Oz announce the first 15 organizations receiving federal funding to combat chronic disease through nutrition and lifestyle care.

Amid a push to bolster transparency, the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) launched a searchable trove of public records, including previously unreleased memos from the Biden administration and Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s calendars.

While the website will be updated over time, the initial drop includes a Biden-era memo detailing how the National Institutes of Health (NIH) considered no longer having a presence on Twitter after billionaire Elon Musk acquired the platform in October 2022, renamed it X and promised to scale back moderation amid free expression concerns.

Also included within the department's so-called Radical Transparency dashboard are Kennedy’s calendars, which reporters have previously requested and have not been publicly disclosed before now.

HHS BUREAUCRATS ACCUSED OF 'SABOTAGING' RFK JR'S EFFORTS TO EXPOSE FAUCI RECORDS

Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. delivers remarks during an event at HHS headquarters Aug. 10, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Win McNamee/Getty Images)

"When President Trump asked me to lead HHS, I promised to bring transparency back to the American health agencies," Kennedy said in a video announcing the launch of the website. "If the government makes decisions that affect your health, you deserve to see the evidence behind them. Not simply to be told, ‘trust the science.’"

Kennedy claims that HHS’ previous method of presenting public records was disorganized and confusing to use. He said public accountability would be aided by consolidating the documents in one simplified dashboard.

The Cabinet secretary noted that his department plans to upload more documents to the website during his tenure and the first drop includes previously available documents that are now easier to search and locate.

TRUMP ORDERS FEDERAL CONTRACTORS TO ALERT WHITE HOUSE ON CONGRESSIONAL RECORD REQUESTS

Though many on the political right are likely to hail the move as HHS’ increasing transparency efforts, Democrats have criticized the agency for lacking transparency under Kennedy's leadership.

Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. speaks during an announcement at HHS headquarters in Washington, D.C., on July 21, 2026. (Tierney L. Cross/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Congressional Democrats, for instance, have repeatedly said Kennedy stonewalls their oversight inquiries and claim that HHS, under his leadership, has provided incomplete responses to their questions. A letter sent by 18 Democratic lawmakers on Nov. 19, 2026, called out "an alarming lack of public transparency" following a delay to the disbursement of rural healthcare grants.

In early 2025, HHS reduced staff working on Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) requests and public records at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and Food and Drug Administration (FDA) — another move Democrats claim stifles or at least slows transparency.

Previous releases of public records by the Trump administration have enabled journalists to uncover millions of dollars in welfare fraud.

President Donald Trump has charged Vice President JD Vance, seen by some as his heir apparent, with leading the White House charge against fraud, signaling the high importance of the issue to the administration.

WHITE HOUSE LAUNCHES INTERACTIVE MAP TRACKING BILLIONS IN SUSPECTED FRAUD

Agriculture Secretary Brooke Rollins, left, President Donald Trump, Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Education Secretary Linda McMahon attend an event at the White House on May 22, 2025. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

In addition to public records releases, HHS has also pushed regulations requiring that healthcare companies make more information public about their pricing practices.

While the administration focuses on fraud, concerns about corruption within its ranks have taken root with voters amid press reports regarding conflicts of interest and alleged self-dealing. A Fox News survey found that 60% of Americans were concerned about government corruption ahead of the recent midterm elections.

HHS did not respond to a request for comment when reached by Fox News Digital on Tuesday.

#5843. What’s Holding Up Ukraine’s Air Defense?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: What’s Holding Up Ukraine’s Air Defense?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Understanding the conflict four years in.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at delayed U.S. Patriot air defense missile shipments to Ukraine, Israeli forces killing a top Hamas official, and yet another purge within China’s leadership.

As Russia batters Ukraine’s cities with missile strikes, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has delayed sending scores of Patriot air defense missiles to the embattled country, including those held by European nations, according to a Financial Times report.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at delayed U.S. Patriot air defense missile shipments to Ukraine, Israeli forces killing a top Hamas official, and yet another purge within China’s leadership.

As Russia batters Ukraine’s cities with missile strikes, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has delayed sending scores of Patriot air defense missiles to the embattled country, including those held by European nations, according to a Financial Times report.

In total, the U.S. has held up around 60 Patriot air defense missiles, including more advanced PAC-3 missiles as well as around 20 PAC-2 missiles. The missiles include those that are already held in European stockpiles but require U.S. sign-off before their transfer.

The missile transfers were originally set to occur in September, October, and November in batches of around 10 missiles each. A further delivery is set for December. Ukraine began sounding the alarm publicly in October.

Patriot air defense missiles are Ukraine’s primary defense against Russian ballistic missiles, which Russia has used in an accelerating campaign to make Ukraine’s major cities unlivable.

Since September, Russia has attacked Ukraine’s energy infrastructure more than 350 times, frequently resulting in days with no regular supply of power or water, a growing concern as winter approaches. The Pentagon is separately delaying approving a plan to allow Ukraine to produce its own Patriot missiles domestically.

The slowed deliveries follow interruptions in recent weeks to the functioning of Starshield, a version of the Starlink satellite internet service that Ukraine relies on for battlefield communication. Ukraine uses Starshield in its mid-range strike drones, as well as in systems used to defend against jet-powered Russian attack drones. Starshield is owned by Elon Musk’s SpaceX. Musk and the Trump administration have traded blame over who is responsible for the throttling of Ukraine’s Starshield access.

Even as Russia scores wins in its missile campaign against Ukraine, however, recently leaked data and Ukraine’s own strikes illustrate the high price Moscow is paying.

Russia has lost at least 490,000 soldiers, according to data derived from a leaked Russian government database and analyzed by independent Russian news outlet Meduza. Even that may be an underestimate—a further 230,000 are listed as missing in action, many of whom are likely dead but not yet officially recorded as such. That means that as many as 720,000 may have died in Ukraine. The losses place the war in Ukraine as the deadliest conflict for Russia since World War II.

Russia primarily sends volunteers to fight in Ukraine and pays large bonuses as an incentive. While Russia has so far been able to find enough volunteers to staff its army, the losses are an economic and social strain on the country. Data compiled by Janis Kluge, an expert on Russian recruitment, shows a slowdown in recruiting for October as compared to last year.

Meanwhile, Ukraine’s long-range strike capacity continues to develop. On Tuesday, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky confirmed that Ukraine was behind a strike on a Russian cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland. Ukrainian drone also recently attacked a Russian warship docked at a port in Libya. Ukraine on Tuesday separately launched yet another mass wave of strikes on Russia, with Russia stating that it shot down 699 drones.

Deadly airstrikes. Israeli forces have “eliminated” Naim Abu Nahal, the head of Hamas’s internal security apparatus, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and Defense Minister Israel Katz announced in a joint statement posted on X on Tuesday. Nahal took on the role in September, after Israeli forces seized his predecessor in a Gaza ground operation and took him to Israel. “No terrorist will succeed in evading the long arm of the State of Israel,” the statement added.

The Israeli military also launched two airstrikes that killed six other people in Gaza on Tuesday, including a teenage boy and two police officers, who were killed at a police station, according to Palestinian health officials and first responders. Gaza’s police force slammed what it called “an ongoing campaign targeting the police apparatus aimed at sowing chaos,” the BBC reported; Israeli forces said they struck “military operatives.”

Corruption probe. A top Chinese Communist Party official once considered a possible successor to Chinese leader Xi Jinping is being investigated “for suspected severe violations of discipline and laws,” Xinhua reported on Tuesday, in the latest example of ongoing purges of China’s leadership.

The official, Chen Jining, is the head of the Communist Party in Shanghai, China’s most populous city and a key economic center. He is also a member of China’s Politburo, an elite group of 24 officials that help lead the country. He had been seen as likely to rise within the ranks of China’s government, having previously served as mayor of Beijing.

The move is part of Xi’s long-running anti-corruption campaign that has seen hundreds of top officials, including many within the military, convicted. Since 2025, three other members of the Politburo have also faced corruption charges, and in the last two months, corruption charges were announced against both Beijing’s deputy mayor and the former mayor of major city Chongqing.

Xi’s consolidation of power comes ahead of a likely fourth term as president beginning next year, meaning that he will hold power until at least 2032.

Money up front. Japan will pay its full contribution to the International Criminal ​Court (ICC) ahead of schedule following an appeal by the court after the United States sanctioned it, according to Prime Minister Sanae ‌Takaichi.

Japan is one of the largest contributors to the court, accounting for around 15 percent of its funding. The early payment will give the court a larger financial cushion after U.S. sanctions take effect in April 2027.

The Trump administration launched a campaign against the ICC in July, starting with pressuring other nations to withdraw from it. That effort has been largely unsuccessful, with only four countries—Tajikistan, Chad, Venezuela, and Naoero—leaving the court. In October, the United States sanctioned the ICC itself, which U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio called a “rogue court.”

The United States has long been concerned about the ICC’s ability to prosecute U.S. soldiers. However, the Trump administration’s pressure on the ICC represents the sharpest attack yet on the body.

Sunset today will see the appearance of a supermoon, during which the celestial body will be about 30 percent brighter and nearly 20 percent larger than normal. In North America, a full moon that falls after Nov. 7 is known as a “beaver” moon, as it usually occurs around when beavers are building their dams and stocking up on food. If you happen to miss this month’s supermoon, though, don’t worry: There are three more coming in a row in December, January, and February, according to NASA. Happy moongazing!

#5842. It’s Time to Settle With Russia

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MICHAEL C. DESCH is Packey J. Dee Professor of International Relations at the University of Notre Dame and founding Brian and Jeannelle Brady Family Director of the O’Brien Notre Dame International Security Center.

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

In October, U.S. President Donald Trump sold his eleventh-hour deal to secure diesel from Russian President Vladimir Putin—pausing sanctions that his own administration had imposed—as a boon for the negotiations to end the war in Ukraine. The arrangement, he wrote on Truth Social, was intended to keep Russia at “the bargaining table.” Of course, it was always obvious to observers that the gambit had as much or more to do with bolstering the U.S. economy and stemming a massive victory for the Democrats in this month’s midterm elections, and in that, it failed.

Cynics have grounds for questioning all of Trump’s moves when it comes to Russia and Ukraine. He clearly dislikes Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky and shows a marked preference for illiberal leaders, especially those willing to give him easy wins. Still, a stopped clock can be right twice a day. And concessions such as the fuel deal are exactly what might move Russia and Ukraine closer to peace, even if they are not wholly motivated by public-spiritedness.

Ukraine and its backers in Europe and the United States have long maintained that Russia only responds to sticks. The problem with their argument is that since 2014, and especially since 2022, Moscow has been subjected to nothing but sticks. According to Kaja Kallas, the European Union’s foreign policy chief, the more than 25,000 sanctions imposed on Russia after its full-scale invasion of Ukraine had cost its economy almost $1.5 trillion as of June. And although Trump has backed Ukraine less consistently than U.S. President Joe Biden did, especially in his rhetoric, his administration has still provided significant financial, military, and intelligence support; his CIA has been more aggressive than Biden’s in assisting Ukraine’s offensive and defensive air operations. All of these coercive measures have proved insufficient to compel a change in Putin’s policy or turn the tide in Ukraine’s favor on the battlefield.

European leaders may fret that Trump, now a lame duck and focused on his legacy, will push for a deal on Ukraine that suits the Kremlin’s interests more than their own. But ultimately, this remains a war in which Russia has the advantage. Europe should recognize that its pressure tactics have not worked and welcome the U.S. president’s willingness to throw in a few carrots among the many sticks used to try to beat the Kremlin into submission. Washington, meanwhile, should continue to pursue a concessionary strategy by encouraging Ukraine to surrender territory, freezing NATO’s expansion, and offering sanctions relief to Moscow. Putin has shown a willingness to sign favorable deals in the past, including the Minsk agreements in 2014 and 2015, which promised an end to the civil conflict in Donbas only to collapse under the duress of Kyiv and European capitals. A more enduring peace is desperately needed in Ukraine, however unfair the price may seem.

Ukraine has had some recent success on the battlefield. Early in 2026, Kyiv launched offensives in western Zaporizhzhia that erased a year of Russian gains there. During the summer, the Ukrainian military embarked on a major counteroffensive, Operation Vivaldi, that made significant progress near Liman, delaying Russia’s campaign to take the Donbas fortress cities of Kramatorsk and Sloviansk. And after four years, Ukrainian forces have finally inflicted serious, albeit not irreparable, damage to the bridge connecting Crimea and Russia, disrupting a military transport route and tarnishing a potent symbol of Putin-era expansionism. Kyiv has also brought the war home to segments of the Russian public with its attacks on oil infrastructure and commercial targets such as warehouses for Wildberries, Russia’s largest online retailer. Supporters in Western capitals have trumpeted these achievements as evidence that Ukraine has turned the tide in a war that previously seemed to be moving in Moscow’s favor.

But tactical wins do not necessarily add up to strategic victory. And, as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby reminded the Ukraine Defense Contact Group at a meeting earlier this month, the war will be decided on the ground. Casualties remain a huge problem for Ukraine. The few times Zelensky has addressed them, he has offered implausibly low figures for his side and cited astronomically high losses on the other. A recent study by Alexander Grable, a King’s College London war studies researcher, dissects these figures and concludes that Ukraine is suffering far higher casualties than Russia in absolute numbers—findings that, if true, refute the canard that Russia is uniquely careless with the lives of its soldiers. These days, while Ukraine pursues costly offensives to temporarily recover territory, Russia is taking cities and densely fortified areas by encirclement rather than frontal assault, reducing the risk to its fighters.

Although Ukraine’s strikes on Russian oil infrastructure have disrupted refining and strained domestic fuel supplies, Moscow has managed to cushion the blow through emergency measures and rapid repairs. In May, the U.S. Defense Intelligence Agency concluded that Kyiv’s long-range drone attacks did not “substantially affect Russian energy production,” which may explain why the Russian civilian population has not felt the full bite of them. The average Russian seems to be weathering this pain with something between resigned stoicism and increased determination to see the war through to victory. If Ukraine’s deep strikes were designed to encourage the United States to support Zelensky’s proposal for an energy truce, its effects have been counterproductive: higher oil prices have refilled the Kremlin’s war chest and greater energy costs are undermining allied support for Ukraine, especially in the United States.

Many Western battlefield analyses suggest Russia has recently lost a bit of ground. In fact, the battle lines in Ukraine remain ambiguous. According to the widely followed Military Summary Telegram channel, which applies a nuanced metric of territorial control to geolocated videos from Russian and Ukrainian sources, Russia has continued to make inroads through October and November. In the north, its troops have carved out a buffer zone on the border, making gains around Sumy and Velykyi Burluk. The see-saw battle for Kupyiansk—a fiercely contested gateway to Kharkiv and northeastern Ukraine—once again favors Moscow. In Donetsk, Russian forces have largely taken the city of Dobropillia, which could enable them to creep north behind the Donbas fortress cities and encircle them. And Russia continues to gain ground in eastern Zaporizhzhia, where its units are now edging closer to the region’s administrative capital. Operation Vivaldi may have slowed the Russian advance north of Kramatorsk, but only at a significant cost to some of Ukraine’s best units. Much like the Kursk campaign in 2024, the counteroffensive seems more like a temporary media victory than a real change in the ground war.

In the air, Russia has commenced a deep strike campaign across Ukraine, using drones and ballistic missiles to turn the tables in key regions. Russian forces have severely disrupted Kyiv’s Black Sea operations, intensified strikes against data centers and electricity infrastructure, attacked locomotives, and hit targets ever closer to Ukraine’s western borders, and are now going after major bridges across the Dnieper, which could literally cut the country in two. Russia’s quantitative and qualitative advantage in jet-powered drones allows these weapons to function as effectively as cruise missiles, and recent air operations have been more sustained and systematic as a result. Ukraine’s dearth of American-made PAC-3 interceptor missiles—a result of the United States’ own dwindling defense stockpiles—means that Russian ballistic missiles are mostly unstoppable.

In October, Trump angrily wrote on social media that Zelensky “doesn’t want to end this war, and probably never did.” This was unfair: undoubtedly, Zelensky does want to end it, just on his own terms. The aid and support Europe and the United States have lavished on Ukraine—initially necessary for preventing a catastrophic defeat—now gives its president little incentive to make peace any other way. Early in the current war, when Ukraine was unexpectedly prevailing on the battlefield, it passed on two promising peace tracks that might have ended the fighting soon after it began.

But negotiations are now the only way forward for Ukraine, and they will not progress by targeting Russia exclusively with sticks. To overcome the current impasse, Ukraine and its supporters have to put their chips on the table. That means forgoing any hope of NATO accession—or of a sustained NATO military presence in Ukraine—and relinquishing territory to Russia. If Kyiv remains unwilling to make the concessions demanded of losing parties in war, then Washington can take steps to encourage better judgement. The United States could taper down or cut off certain services and munitions it provides the Ukrainian armed forces, including Starlink and Starshield satellite connectivity, Patriot missile defense systems, and intelligence support for air defense and deep strikes on Russian targets. Any of these deprivations would be felt immediately on the battlefield.

Washington should offer carrots of its own accord, too. President Trump can lift some sanctions, such as those banning Russian commercial flights from U.S. airspace, without much controversy. Lifting others, such as those restricting fossil fuel exports and financial transactions, will be less palatable but more attractive to Russia and thus more effective. Russia’s readmission to the SWIFT interbank messaging service and other global commercial networks must be pursued accordingly.

Pro-Ukrainian hardliners argue that no amount of carrots will be enough to satisfy Putin’s voracious appetite for territory. They warn that if Russia is not stopped in Ukraine, it will continue its campaign of conquest farther west through Poland, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia, and beyond, with no end in sight. As Zelensky warned European leaders at the Berlin Security Conference in early November, the Russian military buildup is not just directed at his country, “it is directed against you.” But although Russia is making grinding progress on the fronts in eastern Ukraine, at the rate of its advances in 2025 and this year, it would take three decades just to capture the rest of Ukraine east of the Dnieper. And the idea that Russia, with a military of under 1.6 million men and women in uniform, could defeat a NATO alliance boasting 3.65 million soldiers is implausible, especially considering NATO’s overwhelming advantages in major weapons and military spending.

Even if Russia could take on NATO directly, it is far from clear that Putin wants to. The Kremlin’s war aims in Ukraine may not seem just, but they are narrower than many analysts allege. Since his 2007 speech in Munich excoriating the United States’ global hegemony—and after the 2008 NATO summit declaration that welcomed eventual membership for Georgia and Ukraine—NATO expansion has been Putin’s idée fixe. He has been similarly preoccupied with the status of the pro-Russia citizens of Eastern Ukraine and devoted substantial effort to ensuring protection of their linguistic rights and political influence through the Minsk agreements. Only when that failed did Moscow recognize the two regions of the Donbas, Luhansk and Donetsk as independent states eligible for membership in the Russian Federation, paving the way for direct Russian intervention in the region. Nonetheless, Putin continued to pursue a negotiated solution to the conflict through Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan and Israel’s then prime minister Naftali Bennett. As with the Minsk process, it was Ukraine and its backers who walked away from these negotiations when their aims—the complete liberation of all Russian-occupied territory—still appeared achievable on the battlefield.

In a round of U.S.-led peace talks earlier this month in Abu Dhabi, Russia made maximalist demands, asking for the pullback of NATO forces from Poland and the Baltic states, limits on European nuclear deterrence, and restrictions on the size and capabilities of Ukraine’s military. But read in the broader context of Putin’s singular and consistent fixations, these demands look more like negotiating ploys than dealbreakers. It has long been clear what the Kremlin will settle for: control over Ukraine’s eastern border regions and a largely demilitarized Kyiv. Whatever the ultimate terms of this deal, some basic facts are clear. There is no going back to a Ukrainian Donbas, nor a Ukrainian Crimea or Novorossiya. And whatever security guarantees Europe and the United States give Ukraine in the future, they cannot include NATO membership.

The status quo in Ukraine is unsustainable. Four years of war have lulled many of Kyiv’s supporters into complacency about the risk that continued pressure on Russia could create an escalatory spiral. There is increasingly cavalier talk in some newer NATO capitals, for example, about undertaking aggressive NATO exercises near Kaliningrad and overcoming the city’s air and missile defenses. But those steps may only further widen and deepen the war. The clear and present danger of vertical and horizontal escalation must not be dismissed.

Cracks are already showing in the West’s united front against Russia. France and other western European states are experiencing heightened social and political unrest, fueled in part by the costs of the war. In late October, Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian Prime Minister Jonas Gahr Store made a rare phone call to Putin to “convey messages directly regarding future negotiations” toward peace, according to a statement released by the two leaders. In the United States, a once bipartisan hard-line toward Russia is starting to fall out of favor, even among Democrats. In mid-October, Democratic Representative Jim Himes told the Warsaw Security Forum that a future Democratic president will have to acknowledge the “tectonic fact” that the United States can no longer afford to play its historic leading role in confronting Russia.

There is reason to think that the Trump administration’s more conciliatory approach has already opened the door to diplomacy. The Oleshky truce brokered by First Lady Melania Trump and the Vatican successfully evacuated civilians trapped in a Russian-occupied city south of Kherson. And the president’s comments in his 60 Minutes interview this month suggested that he is ready to ply Russia with the incentives necessary to get a deal over the line. These are steps in the right direction, but in addition to altering the balance of carrots offered to Russia, the Trump administration must consider providing Ukraine security guarantees outside of the NATO framework.

Now in the twilight of his political career, Trump has made clear his desire to leave a legacy in the form of Nobel Peace Prizes and triumphal arches. Ending a major war in Ukraine would be a crowning achievement, which is why he has waxed lyrical about a “big, beautiful agreement” since he entered his second term in office. And if Trump still believes that China is the pacing threat of the twenty-first century, reintegrating Russia into the global economy and weakening the axis between Putin and Chinese leader Xi Jinping will be paramount for advancing his long-term political vision.

No one has lost money betting against peace in Ukraine since 2014, and it would be foolhardy to expect Trump to pursue diplomacy solely for altruistic reasons. But perhaps if he sees peace as being in his own interest, as well as that of the United States, he might, despite himself, broker an end to a bloody and increasingly fruitless war. Doing so could be one of the few positive legacies of his second term, even if it does not get him to Oslo.

A cyber breach exposed unclassified information on a CIA network last week, the same day the FBI was hit by a cyberattack on a database used by law enforcement agencies across the country.

Multiple sources told Fox News that the CIA, the Office of the Director of National Intelligence and the FBI were breached on the same day.

The New York Times first reported that the CIA and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence discovered the breach.

A cyber breach hit the CIA the same day the FBI last week. (Saul Loeb/AFP via Getty Images, File)

#5839. Armenia’s Pivot to Washington

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Interview: Armenia’s Pivot to Washington

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The photographs lining the walls of the stairs of the Armenian Embassy in Washington are impossible to miss. There are multiple portraits of Armenian Prime Minister Nikol Pashinyan with U.S. President Donald Trump in the White House last year; pictures of Pashinyan and U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance in Yerevan from February of that same year, when Vance became the senior-most sitting U.S. official to visit Armenia; and photos of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio during his visit to Yerevan last May. There are also multiple pictures of the Oval Office prayer meeting organized by the embassy in September, where Trump met with leaders from the Armenian Christian community in the United States.

I asked if they change the photographs with each administration, and the embassy’s spokesperson informed me that this is the first time any photographs have been put up—to mark the momentous nature of the Trump administration’s relationship with Armenia.

The photographs lining the walls of the stairs of the Armenian Embassy in Washington are impossible to miss. There are multiple portraits of Armenian Prime Minister Nikol Pashinyan with U.S. President Donald Trump in the White House last year; pictures of Pashinyan and U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance in Yerevan from February of that same year, when Vance became the senior-most sitting U.S. official to visit Armenia; and photos of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio during his visit to Yerevan last May. There are also multiple pictures of the Oval Office prayer meeting organized by the embassy in September, where Trump met with leaders from the Armenian Christian community in the United States.

I asked if they change the photographs with each administration, and the embassy’s spokesperson informed me that this is the first time any photographs have been put up—to mark the momentous nature of the Trump administration’s relationship with Armenia.

Trump brokered the signing of a joint declaration in August 2025 between Pashinyan and Azerbaijani President Ilham Aliyev, which committed Armenia and Azerbaijan to resolving their decadeslong violent conflict over disputed territories. It’s one of several wars that Trump claimed to have solved during his second term as he lobbied for the Nobel Peace Prize, though the two countries have yet to sign a formal peace agreement more than a year later.

“The final peace deal is not there, but we have peace on the ground,” Armenian Ambassador Narek Mkrtchyan said. “We have trade relations with Azerbaijan right now—we are buying some stuff from them, they are buying from us—and this is very important for the peace. Of course, there are still some issues that need to be addressed, but in general, peace is in the region.”

Monday marked movement on two of the biggest outstanding issues.

Armenia put forward a draft proposal of its new constitution that removes language calling for reunification with the Nagorno-Karabakh region, a disputed territory within Azerbaijan that was controlled by ethnic Armenians until an Azerbaijani military offensive deposed them in 2023. Pashinyan has supported the new constitution—which Azerbaijan has demanded as a condition to reach a permanent peace deal—and has said that he will resign if a referendum to adopt it fails.

The second development was the official launch of the Trump Route for International Peace and Prosperity (TRIPP), a roughly 27-mile transit corridor between Azerbaijan and its exclave of Nakhchivan that was agreed (and rebranded from the “Zangezur corridor”) during the joint declaration in Washington last August. The TRIPP corridor passes through Armenian territory and will be controlled by the Armenian government, though it only retains a 26 percent stake in the corridor’s development—the remaining 74 percent will be held by the U.S. government.

Mkrtchyan, who previously served as the Pashinyan government’s minister for labor and social affairs, was appointed to be the country’s ambassador to Washington in late August 2025, just weeks after the Trump-brokered accord with Azerbaijan was signed.

“The current agenda that we have with the United States of America, we never had before,” he said.

Armenia has high hopes from Trump and TRIPP. “It actually has the potential to change the concept of the South Caucasus. Previously, this region has always been associated with conflict, with instability,” Mkrtchyan said. “This region has the potential to turn into a territory of dialogue, of connectivity, of prosperity, and cooperation, and the TRIPP project is an organic component of this.”

Mkrtchyan also corrected me when I referred to TRIPP as a “corridor,” describing it instead as an “infrastructure project route” with “roads, railroads, fiber optics, transmission lines, possibly pipelines.” TRIPP is now “becoming more than a bilateral project” for peace and connectivity between Armenia and Azerbaijan, he said, and will eventually turn into a key trade passage between Central Asia and Europe—entirely under Armenian jurisdiction. “It’s going to be a new global supply chain,” Mkrtchyan said.

Those ambitions could run headlong into Armenia’s geopolitical reality. The landlocked country also shares a 193-mile land border with Turkey that has remained closed since 1993, when Armenia’s conflict with Azerbaijan first began. “Right now, 80 percent of Armenia’s borders are closed,” Mkrtchyan said, adding that talks to reopen the Turkish border continue in parallel with the Azerbaijan negotiations, “and there is a lot of hope and a lot of potential.”

Then there is Armenia’s 27-mile border with Iran that the TRIPP route runs uncomfortably close to. Iran has bristled at the prospect of having a U.S.-led initiative so close to its territory while Tehran and Washington remain at war, and a senior advisor to Iran’s supreme leader warned this week that “if Trump and his allies want to cross this Iranian red line, we will break their legs.” Armenia’s entire railway system is also still controlled by Russia—a legacy of the erstwhile Soviet Union that both countries were a part of—and the once-tight relationship between Yerevan and Moscow has steadily deteriorated thanks to Russia’s war with Ukraine and a perceived lack of Russian support over Nagorno-Karabakh.

But those conflicts have only spurred Armenia to diversify from its neighbors and traditional partners by aligning more closely with Washington, according to Mkrtchyan. “This is a new reality, and we live in this new reality, but this is our strategic choice,” he said. “But we were very transparent and open to our colleagues in the region—we told them that this is not against you. It’s not against Iran, it’s not against Russia. It’s first of all for Armenia’s national interests, for Armenia’s security, and for regional cooperation,” he added. “. This is a unique opportunity to transform the region.”

Armenia is also hoping to parlay its newfound U.S. links into becoming a technology powerhouse. Earlier this year, a San Francisco company called Firebird established a new data center in Armenia in partnership with fellow U.S. firms Nvidia and Dell, in a deal that will be powered by 70,000 of Nvidia’s advanced semiconductor chips by the end of 2027. “We want to become a key partner of the U.S. in providing sustainable supply chains for future AI systems,” Mkrtchyan said. That ambition is further bolstered by nuclear cooperation and critical minerals agreements signed by Vance and Rubio during their respective visits this year.

“Especially after the peace deal, it gave a strong signal to the American companies that right now is the moment to consider Armenia as a platform for cooperation and investment,” Mkrtchyan said.

Given that our conversation took place just days after a resounding defeat for Trump’s Republican Party in the U.S. midterm election, officially consigning the U.S. president to lame-duck status for the remaining two years of his term, I asked Mkrtchyan how Armenia is thinking about its relationship with Washington heading into a post-Trump world.

“It’s very natural that at some point, President Trump and the Trump administration will not be in power. It is also natural that in the coming 50 years, only Republicans or President Trump will not be ruling the government, because our agreement with the United States of America—the TRIPP agreement—is for 50 years,” he said, adding that historically Armenia has had “bipartisan friends” in Washington.

“Of course, President Trump will always be remembered as someone who brokered this peace deal and the Armenian nation is very thankful to him,” Mkrtchyan added. “But we are building our relationships with the United States of America institutionally.”

Rishi Iyengar is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @iyengarish.bsky.social X: @Iyengarish Instagram: @iyengar.rishi

#5838. Kamala Harris' former political mentor and boyfriend delivers stinging warning about 2028 comeback

Tyrus and Ryan return for another round of News or Noise on Planet Tyrus, putting bizarre studies, viral animal headlines, and Kamala Harris’s brutal 2028 straw poll numbers to the test. Can Tyrus spot the real news from Ryan’s fabricated headlines, including a shameless New York Giants Super Bowl trap?

Willie Brown, a longtime California Democrat and a power broker in the state party, said former Vice President Kamala Harris lacks the communication skills needed to run for president again in 2028.

"She’s not a communicator," Brown said in an interview with Tara Palmeri, an independent journalist.

"No Baptist preacher flavor in her soul," he added.

Although Harris remains one of the country’s most recognizable Democratic figures, Brown’s skepticism comes from a longtime political ally and casts doubt on her potential candidacy.

DEMOCRATS SHY AWAY FROM QUESTIONS ON WHETHER HARRIS SHOULD RUN FOR PRESIDENT IN 2028

Kamala Harris, former vice president and 2024 presidential candidate, speaks with Al Sharpton during the National Action Network (NAN) Convention in New York, April 10, 2026. (Angelina Katsanis/AP Photo)

"I’m sorry that she even thinks about running for president. I don’t want her to run," Brown said.

"She’s reached the highest electoral level for any woman in America," he added, referring to her post as President Joe Biden’s vice president.

Brown, who once served as San Francisco mayor and California state Assembly speaker, has a long history with Harris. Early in her career, he appointed her to the California Unemployment Insurance Appeals Board and then the Medical Assistance Commission.

The two were also romantically involved between 1994 and 1995.

Brown, in a San Francisco Chronicle op-ed, openly discussed their relationship and his role in her political career in an article titled, "Sure, I dated Kamala Harris. So what? "

Several other Democrats have already hinted at their own interest in a 2028 bid, crowding the potential field.

Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., a top progressive in the House of Representatives, has hinted at a run. Former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg has said he is "inclined" to run, and California Gov. Gavin Newsom announced plans to decide on a presidential bid at the beginning of the new year.
